<meta>This page transitions from the physical description of the manuscript to its literary and theological content. # Piot 2 (Spanish Collection No. 478)

->written in 5 lines.<-

107 folios

There remain in the volume 107 folios.

The first 28 are missing.

Among the rest, 3 other folios [are missing/misplaced].

Pope Celestine V, who abdicated, was perhaps the prototype for the novel; he had retired to a hermitage. 1294. <note>This refers to the protagonist of "Blanquerna," a famous 13th-century Catalan novel by Ramon Llull. Celestine V was a contemporary of Llull and famously resigned the papacy to return to a life of solitude.</note>

<summary>This page contains introductory archival notes regarding missing folios and a comparison between Pope Celestine V and the literary character Blanquerna, followed by a theological treatise in Catalan. The text explores the nature of the Trinity, the Incarnation of Christ, and the soul's capacity to love and understand the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Ramon Llull, Blanquerna, Pope Celestine V, Catalan literature, Trinity, Incarnation, Christology, medieval philosophy, Jeroni Falcó</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the theological discourse from the previous folio, shifting from the nature of the Trinity to the mystery of the Incarnation (the Nativity) and the Eucharist. It introduces a moral narrative involving an Abbess and a repentant woman to illustrate the struggle between hope and despair.</meta>

the nativity of the man and god

It happened as something so mar-
velous as the conception which the angel 
announced being the messenger of the Son of God; that he saw in her 
man and God, being in the Virgin His 
marvelous work, contrary to natural works, to 
demonstrate that God has the greatest power and 
infinite power and will, which nature does not have, 
which happens not by her nature but for us, 
demonstrating Himself as the Lord of nature. Wherefore, if God 
did not make use of such a work above nature, 
He would not demonstrate that His power was above 
nature. And with corporal eyes it is 
not possible to be seen <unclear>unclear</unclear>. Man is 
placed <unclear>unclear</unclear> and the corporal eyes 
cannot see if not by 
<unclear>unclear</unclear>. But with the eyes 
spiritual and corporal it can be seen 
man and God <note>original: "home de[u]"</note>... the mind and the soul 
and the corporal eyes 
the body. Wherefore with corporal eyes 
man sees the outward shape and color 
of the bread, but with spiritual eyes one sees 
the flesh of Jesus Christ. For just as 
corporal eyes see corporal things, 
so spiritual eyes 
see spiritual things. And just 
as corporal eyes see the figure 
and color—as the beast does—so the spiritual 
eyes see with the knowledge of God in the let-
ter <note>This refers to seeing the divine truth beneath the literal text.</note> the flesh of our Spouse <note>Christ is frequently referred to as the "Spouse" of the soul or the Church in medieval mysticism.</note> who came with 
infinite will and with all-powerful power, 
and with wisdom where all is pertinent 
in that form and color of bread, to the 
true flesh and blood of Jesus Christ. And if 
God did not will it so, it could not be 
followed: for His will, power, and wisdom 
would not have infinity nor completeness. And 
because our belief must be

In the monastery there was a lady 
who had been a great sinner 
in the world, and after she was in their 
[order] she had performed many moral deeds. 
And [yet] she felt shadowed from the grace and justice 
of God because of the sins she had committed 
while in the world. And they—so strongly 
did these sins overshadow this lady—[that] she 
[lost sight of] the mercy of God and felt herself 
so guilty for the sins, and especially 
for those she had committed in the mon-
astery, that she fell into despair <note>original: "entelspania cayer," contextually meaning she fell from hope into despair.</note> that God 
would have forgiven her: it seemed to her 
that no matter what she did she could not have 
the blessing of God. And she came 
to the lady [the Abbess] in 
this manner, wishing to overcome her 
sin in which she used to be mired, through the 
merits of the penance she had performed and

sins, by the likeness of the Abbess

with eyes of mercy and make her

hope <note>original: "espansa," again for "esperança."</note> and of the other virtues. In

all the convent, she confessed the temptation that 
she had felt against hope and 
asked for pardon and counsel against the temptation 
that was distressing her. The Abbess 
responded, saying these words: 
"Daughter, regarding hope, understand that it 
makes a man or woman trust that God is more 
merciful than just. And therefore a 
sinner falls into despair. And because God 
is mercy and [His mercy is] greater 
than any necessity, it follows 
that man, who is a creature, cannot 
commit as much sin as the mercy of 
God can forgive. And therefore it is a 
wrong thing that when one considers one's 
great failings, one does not trust in the great 
mercy of God, to which one gives 
good praise and honor when one com-
pares it as being equal to the great justice of 
God. By which man receives honor; 
mercy pardons a man's 
mortal failings, giving to man 
contrition and sorrow for his sins. Re-
member in the conception of the Son of 
God and in the Passion a rich hope and 
mortify despair. For if God 
willed to unite and humble Himself to our 
human nature, and if God willed 
to give [Himself] for the weight of our failings out of love, 
the nature that He took so that He might resemble 
[us] and [defeat] the demon, it follows that He wishes 
to forgive us if we trust in His piety

<column-break/>

and mercy. For one loses the height of 
charity when it is not in the soul, because 
despair comes when man does not remember the 
humility that God showed when He took 
human nature, nor His passion. And there-
fore through despair one loses hope. 
Wherefore if a man has hope according to what is 
given of hope and [confession of] sin, then 
God is moved to forgive, for the act 
makes a man love and have good trust: 
wherefore whoever with charity makes use of hope 
in their memory and in their 
will: the truth is that all 
sins can be forgiven."

<summary>This text discusses the theological distinction between physical and spiritual perception of the Eucharist and provides a moral lesson on the virtue of hope. An Abbess counsels a repentant sinner against despair, emphasizing that God's mercy is inherently greater than any human sin.</summary>
<keywords>Incarnation, Nativity, Eucharist, corporal eyes vs. spiritual eyes, Abbess, mercy, hope (esperança), despair (desesperança), monastic life, Ramon Llull</keywords>

Old Catalan (ca. 14th-15th century manuscript transcription).
English (accessible, "warm museum label" style).

        *   Preserve headings, bold/italic, tables, centered text, `<column-break/>`, line breaks, paragraph structure.
        *   Translate *all* languages (Latin, Greek, etc.) into English.
        *   Use specific tags: `<note>`, `<margin>`, `<gloss>`, `<insert>`, `<unclear>`, `<term>`, `<meta>`.
        *   Hidden `<meta>` for continuity.
        *   Ending: `<summary>` and `<keywords>`.
        *   *Constraint:* No code blocks/backticks.

    *   *Column 1:*
        *   "na sa colpa la ql alma anima deve respõ de na caritat..." -> Discussion of the soul's debt of charity/love to God. The soul must love God more strongly because He gave her [grace?] and because He spared her eternal pains.
        *   "Labatella respõ ala dona..." -> The Abbess (Labatella) responds to a woman. Mention of the "excellent goodness" of God.
        *   Discussion of why one should love God: for glory and fear of hell. One should love God more than oneself.
        *   "Ent labatella ac motrada la viatò..." -> After the Abbess showed the way to love God... she speaks of another woman who had sinned against God's laws.
        *   "fo pro car era paupira en le fegle..." -> She entered the order because she was poor in the world and couldn't sustain herself, rather than for pure devotion.
        *   "la batet sa respõ..." -> The Abbess responds that in poverty/entering an order, one's intention can be ordered or disordered. If one enters for poverty, can one have charity?

    *   *Column 2:*
        *   "caritat principalmet a dieu..." -> Principal charity to God, secondary to others.
        *   "E car nos auem una etencio nit fola met..." -> We must have an intention not only for ourselves but to enter the order loving God and the society of companions.
        *   Another woman is mentioned who wants to be "raised" or "honored."
        *   "Labatella respõ amar p relacie p esser honrar es erguyll..." -> Loving for status/to be honored is pride and vainglory, contrary to the life of Jesus Christ who was poor.
        *   One must mortify their own will. Remember the life of Christ and the saints who loved poverty.
        *   Refers to the "will" (volontat) being the source of merit.

    *   *Page 3 (New page marker):*
        *   "uolontiera cābieu en d'offici..." -> Willingness to change office/duty.
        *   The Abbess speaks to a woman who has "long since" sought to know how to love God firmly.
        *   "Labatella respõ qui uol auer caritat..." -> He who wants to have charity must know it with mind and understanding. Without understanding, there is no charity.
        *   Memory, understanding, and will (the Augustinian triad of the soul: *memòria, enteniment, voluntat*).
        *   Discussion of the unity of this world and the glory of the next; the pains of hell.
        *   "gre mēt r a r’ētendre la guĩa amor q̄ d’ieu a fait anoſ autraſ..." -> Great love God has for us, taking our nature.
        *   "ſo fill de dieu uolc p’noſ autres preire..." -> The Son of God wanted to take [our nature] for us and be sold and die.
        *   "E p ublidar r ignorance..." -> Forgetfulness and ignorance cause a lack of charity.
        *   The Abbess tells all the women that above all things they should strive for charity.
        *   One can suffer all "trebaills" (labors/hardships) for it.

    *   *Column 2 (Page 3):*
        *   "Car et nobla ralitat d'un caritat a dieu..." -> Noble reality of charity to God, who descended from heaven to earth to be incarnated.
        *   Mention of the "trouble" and the "cross" (crueltat / cretat?).
        *   "Etantat d'anima noſ aa car paradiſ..." -> The soul reaches paradise where there is eternal glory.
        *   "Son cõ tant ſet etā gra..." -> If it is so great and necessary, why are we so poor in it?
        *   "Entat ſion dō d’e..." -> Transition to a new story about a woman who was "mort ſortuẽ malautal" (very strongly/severely sick).
        *   The Abbess visits the infirmary (*enfermeria*).
        *   She finds the sick woman suffering with "impaciencia" (impatience).
        *   The woman says she is in such great pain/sickness that no virtue can fit in her soul.

    *   *Vocabulary/Style Note:* The text uses "Labatella" which is an Old Catalan form for "The Abbess" (l'Abadessa). I will use "The Abbess" in the translation but note the term if necessary. The tone is instructional and moralistic.

    *   *Drafting Column 1:* Focus on the debt of the soul. God gave her grace instead of eternal pains. The Abbess explains that loving for glory/fear of hell is common, but one must love God more than self.
    *   *Drafting the "Poor Nun" segment:* The Abbess addresses the nun who entered the convent because she was poor. The Abbess clarifies that even if motivated by poverty, one can still find true charity if the intention is redirected to God.
    *   *Drafting the "Pride" segment:* Warning against seeking status/honor in the convent. Christ was poor; we should be too. Mortification of the will.
    *   *Drafting Page 3:* Focus on the three powers of the soul (Memory, Understanding, Will). The Incarnation as the ultimate act of charity.
    *   *Drafting the "Infirmary" segment:* The sick woman's struggle with pain and impatience.

    *   *Latin/Old Catalan phrases:* "merer" (merit), "paupira" (poor), "erguyl" (pride), "malautia" (sickness).
    *   *Annotations:* Add notes for "the three powers of the soul" and "mortification."

    *   Keep `<column-break/>`.
    *   Keep `<unclear>`.
    *   Preserve line breaks.

    *(Self-Correction during drafting)*: The transcription has some messy abbreviations ("q̄", "p’"). I need to ensure the English flows naturally while acknowledging these are theological instructions.

    *   *Summary:* The Abbess instructs the nuns on the nature of charity, the necessity of proper intention when entering religious life, and the importance of the three powers of the soul. It concludes with a visit to the infirmary where a sick nun struggles to maintain virtue through her suffering.
    *   *Keywords:* Charity, Poverty, Intent, Three Powers of the Soul (Memory, Understanding, Will), Incarnation, Infirmary, Patience.<meta>This page continues the moral and theological instruction given by the Abbess to her community. It moves from the theological mysteries of the Incarnation to the practical application of "charity"—divine love—within the monastic life, specifically addressing the intentions of those who enter the order and the struggle for patience during illness.</meta>

merited with holiness, pleasing to God and

will; that is: to love for status to be honored.

loved poverty. And one can

our sake. The Abbess says: my

understanding, his will cannot be in
charity; and therefore, to remember and
understand are in God, and they are intended to be
the will and the <unclear>excess</unclear> of His virtues.
One must remember and understand the unity
of this world and the glory of the other, and
for the eternal pains, one must have grief;
and one must understand the great love that God
has shown to us others. How we are clothed
in one nature... <note>referring to the human nature shared by Christ</note>
the which nature the Son
of God wished to take for us others. And
He wished for us to be sold and killed and
[placed in] sacks <note>referring to the indignities of the Passion</note>. Wherefore, by such remembering and
understanding, charity and love are in the will.
And through forgetting and ignorance,
charity is shaken from the will,
and a bad, solitary will and
wickedness enter. When the Abbess had
prepared all these things, she said to
all the ladies that above all things
they should strive to have charity. For
no person should be without it who wishes
to have it. And since no one can know [its full depth],
one should want to have it. And for its sake,
a man can suffer all hardships, if he
prays with his tongue and mind
and exerts himself in that which is pleasing to God.
And [this] makes a man rejoice in all the

<column-break/>

refusals that imprison him or [cause] bitter
remembrance. For it is a noble
reality of charity that God descended
from heaven to earth to be incarnated,
to be troubled in <unclear>holy</unclear> ways,
and to be treated with such cruelty in this world;
such is His charity. He took us others,
and all creatures which come from you, to
receive them as His own; He has given us
all creatures that are used. And so much
has He given the soul, for paradise
calls to us, and in it there is
eternal glory, and we follow Jesus in our
hardships. Since [charity] is so great
and so necessary to us, why are we
poor in it? And charity is given to whom
He wills, and it is a great fault
to be without charity. Then
there was a lady who was
very severely sick. The Abbess,
according to her custom, went
through the monastery to see
if in any matter she could improve [their state],
and if by her presence she could
bring any comfort. The Abbess entered
the infirmary where she found that
sick lady suffering her
sickness with impatience. And the lady spoke words by
which the Abbess perceived
that in her soul there was no justice. The Abbess
asked the sick lady what
she had done <unclear>in her faith</unclear>, and [spoke] of the
virtue of patience. The lady responded so
strongly and [with such] great hardship of her sickness that "in my
soul no virtue can fit. I am
oppressed by such great grief that I would
never wish to be <unclear>born</unclear>..."

<summary>The Abbess instructs the nuns on the necessity of having the correct spiritual intention (charity) regardless of their worldly reasons for entering the convent. She emphasizes the three powers of the soul—memory, understanding, and will—and visits a sick nun who struggles to find virtue amidst her intense physical suffering.</summary>

“O foolish thing,” says the Abbess, “I want you to respond to me: in what way would it be a worthy thing? <note>original: ".n. ioz"</note> Pain and suffering are a great world full of force and sudden misfortune that you sustain; if you die without justice, your soul is on a path to hell. Be certain of this: for where there is no justice, misfortune gives way to the query of the soul. All your patience regarding the evil that misfortune gives you is because it will be a punishment for your faults—where justice is, false works are never hidden. Fortitude <term>Fortitudo: the virtue of courage or inner strength</term> is in your heart, for no misfortune can stand against justice; fear it, and if you have patience, you shall find joy.” While God, our Lord, asks the Woman of Misfortune <note>original: "dona malauria"</note> what merits she has through justice—for patience, if it is of a saving nature, brings a blessing. So many good words did the Abbess say to the Woman of Misfortune—singing of justice, fortitude, and patience—that she recovered the soul she had lost. And the woman said these words: “Blessed King, you have deigned to give me the virtue of justice and such patience; for you punished me and you soothed me. I am yours, for I cannot pay according to the greatness of my faults. I am worthy to sustain these trials; if I had more, I would give them. Let that which is pleasing to your will be done to me; I want nothing but what you want. I am not worthy of glory; my faults are so many that they lead to deep and perdurable pain. If you wish to punish me, you use your great justice. If you wish to forgive me, you use your great mercy. Whether it be punishment or whether you forgive me, I adore and bless your great justice in everything—and between the two stands the sweet mercy that the Queen

<column-break/>

of the Heavens <note>A reference to the Virgin Mary</note> asks of you for us.” While the woman said these words, she felt so great a fervor of devotion and her heart was converted into a true fortitude. Patience was given to her so that misfortune was absent from her speech... Now the Abbess, having consoled and determined the Woman of Misfortune, entered into a secret house—in truth, a prison—where there was a woman who had been there for seven months, kept very strictly within her order. While the Abbess went to judge that woman so that she might console her in her penitence, the woman was on her knees, weeping, and said these words: “O holy justice of God, who has all things in your power! You are adored and blessed, for I receive the things I sustain by recognizing my faults. From this knowledge is born pleasure to my soul, so that your justice may be loved by me and my faults hated. The more strongly you labor <note>meaning to discipline or put to work</note> me, the more you show me the way to knowledge, and the more you make me remember the great mercy that is in you. In this bodily pain I wish to stay forever, so that my soul may rejoice.” So sincere were the words the woman said that the Abbess was moved to devotion and mercy and granted her pardon. Weeping, the Abbess said these words: “O sister, let no failing be in you, for where the justice of God is, no parity exists; it was prepared for pardon, and to pardon it is necessary that the divine justice forgives all those who praise Him in His sovereignty.

---

receive the blessing. But your person is greatly burdened by the great affection <note>in this context, 'affection' refers to physical needs or worldly attachments</note> of your body. Poor are our clothes, magnificent and few are our commands; your bed is hard, our company is solitary and physically dark. But your soul has in its company the divine splendor that makes you love and know His justice. Ask and it shall be given to you; repent and you shall be forgiven. Your understanding and your devotion make you more noble than my virginity.” Weeping and with devotion, the Abbess spoke these words. And the woman gave many thanks to the Abbess, saying these words: “It is the nature of a good Lord that with the blessing of His lot He puts my soul in prison. My harsh life is a teaching to women against the failure and wickedness in their hearts. Now, to live well is to punish my body and to exemplify God in His justice. Therefore, I ask for the poison <note>perhaps meaning the 'bitter medicine' or 'sting'</note> of my faults. This penitence in which I am, I ask for all the days of my life; the more my person is labored, the more my soul is mastered and at rest in God. Let God be in my soul; let penitence and labor be in my person.” The Abbess then went to the place where, sitting under a tree, she saw a woman who was weeping. That woman had been in the world with great riches and good manners, and she was weeping for the harsh life she sustained in the monastery. The Abbess asked the woman why she wept or was so discouraged, and the

<column-break/>

woman recounted to her all the will of her heart. “O foolish soul! Justice is just! Have knowledge of justice, which commands you to punish yourself for that which you enjoyed so delicately in the world. With humble clothes, humble that which was dressed in proud clothing; with a hard bed, torment that which has lain in noble beds. Foolish thing, why do you not go to the greatness of weeping? Lift your eyes to the cross and see our spouse Jesus Christ, Lord of heaven and earth. In what bed is He? See His clothes, which are rare and of a marvelous color <note>referring to His blood</note>, and see His heart; see how He is silent and of great nobility. See how from His side thirst and hunger are satisfied; see what crown He had for honor, being beaten and judged. Justice and all things...” And the Abbess reproved the woman who had gone forward in her error; she promised to leave the folly in which she stood.

### A woman of prudence [Chapter] 23.

There was one in sin who had a certain knowledge of whether it was a mortal sin or not, yet she did not want to clarify or ask if it was mortal or venial. ### The Story of the Mother and the Advocate

In the chapter concerning merit, the Abbess directed her words. That lady had a son who was a great advocate <term>aduocat: a lawyer or legal representative</term> and was very wise. That son of hers wanted to be [a religious man] and set himself to learn the Divine Scripture until he could be an advocate [for God]. But his mother made him learn the secular arts <note>original: "lo est d’art" refers to the Seven Liberal Arts, the standard secular curriculum of the Middle Ages.</note> so that he might be [successful] in this world. This woman, the lady, remembered how she had seen her son [use] his knowledge badly, and for this reason... [she used] words of iniquity <margin>fault</margin> against Wisdom and against the Holy Scripture through which a person gains the knowledge of God. The lady remained [troubled] by the fault she had, according to what we said above. The Abbess said to the lady that she had erred against Wisdom and the Understanding of the Holy Spirit, for the will of her son to be a religious man and to understand the Holy Scripture of God was given to him by the Holy Spirit,

<column-break/>

who wished to instill Wisdom so that He might be known and loved through him, and that he might be honored in celestial beatitude. From this failure, others err who make their children swear [to worldly things]... the holiness of God [judges] the lady’s fault, through which she shall have less glory or even [miss] salvation; where she shall have greater pain or even damnation.

Now the Abbess was moved against the woman, and gave her a heavy sentence. And while the Abbess spoke of how the Holy Spirit gives [gifts], the lady remembered in her heart a [lack of] honor which she had; for she did not know the "Holy Eight Articles" <note>Likely referring to the Eight Beatitudes or a specific medieval grouping of faith articles.</note>, nor did she know the gift that the Holy Spirit gives, nor did she know the holy beatitude <note>original: "benauransa"</note> that one seeks to find the guided way, nor did she know the "Eight Virtues" <note>While seven is standard, some medieval traditions counted eight virtues to counter eight principal vices.</note> by which a person goes to Paradise, [nor the] eight mortal sins <note>original: "vuy peccats mortals"; early medieval traditions, following Evagrius or Cassian, often counted eight principal vices before the standard Seven Deadly Sins were codified.</note> through which one falls into Hell. Nor did she know the [signs] that God gave in the Law, nor did she know [the commandments]. The woman was ignorant of all these things in her soul. These things are very [necessary] to know, and because of this ignorance, the lady [grieved] and prayed that all the things mentioned above might be shown to her. <margin>Now the Abbess rebuked her</margin> The Abbess spoke these words to the lady: "O, do you not know the Eight Articles where our faith resides? One does not know how to [behave] according to faith if they do not know these. And whoever does not know the Eight Gifts that the Holy Spirit gives, how can they have great [spiritual health]? And whoever does not know the Eight Beatitudes does not know the way to eternal glory. And whoever is ignorant of the Eight Virtues, where is the path by which they go to salvation? And whoever does not know the eight [sins]...

<column-break/>

...how can they know how to guard themselves, or do penance for the faults where the conscience [is burdened]? And whoever does not know the Ten Commandments, how can they be obedient to God, or receive Him, or know how to [live]?" Whoever [lacks] all the things mentioned above stays in the shadows, even if they have [worldly] things. This is explained more clearly in the **Book of Fortitude** <note>original: "libri... De fortitudo"</note>.

<gloss>She had learned Prudence as a lesson.</gloss>
Regarding this [virtue] called Fortitude <term>Fortitudo: inner strength or courage, one of the four cardinal virtues.</term>, through it, one is given light and knowledge. Wherefore, a woman adoring her [holy] God [prayed] that He might give her the virtue to defend her against [evil thoughts]. But the woman was [caught] in vainglory <note>original: "vana gloria"</note>, which she had [experienced] in her life... seeking the mercy of God. <margin>So strongly she returned to rebuke</margin> [She was in] that land of vainglory, [and prayed] that God would [strengthen] her... that she might find goodness in His glory. The lady sought to understand how this woman had vainglory in her prayer... she recognized her failure... for vainglory seeks to enter "through the hand" <note>An idiomatic expression meaning it creeps in through one's own actions or deeds.</note>. Then the lady, with [great humility], asked the Abbess where the failure mentioned above came from. The Abbess responded in these words: "O, [know] that there is a struggle between the virtues, for the [vices] are contrary to the virtues...

<column-break/>

...which mortify the vices. Where [vices] are spread in the [soul], they are many... but through the virtues of God—Prudence and Fortitude—they cannot stay in their company. When the soul is [filled] with vainglory, the soul does not know how to recognize [temptation]. It is hard to fight against temptation unless the remembrance of our [Lord] and the victory over sin [is present]. [We must] increase the understanding of the nobility of God; [His] goodness, wisdom, and strength are the [true] company of the soul. But vainglory makes the soul forget its failings; it does not understand the virtues of God while it is [intent] on vainglory, which comes from a failure of wisdom and strength..." <margin>Now the Abbess</margin> This was the manner in which vainglory [tempted] her.

Another woman said to the Abbess that she had [lost her heart]... the Abbess responded: "This [distress] is through a failure of Fortitude <note>original: "fort cotatge"</note>." She failed in the manner of loving [God], for her memory remembered the world and forgot the honesty and the [holy] life of religion. Fortitude is mortified in the will; therefore, one must remember [the virtues]. Another woman said to the Abbess that she [was troubled] by talking with the [angel of the abyss?]. The Abbess said that God has ordained His creatures... so that in many and diverse ways they serve [Him].

will, if it fails, or if any good thing [is missing]. Each of us should remember some saint who is [...] so that they may be our advocate <note>original: "procutũdoz", likely "procurador"</note> in the heavenly court. Oh, members who see themselves in holiness, so that we may have love. I return to the [rule] so that I pray to God that He [grants] the light of intelligence and of faith, through which they may know and love, if they have the devotion of salvation. At the hour of Compline <note>The final church service of the day.</note>, each of us should remember if she has returned to offend [God] by erring. In the morning, after Matins <note>The service held in the early hours before dawn.</note>, one should reflect on what state she is in, seeing all as has been said, regarding life and death and other warnings. The Abbess had called all the ladies together so that, through the divine pity and mercy of God, they would be mindful... and seeing it, they blessed God that He had given so much favor and goodness to their Abbess in her teaching. Regarding Understanding:

She told the ladies that [Understanding] was entirely a spiritual power that illuminates the soul with true faith. [Understanding] had all its works made willingly, unless it is difficult for the will; and loving the cause, it recovers [strength] from the understanding, so that its works do not err. For just as a man is strong in his [purpose], so does he compose himself. And thus, the soul, in its remembering and its willing, does not receive [perfection] without understanding.

Now there was great fervor because our [state of] remembering and willing, and our spiritual height, comes from understanding. And for that which the soul wishes to receive spiritually, it is necessary to have a reprieve in its willing and

<column-break/>

its remembrance in understanding. For he who does not understand should know how to remember and will; and he who does not remember and will should know how to understand. For remembering and willing is the birth <note>original: "engendramẽt"</note> of understanding, and through understanding comes true remembering and willing; for man knows according to the work of reason: remembering, understanding, and willing. Since we wish to understand, it is necessary that we proceed by the value of remembering, so that we believe that which we cannot understand. And let us understand that God's essence is perfect <note>original: "feticia", likely a corruption of "perfectíssima"</note> and in His works He is greater than what we can understand. For if in all that we say and do, our understanding is not enough to comprehend how great a Lord and good parent He is, how can our understanding aid us to understand all of God and His works? And if we did not do it this way, we would foolishly enter into an essence and works greater than God, and that is an impossible thing.

Regarding the cessation <note>original: "cessallimẽt"</note> of understanding in that which it cannot comprehend: cessation is when the understanding ought not to strive for what it cannot understand. And he who wishes to understand and guard his understanding in that which cannot be understood, does not wish to make use of the best [part] that God wished to create. He wishes to have a secret pleasure... and moves away from the sadness in which he was kept by ignorance. Wherefore, understanding is a more noble and greater thing; it is higher in its understanding than memory is in its remembering (for memory is lesser in its remembering) and higher than the will is in its willing (for through too much loving, the will becomes lesser in its willing). For whoever wishes to understand what can be understood, must first wish that his 

---

understanding [be clear], because ignorance is that which one cannot understand. And if the understanding is joined with the will, [the will] should love it; and if the understanding is false, the will should hate it. Understanding is that which the will must love. For just as the will is at fault for not loving understanding, so is understanding at fault for not understanding the will. In memory, remembering without understanding or willing accustoms the imagination to imagine in such a way that a man becomes a fool <note>original: "foll"</note>.

While the Abbess gave divine [teachings] to the ladies, one lady asked the Abbess in what manner the memory could remember without willing and understanding. The Abbess said to the lady that just as a man might remember something and see another thing that the will cannot reach to love, nor understanding to comprehend, by chance in his remembering he begins to imagine in a disordered way, by which disorder the power of memory <note>original: "vicur memoza t i u a"</note> is destroyed.

## Of the Will . 32 .

The Abbess told the ladies that God had given will to man... and it seemed that his will, which was given to man and his seed, was more noble than the will of other creatures. This is because the human will should not wish for any thing without reason. Wherefore, it is clear that a man offends when he loves something without reason, or loves anything that is not 

<column-break/>

right. To cease from loving that which is his [own], or loving that which is evil: for when a man loves more that which is lesser or of bad value, then he is led by his will to love what is evil and to hate what is good. Wherefore the Abbess spoke subtle words; a lady said to the Abbess that she had not heard such subtle words spoken before. The Abbess replied and said that since they were gathered, they should seek to understand. It was a great thing that the will should wish for the understanding to expand and surrender itself, so that both of them could contemplate and understand God in His works. For if God did not wish to be contemplated and loved, the understanding would not have been placed in such great [importance], nor would memory, understanding, and will [exist]. These have been placed there so that [the sisters] might understand and love more. And if the will and understanding encounter any things that gender some doubt, it is necessary that man [submits himself to God].

Here is what is known through the will, and with memory and understanding. And obedience is necessary in the world and in all religious life; for if there is more constraint in religion than in the world, it is because the will loves the submission of obedience, so that it may be more [elevated]... if the will is not set in obedience, it fails to love.

She explains that while Understanding is a noble faculty, it must recognize its limits before God's essence, and she warns that a Will not guided by Reason or Obedience leads to spiritual disorder.</summary>

...if the will were obedient to itself, for the will is placed within itself to adorn itself with the greatest preparation. If in this subtlety it does not have the obedience of practice—a rising up of the soul <note>original: "ra sa", likely "raça" or "raó" in context of the soul's movement</note> which it ought to love because from it issues the greatest strength—then what I say to you signifies to the certain ones that which, according to the respect it owes, we cannot all attain. Each of us has her own will, and each subjects her own will to her understanding—and the will of each commands that it be bound, through itself, to the divine will. And my understanding and the divine will should be bound together generally to the understanding of you all, for in this manner it is found in the official religious rule of the Abbess.

<margin>To have order in one's prayer with its cause</margin>
In the **chapter house**, the Abbess is with all the ladies of the convent. The Abbess wishes to know the manner in which they pray, for it is a most necessary thing that prayer be the noblest work that exists in religion—and disordered prayer is most displeasing to God. While the Abbess was saying these words, a certain lady [was present] who had erred during the hour of <term>Matins</term><note>The canonical hour of prayer held in the middle of the night or very early morning.</note>, saying certain words of prayer while turning her heart toward other vain things, even while she stood in prayer. The Abbess, giving her response, said that prayer is, in judgment, a pressing of the heart toward God alone, so that the voice says no words [distractedly]. The second [manner of prayer] is that...

<column-break/>

Column 2:
...the heart and the mouth agree in prayer, the soul understanding that which the words say. The third manner is when a person lives a good life and is without mortal sin, for everything such a person does is prayer. The fourth manner is when the mouth says words of prayer and the heart considers other things. This fourth manner is displeasing to God and cannot bear fruit because of a failure of charity and strength. Through this failure, the soul forgets and ignores what the words signify. Therefore, in prayer, it is necessary for one to cast out the bad strength of carnal love, so that with [virtue] one may make the soul and the words match, uniting the soul with the words the mouth speaks.

Understand how **Blanquerna** <note>A reference to the protagonist of Ramon Llull’s 13th-century mystical novel, often cited as a model of monastic life.</note> adored God in the ten articles of God, most high, adorning God in essence and virtue. And how the man thought of his son <note>Likely referring to the relationship between God the Father and the Son, or a specific parable.</note>, and how **Our Lady Saint Mary** in the great affection and love she had, accorded her prayer with her words. And for this reason, it is necessary that we love God strongly and healthily, so that the soul and the words may join with God in such a manner that the soul of our heart rises to our eyes so as to shed tears—so that in our souls the virtues may conquer and empower our hearts with joy, faith, and devotion, giving glory and blessing to God. Furthermore, we must remember, understand, and love the virtues and the works of God—abundant charity, justice, holiness, faith, and hope. We must order our soul and our heart so that we may...

---

<page-num>8</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<columns>2</columns>

Column 1:
...climb on high, with increasing memory and a willing heart to contemplate His glory. And first, it is necessary that we remember our faults and the vileness of this world; and for this work, we ask for the help and the voice of the **Holy Spirit**, that our soul may be illuminated to approach God. In our prayer, we must serve and lament, seeking the mercy of God in which all perfection lies, and which a perfect prayer reaches through all healthy exaltation.

We ought to pray for the Lord **Jesus**, the apostles, for the cardinals, for the prelates, for the princes, and for all the Christians of God, so that their devotion throughout their lives may be to know and love God. And we pray that in this world <note>original: "uerome", possibly "ver món"</note> there may be the devotion that used to be in the world to awaken faith in the respect we owe to our spouse **Jesus Christ** and the apostles.

Though invisible, our soul is clothed in prayer by the same [Spirit], for our Savior and our likeness <note>The "Imago Dei" or image of God in man.</note> are with us. Those ignorant of the land and of science are in failure of our gifts, for they do not have the Lord whom they should worship. Such people do not know God, nor love Him, nor believe in Him; and for the gifts God has given them, they do not give thanks—for they are among those who daily [offend] our Lord Jesus Christ. They do not think that He became man for others. We should give thanks for the virtues that these men offend in the name of God... which is agreeable to our spouse... to honor Him who clothes us with the likeness of Jesus Christ... at the Day of Judgment, to exalt the name of God...

<column-break/>

Column 2:
...passion. <image-desc>circular decorative flourish</image-desc> We must submit to God because He granted us to be human; we cannot sufficiently thank Him for the good we have received and receive every day. May **Our Lady Saint Mary**, and all the angels and saints in Paradise, give thanks for us, welcoming us as guilty and coming for all. We do not hold ourselves worthy of the glory we hope for. Thanks be to God who made us flee the fury of the world and gave us friendship in this place in which we do penance.

May we be defended from evils by the judgment of God, and with a sincere life let us adore our Savior with fervent love through the commands of devotion, so that we do not grow weary of adoring, praying to, and loving Him who gave us a heart for loving, eyes for weeping, and a mouth for praising His justice and His works. In all these ways, the Abbess gave doctrine to the ladies on how they should pray and contemplate.

And before all the ladies, the Abbess, remembering these words in a common spirit, [said]: "I see my lady the Abbess... God in His presence..." And it seemed she had no [earthly] knowledge, so that the soul might at all hours remember the justice of God. With the counsel of you all, I wish to make a **new establishment** <note>A "new establishment" refers to a new rule or regulation for the community.</note> in this monastery: that is to say, that every week a lady shall be secretly elected to be a **watchful sentinel** <note>original: "espia gacha", a monastic official similar to a circator who monitored the behavior of the nuns.</note> who shall take guard over what you do, and that none of you...

<meta>This page continues the regulations for the "Order of Ladies" (nuns) under the leadership of Abbess Natana, then transitions into a new book or section where the protagonist, Blanquerna, travels into the wilderness and encounters a personification of the Ten Commandments.</meta>

...observed so that it may be known which lady is being watched, so that the serenity of the one may be loved by the other as if in the other's presence. In the **chapter house**, let that lady recount everything she has seen or done. If it seems to her that anything is unseemly or exists within our order [that should not be], let her not order anything before she has placed sentries <term>gachas</term><note>The term 'gacha' refers to a watchwoman or sentry; in this context, it is a nun appointed to monitor the conduct and spiritual discipline of her sisters.</note> in the city. These shall close [watch] our ladies if they go into the city for any necessity, so that they may take heed of their works, where they go and where they stay. And if they hear any dishonest words from them, or any matters for which our [ladies] might be found blameworthy, [they must report it].

Therefore, let no one be given the role of sentry unless she is of great virtue, for she is like a friend to the sentry... <unclear>p r’ al</unclear> who is within me, avoiding all failure. For this reason, I wish that you should close [the reports] every week, and that these ladies should give their reports to me. That is, a lady who takes care of what is being done, so that no one knows she is a sentry until it is reported in the chapter house. No one should command... if the ladies are gathered together... if any cause for discord arises... just as an order is ordained... that which you might bring to the Queen to ask for pardon.

All the ladies requested this through the [love] of God, and the Abbess, according to how she wished to ordain or confirm it, [agreed] to all the rules. 

The time was ordered by the Abbess in that monastery according to all the ordinances previously mentioned. She placed a good lady in that monastery so that she might assist in the instruction of the Abbess Natana. A lady from the city entered there, having taken her example [from the others], in the garden of other monasteries, following the rule in the same way as that which Abbess Natana ordained.

<column-break/>

### Here begins the Book of the Order of Women
->The great providence of God.<-

For the sake of the good that Jerome did for Blanquerna <note>The protagonist of Ramon Llull’s 13th-century novel, a man seeking spiritual perfection.</note>, who is in the place... where they see and adore God, so that they may conceive of God. Amen. It is [chapter] five.

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'O' containing a kneeling figure (Blanquerna) in prayerful contemplation.</image-desc>Now, on that same day, he went along the bank through the **Forest of Perusa** <note>A symbolic wilderness where Blanquerna encounters various allegorical figures.</note>, departing from the coast and the sea. Traveling... and in that part where there was a beautiful fountain, above which... in that place Blanquerna rested and slept that night. A great part of the night Blanquerna spent in prayer, as was his custom. Because of the solitude and the strangeness of the place—for he saw the sky and the stars—he was moved to contemplate God. And because of the wild beasts that went through the forest, he was moved to give thanks for all that he had. He was filled with a fearful strength of force, so that he would not be overcome by his deeds.

When the sun rose, Blanquerna was in prayer, and then he set himself on his way to travel all that day. At the hour of sunset, he came to a place where there was a great abundance of trees, and in that place there was a beautiful palace, where noble people lived. Blanquerna went to the gate of the palace. At the top of the portal, there were written in letters of gold and silver these words: "Thou shalt have no other gods... remember the Sabbath... honor thy father and mother... thou shalt not murder... thou shalt not commit fornication... thou shalt not steal... thou shalt not bear false witness... thou shalt not covet thy neighbor's wife... thou shalt not desire... [these] are the **Ten Commandments** of God." <note>The original text uses the Roman numeral 'v' but lists the Ten Commandments; this is likely a scribal shorthand for 'X' or a reference to the two tablets of five.</note>

---

Within that palace, those who have performed great works are gathered and rewarded. In the midst of the people in that palace, full of order and strength, is the honor that they should have in the world... so that the people would not... Blanquerna.

Now Blanquerna was very comforted to read the letters of God... and he wished to enter the palace to see the [personification of the] Commandments, and to go... When Blanquerna wished to enter, he met the porter, who said to him that no one could enter the palace unless they were obedient to the Ten Commandments. Blanquerna replied... a good lady... Blanquerna stood honestly at the council... the palace... and the porter at the gate said that before he would let him enter the palace, he must ask permission from the Ten Commandments. And he asked permission from the Ten Commandments, to whom he recounted the state of Blanquerna... and then the Ten Commandments were pleased. The porter told him he could enter. Blanquerna entered and went before them.

Blanquerna went with grace and love toward the place where the names of those who are disobedient to the Ten Commandments are recorded. In that hall were ten chairs <note>original: "v. cadret", likely meaning seats or groups for the Commandments</note>, and there was a great sound of mourning and weeping. These [figures] were the Ten Commandments, and they were dressed nobly in the garments of faith. They were tearing their hair, and in their countenances appeared great sadness. Each one was weeping and lamenting.

<column-break/>

They spoke these words very loudly:

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'P' featuring a standing holy figure in long robes.</image-desc>"O people, hear the people's lament!" The **First Commandment** said: "I have few lovers among those who exist. Men love... the men in this world love more the things that God has made: the sun, the moon, and the stars. They love the son of the king... they make a god of that which they love more than me, who am the source of all good things." 

And those who love God above all things are few... and those who, through love of God, would endure death, all labor, and great pain... I am forgotten by the great majority who fail to love God. [They think] more of worldly things than of loving God... and there is great sorrow. I am neglected... the mercy of God... those temporal riches... or some who embrace the grace and justice of God, nor do they give thanks to God...

When the First Commandment had finished these words, he wept and lamented [even more]... [he felt] destroyed. 

Blanquerna wished to ask the First Commandment what he could do... the son of the first commandment... the celestial bliss... he who sustained those friends... disobedient...

<margin>Who are disobedient</margin>

...to those who were disobedient to God... for I am not to blame... all the world... to the Master... and because they are... more strong and more...

<meta>This page continues the narrative from the previous section where the focus shifts from the internal rules of the convent to Blanquerna's travels. He has entered a mystical forest where he encounters the Ten Commandments personified as Ten Ladies. This page details his interaction with several of these figures.</meta>

Those persons whom God creates in the world most divinely <note>The text suggests these are celestial or allegorical figures</note> so that they may be defined and revealed; and it did not fail that every time he traveled in this book, he encountered them. The **First Lady** spoke with Blanquerna; the **Second Lady** complained and wept so strongly that Blanquerna listened to her words and her weeping, and he looked upon her, which moved him to pity and devotion of heart...

**The Second Lady** [spoke]... of God, failing not in truth. She spoke against the false lordship that some take over their Creator. God loves one soul more than all the treasures of this world. And he who loves it more than this [world] shall see the glory... which everyone desires.

In a similar manner, the **Sixth Lady** complained and wept with these words: "God made me from nothing so that I might be honored, yet the Son of God is held in low regard." She spoke of how Christians should honor the Lord's Day <note>Original: "lo dignier gue," referring to the Sabbath or Sunday</note>, yet instead of honoring it, the name of our Lord Jesus Christ is blasphemed by those in error. They follow their own desires rather than the dignity of the day. One soul is worth more than all the men at their false feasts, which are full of vanity and lack the firm love that those who love obedience should have.

**In another** manner, pity was taken by the **Fourth Lady**, who appeared and was joined by... [concerning] the mercy and justice of God. Whereby a man should be obedient to the good God, for His mercy is such that those who trust in Him and fear the justice of 

<column-break/>

**God**. The **Seventh Lady** offered these words: "Hear now the charity in which the saints are placed. To be disobedient brings the strongest devil and a spiritual and corporal death." Great respect is due to the celestial Lord... [to be] obedient. Those who are in sin... all will die.

**Lady**... the **Eighth Lady** spoke of the love of God that creates silence. God gives profound thought to the heart, yet people remember their failings and their sins. Their color fades, and they are like a... that is lost. They should fear the justice that takes hold of those who are disordered.

**The Sixth Lady** wept again at these words: "Friendship and love are found where there is mercy and justice. Yet here I find only great... between the wars." Justice punishes the light, but does not... the mercy of God. [People] are more willing to give to those who have much than to the poor. If a man is poor and in pain... [he receives] no mercy.

The **Seventh Lady** [returned]...

<column-break/>

...devotion of heart. God hears these words against those who bear **false witness** <term>fals testmōm</term><note>The Eighth Commandment: "Thou shalt not bear false witness against thy neighbor."</note>. This lady shall help to create goodness, for false witness is the enemy of reason. God loves those who love Him for a long time. The Son of God was betrayed by false testimony, yet he suffered it for the sake of the good. Those who replicate lies and false witness... God gives the goodness and the glory of God to those who follow the truth and avoid death.

The **Ninth Lady**... and the **Tenth Lady**... she who had been neglected. God put this... in her heart. This lady... lust and the fire of sin... This tenth lady spoke of the judgment...

If a man is public in his sins... he is like a noble heart that has become corrupt... he shall lose the life. Obedience is better than lust, for lust is the companion of the devil.

The **Tenth Lady** who wept... [she lamented] the state of the world...

<column-break/>

...to see that no one values the power [of the commandments]... in their hearts. They do not sigh for the truth, nor do they seek the light. They have turned toward vanity and away from the commands of the Lord.

<margin>
One of them wept so much that Blanquerna could not stop weeping. For a long time she wept, and Blanquerna spoke with the lady.
</margin>

**Blanquerna** spoke to the **Tenth Lady**... that he might find the way to restore her honor... and he saw that she was right to weep. He asked how he could serve her and follow the rules of her order.

**Blanquerna**... prayed that he might be given the strength to fulfill the law... the **Tenth Lady**... showed him the way and the place where he could best remain in her service. "If you are a good man," [she said], "you will follow Blanquerna in his good works."

<meta>This page continues the allegorical journey from the previous folio. Following the encounters with the various Ladies (representing commandments or virtues), the narrator now engages with the Fourth Complaint. The dialogue shifts from moral failure to the intellectual and spiritual labor of converting others through "necessary reasons" and the philosophy of love. The page concludes with a vivid description of the metaphorical Tree of Love.</meta>

In the words of this fourth complaint <note>Original: "blasima," a term Llull uses for a formal reproach or lamentation</note>, I stood there; for in the blindness of the judgment of the fountain of grace—from which comes the mercy that allows one to understand—it happened that as I went along the street where the seven paths of the world meet without a guide, my friend, the daughter of charity, was there. She was foolish in her speech, for the daughter of love loved her physician, for he, through his strength <note>Original: "fortitudo"</note>, would return to him much of the power of God, for from this justice she extended her courage.

**F**or in his face, looking toward God, he gave her such a look that by it he would return his value; for in his expansive nature he bore the burden, since he intended for justice to be loved in truth through courage. Thus, before him, I wished to die by grace for the daughter of his justice... a fountain that had violated the complaint of the wrong done against her. For if he loved her so much for himself, it would be plain as a vision, if only he would return to her the solace that he makes so nobly from on high... a favorable protector who saw her path where she wept and lamented loudly. My complaint was a stream of bowing, which, upon knowing him, returns to him.

<margin>Sorrow is the opposite of joy. To truth.</margin>
**T**he lady who was weeping, whatever the cause was, at the end of her own laughter and justice, the lady who was weeping replied immediately with these words:

**S**he was faithful as a friend to this lady, joined in a path of the lover for whom she seeks. Love has spoken to you; you have not seen anything in my inner being... in the soul that is not honored. When a merchant in this place feels pain and pity for the depletion of these ignorant people, and I have followed the damage of the fall, it is fitting that the merit—because they did not show him the goal of pure love—should love that. **A**nd the lady who was there, who was his servant...

<column-break/>

...with the seventh point, this lady was my understanding, and the pure love to which I go so that she may go to those people. As is true, she shows these people through "necessary reasons" <note>Llull's "raons necessàries" were logical arguments intended to prove the truths of faith to non-believers without relying solely on scripture.</note> why they should go there. For time and shame are what wish to receive the authority of the saints and the miracles, which were not the means by which the ignorant are enlightened. After this, because the people require reasons and necessary logic, let us see without doubt that the power and the truth of God appear. **R**egarding the complaint of the one who wished that only merits were shown, showing the articles [of faith] by which faith is illuminated through reasoning in the understanding; and the cause of this complaint was not contrary to the principal reason why we wish to convert the unfaithful. For faith should not be the cause of losing merit; rather, it is necessary that faith be given as a gift to the primary creation, so that God may be known. I prayed that I might use my understanding so that I could go into its essence, to see clearly with the understanding, moving upwards to understand the articles of authority. I could then place the power of artful skill above the understanding in that which he believed. For he did not believe in error, but affirmed that my faith was not necessary in its sinews. This is against me, and I will not be... even if they can be slandered by some...

**T**he tear of one who has many times seen religious men go to other men to teach the Roman faith and Jesus Christ, and yet they had not succeeded in converting them. Because of this, it seemed that God did not wish to hear their request; for God...

<column-break/>

...your adoration, without a running tongue <note>Likely referring to speaking without sincere intent or understanding</note>. Faith replied: "Does God turn away from the coming of people for whom He took on flesh?" He wished to sustain the Passion in his body, for which the Apostles and a thousand martyrs sustained death to exalt Him in the world. But God is a lover of all those whom He wills should come to Him. God turns to whoever gives their will to sustain martyrdom for His love. **A**nd what punishment is stronger than if God wills that a man sustain rebukes for His love? More than that which is seen in our use of the words that Jesus Christ spoke in the Gospel <note>Original: "lantageh," referring to the Evangeli</note> when He said to Saint Peter: "Simon, son of John, do you love me?" <note>Refers to John 21:16, "Simon Ioannis, diligis me?"</note>. If he said yes, then he was my love. What error is against me? But because you are providing reason and the continuation of dispute against the infidels <note>Non-believers, specifically referring to Llull's mission to Muslims and Jews</note>, it seems to the people that error can be seen by us. **C**onsidering the reproach, my lot became a complaint to return to his full reasoning, to be lamented and recorded according to his oil. I began my journey, and following my complaint, I saw her judging... for she preached and comforted herself.

**G**od [spoke] these words: "**S**afe is God in all things; His justice and mercy never fail. Since God knows what He wills, according to our power in wishing to convert them; if you saw the justice of God, it would be a place of rest for us. Comfort yourselves according to her, for the justice of God returns now the grace to your merit, as you loved to convert the unbelievers..."

<column-break/>

...as much as possible in converting them. He replied: "I could complain to the souls... I never thought at any time that I would be so prized by the people. **N**ow let no one see me deprived of comfort." I was standing there, and I myself, an ignorant shepherd, was reproached by the people. **S**he held me as she comforted me first, and the light of love, devotion, and piety was in my will. Such consolation is through the light of love, devotion, and piety, which sustained me in the justice of which my consolation was made, while the complaint held its vigor... I prayed that I might be held and guarded in my understanding...

**I** went to understand the shadow of the sweet tree <note>The "Tree of the Philosophy of Love," the central metaphor of this work</note>, which is laden with flowers and fruit, standing over the beautiful and clear fountain. There I heard the cry of the Truth of Love, dressed in gold and silver, for the sin in which God made man. In that place, God and man were united, not only in the truth of his beloved, but also signified by the Passion of the Son of God. In that man was the labor of pure love, joined by Philosophy and Theology. **W**hile in my understanding I logged the comfort, and the complaint of justice saw that in that fountain of salvation, the understanding was strengthened by the pleasant medicine of the flowers of authority, and the understanding took up the others.

**U**nderstanding of the fearful earth shall be the truth of the testament, in finishing that which belongs to the human margin. **W**ith great labor and sorrow, he replied to this.

<summary>This page describes a mystical dialogue regarding the "Fourth Complaint," focusing on the necessity of using reason to convert non-believers and the role of martyrs. It concludes with the narrator finding spiritual refuge beneath the "Tree of Love" near a fountain of salvation, where Philosophy and Theology meet.</summary>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical journey of Blaquerna, following the previous page's discussion of the "complaints" regarding the lack of devotion in the world. The dialogue now shifts toward the intellectual mission of the character—and by extension, the author Ramon Llull—to convert non-believers using logical demonstration rather than just scriptural authority.</meta>

...to follow and restore all the support according to which the understanding reaches the Saracens <note>Medieval term for Muslims</note>. Once he has found men very skilled in philosophy who were wavering in the faith of the Saracens and did not wish to receive the authority of saints nor have blind belief. And Truth spoke very loudly: "And I come to you so that you should go to them, showing by reason and necessity <note>original: "razo e neccssarietat"; this refers to Llull’s "necessary reasons," logical proofs intended to demonstrate Christian dogmas to non-believers</note> the truth, so that they may leave the error in which they are deceived." He prayed that God might be known and loved by those who have so elevated my sorrows.

Then, looking at his students, he spoke these words: "Always keep yourselves in that which our knowledge is exalted." And Jesus Christ requires the reason of necessary demonstration <note>original: "razo denostratio uccessaria"</note>, for it is now time for the Christian to go to the sect that does not honor him as they should. He must honor Him because He made all the offices and everything that is; he should not want to have the doctor nor the glory that he could have except through our knowledge. Now it is a great burden that the Saracens remain in such adversity. The Jews are in doubt because of their captivity and they demand to have knowledge. Idolaters are in darkness because they have no belief. There are men who have no knowledge of us who have the will to go to Spain from God and from my sisters. Under that support they have to dispute with the infidels, showing the abbreviated art <note>original: "air abrieuyada"; likely referring to Llull's *Ars Brevis*, a condensed version of his logical system</note> of finding justice; and when they have learned it, they can overcome them through the art of pious arguments.

<column-break/>

It is now the hour to sustain hunger, thirst, fatigue, to leave one's home and friends and go into strange lands among people who seek a man’s death or who reproach their belief. <unclear>...</unclear> While the students spoke these words, they could not refrain from saying these words: "If we return to the cause, these things are all that you have told us. What a most extreme cause it is to be an enemy of God, of my sisters, and of our following, and of the love of hope, for justice and fortitude." If our God does not help us, where is the honor that we shall have? We are filled with such brightness that comes from that court where God is, who is similar to Jesus Christ on the Day of Judgment in only humble clothing. If by natural love we do not rise, which of us would want to die for our celestial Lord? To leave the city, to renounce faith, to lament with heavy sorrows. He said and repeated: "Alas, those whose place is to go here where I have wet the path with tears."

"I intend to go to our devotion, which comes to these students who feel no pity. Woe to him who falls in love and does not give his heart to follow God in the margin where he will have placed devotion." While Blaquerna followed his path, he cried out; he saw God, who had led him to such a place where he had heard all the words of love and of these things which he would not forget at any time.
<insert>Through justice... of devotion...</insert>

---

A very beautiful and strange contemplation and weeping to increase the honor of Jesus Christ and remember his heavy Passion. Devotion approached and greeted Truth with a pleasing countenance. Oh, what a burden Truth carries with a semblance of mastery and tribulation! Devotion and those whose countenances Devotion acts upon are making Understanding <note>original: "entendemie"; Llull personifies the faculties of the soul</note> feel some grievance. And they asked Truth; they responded to Devotion, receiving the serenity of the state of their following.

And Truth, since Understanding was her brother, begged him very strongly that she desired to go and dwell with him. The students of Understanding, so that they might follow him to give honor to the Holy Divine Trinity and the Incarnation of the Son of God. So that God and his works, which are the honor, are known among us and that we may be pleasing to God—those who are in brightness and certainty shall be illuminated by faith and by truth in return. As Devotion said: "The students of my brother have no heart, for my brother shows them Truth; my sister runs to our discourse." Devotion knows God and His works and wishes to have charity. Devotion prays to God and his works where these things are contained and gendered, so as not to forget the divine beauty and the celestial glory. The infernal signs and the fear of the vanity of this world, and taking many paths to the high mountain where there is hope, charity, justice, prudence, and fortitude. And between friends our nature and the holiness of my Lord. And there we make Devotion a wife, and many are strengthened by the words of love spoken.

<column-break/>

**W**hile Devotion marveled at the lack of knowledge of the students of Love, she said that Truth is more powerful. And she labored to honor God and to have the celestial honor, to be a defender of Truth and to expand fortitude. In their journey, they begged Devotion to go to the students, for they wander too much and are filled with the fear that the infidels are prepared to return them to the eternal fire. Beware of the ends that do not cease to draw one toward the sea.

Devotion responded: "You should know that powers do not suffice over a heart that is not in the disciples of my son, and for this reason, I pity them and enter into them without their fantasy or will. For if they did works of charity and justice, they would be known by the merit of the glory they gather." And she spoke thus: "You must return with my brother and to the students so that you may tell them that from me they can have all the good things they wish to have in this company; nor will their hearts contain what they wish to remember or want. And for that which they wish to have of me, the image of Jesus Christ is represented on the cross, from whom they should want great mercy. To honor his goodness."

And in truth, they returned to Understanding with great consolation. Blaquerna, through their very agreeable thinking, had done his task. While he went all alone through the forest, he remembered the words that Devotion had said. And he praised the charity, imagination, and justice of God, to whom he was now obligated through the Incarnation and Passion of the Son.

<summary>Blaquerna reflects on the mission to convert Saracens and Jews through logical "necessary reasons" rather than force. The text uses an allegory involving Truth, Devotion, and Understanding to explain the intellectual and spiritual preparation required for this divine work.</summary>
