--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the journey of Blanquerna through a forest. Having previously discussed the lack of devotion in the world with Truth and her sisters, Blanquerna now observes firsthand how the "diligence" of worldly men is directed toward material gain and conflict rather than toward God.</meta>

...to God, and if no labor or death for man in God departed at dawn, then it is otherwise.

**Blanquerna traveled. Of Diligence.** <note>The concept of "diligence" here is used ironically to describe the intense, anxious effort people put into worldly affairs.</note> As he went for the treasure, searching for the place that his heart so greatly desired, he did not cease from loving, nor his hand from trembling, nor his mouth from praising the name of God. While Blanquerna went in this manner through the forest, he saw a knightly traveler <note>original: "cauatler si caualcan"; likely a messenger or man-at-arms</note> who was going very fast; that man carried a large quantity of money. Blanquerna greeted that man and asked him what the reason was that he went so hurriedly. That man responded, saying that he was a merchant's messenger <note>original: "m̃ercader d’un rẽ"; likely a royal or merchant agent</note> from a city called the Hospital <note>This likely refers to a specific locale or a hospice-city</note>; and to deliver those ounces of gold that the king had need of for his honor and for his court, which he wished to hold with many great barons. Blanquerna asked him where he lived, but that same man did not want to stop with him so as not to be hindered in his business, and he did not want to respond to all that he asked him. For that reason, Blanquerna continued on until he found a fountain where he might eat his bread and herbs, and as Blanquerna was eating and resting, a squire <note>original: "esclandier"; a servant or messenger</note> came riding his palfrey very fast. That squire was coming from the court; he was going to a city in which a bishop had been elected, for he had been confirmed by the Apostle <note>The Pope</note>. That squire was going to make it known to the cathedral chapter and to the parents of that bishop. He watered his palfrey at the fountain that Blanquerna was using; while his palfrey drank, the squire exchanged words of friendship with Blanquerna. And being so hurried, the squire

<column-break/>

could hardly make his words understood to Blanquerna, nor did he wish to stay; he held the palfrey at the fountain. Blanquerna, having finished, stood on his knees giving thanks to God. While Blanquerna was praising God at the hour of Nones <note>The ninth canonical hour, approximately 3:00 PM</note> as was his custom, there came a merchant on foot, disheveled, who was crying and lamenting loudly, saying these words: "Alas, wretch that I am, who has labored so long in various affairs and suffered such great hunger, cold, and heat, and endured so much to earn what I carried. How miserable I am, for I have lost my wife and children whom I loved! I have been robbed and stripped of everything I had labored for, all because of this vanity." In peril of death, he wished he had never been born, for he was in such a state of ruin and total grief. Blanquerna said to him: "Where are you going? I will help you in your great misfortune, which represents our wretchedness." The merchant recounted to Blanquerna how he had been robbed by a knight who had a castle near the place where he passed; he was going to complain about that knight who had robbed him of everything he had worked for his whole life.

A short time after the merchant had departed from Blanquerna, a man came on foot, very agitated and tired, carrying a basket of chickens and hens on his neck. Blanquerna asked him if he knew in all that forest of any place where he could live—a place that was in a remote wilderness, where there was a fountain and wild fruits on which a man could live a temperate life. But that man did not respond to the words, so much was he

<column-break/>

preoccupied with the legal dispute <note>original: "plag"; a lawsuit or conflict</note> he was conducting. He was afraid that Blanquerna might ask about his condition or his business. After this, Blanquerna said these words: "It seems to me that as I came here near a castle, I entered into a dispute against my brother because of a vineyard that my father left me in his will. And I carry this present <note>The chickens</note> to give to the judge and the lawyers on one side and the other; and I even carry coins which I have raised with great haste. For God's sake, I pray you, if you know of any shortcut, tell me so I do not fail." Then Blanquerna was amazed at the great affection that man had for winning the vineyard and for the words he spoke. Turning his understanding to devotion and the great diligence he had recognized in these men for money, he said: Blanquerna knelt, and his heart was moved to devotion, and his eyes were full of tears, and he said these words: "O strange will, which has neither order nor nature, in which there is a failure of teaching and of conscience! From where have you come, you who make one forget the honor and the knowledge of God so much? And you do not wish to give devotion to this one who fears neither hardship, nor labor, nor death to honor God, and to lead them on the path of salvation. You are a solitary will, and little is achieved by what you have in your blindness; and little do you value the eternal labor in which you will never find rest. Where is the heart that seeks to be rewarded by the hand of man? Neither the squire, nor the merchant, nor the peasant remembers how they are restored

---

<page-num>13</page-num>
<columns>2</columns>

by the things they have in this world. Why is there no fear of losing that which is in the future and is irreproachable?" Because the peasant was amazed by the words that Blanquerna said, he left Blanquerna and fell into the thoughts he was having.
### Chapter 48

<column-break/>

The weather was clear and the sun illuminated all that forest through which Blanquerna was traveling. On another mountain stood a knight on his horse, equipped with all his weapons. That knight was an augur <note>original: "augurrer"; one who seeks omens from birds</note> and had come to wait for bird-omens that might signify the outcome of a raid he wanted to make against another knight who was his mortal enemy. While the knight waited to see if any eagle or hawk or other bird would appear in which he could know what he ought to do, Blanquerna climbed that mountain to ask the knight if he knew of any place that would be suitable for his life <note>i.e., his life as a hermit</note>. When Blanquerna was with the knight, Blanquerna and the knight greeted each other amicably; then each asked the other of his condition, and each recounted to the other the reason why he had come to that place. When Blanquerna had understood the reason why the knight was there, then he said to the knight these words: "It seems, knight, that you are so certain and noble of heart, and the beauty and disposition of your body and your horse and your weapons show me that you seem well-prepared to fight and defend yourself against another knight..."

<summary>Blanquerna encounters various people in the forest whose intense "diligence" toward worldly gains—money, legal disputes, and omens—contrasts with his search for a spiritual life. He laments their misplaced devotion before meeting an armed knight seeking bird-omens for a feud.</summary>
<keywords>Blanquerna, Ramon Llull, diligence, vanity, pilgrimage, hermit, medieval law, augury, knight, spiritual devotion</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Blanquerna's journey through the forest. Having previously encountered worldly messengers, he now meets a knight and engages in a philosophical dialogue regarding the role of divine reason versus superstitious chance (adventure) in the life of a warrior.</meta>

# [Left Page]

Thus spoke the knight: "I am certain of my person and I have never held a heart of cowardice, nor have I known any failing by which I might be led to wickedness or to displeasing God during the time that we have held the Order of Chivalry. <note>The "Order of Chivalry" refers to the spiritual and social code of knights, which Ramon Llull wrote extensively about in other works.</note> For this reason, it seems to me that I could not be better dressed or more noble than I am." Then Blanquerna, who is always devout in God, was given the occasion by chance—or perhaps it was through his gaze upon the causes that happen according to destiny, or through a movement of the soul—to ask: "Which cause is stronger: Occasion or Adventure?" <note>In this context, "Occasion" refers to a purposeful, reasoned cause, while "Adventure" refers to random chance or luck.</note>

"Fair friend," <note>original: "Belfanys," likely a transcription of "Bels amys"</note> said the knight, "the stronger cause is Occasion, which accords with reason and discretion; whereas Adventure accords with chance without the deliberation of reason and discretion." Blanquerna replied to the knight, saying that he had answered wisely. But he asked if the knight held a high regard for the practice of augury <note>The practice of predicting the future or making decisions based on the flight patterns of birds.</note>—observing how birds, when they fly, fly by Occasion when they seek to find food, yet their entire flight is Adventure according to the destiny of the man who sees them flying. "A knight who goes to war with another follows the 'adventure' of birds or of strong or weak omens, rather than the art of war. He who wars by the light of reason and discretion uses his understanding to signify the causes that happen to him according to the circumstances of the war."

<column-break/>

"By these words, you can understand that your armed enemy is stronger if he follows what reason demonstrates to him, while you are not if you follow what the birds do by adventure, wherein there is no necessity of reason. You cannot know the truth through them, for such a custom is displeasing to God and spreads the corruption of justice. Because of this, you have suffered the worst in your war." Then the knight considered the words that Blanquerna spoke to him, and through the restoration of reason, the knight recognized his failure. From these words, he once again realized: "Reason demonstrates to me how I should act with boldness. But because I believed in auguries, which I loved, I entangled my path. That which reason told me was true, yet I acted according to omens and signs, and not according to intelligence. And may it please God that through you, this knowledge is made manifest to me, so that I shall no longer have any belief in augury or divination."

Then Blanquerna set forth another lesson regarding war, which was this: "When reason demonstrates how one can do harm to the enemy, then reason must also consider if it accords with Charity, Prudence, Justice, and Fortitude. <note>These are the four Cardinal Virtues plus Charity, the greatest of the Theological Virtues.</note> For all these virtues are made of reason, and reason cannot exist in a thought if it is contrary to these." And then it happened that this man, who was boasting, understood that he must act by reason and...
<meta>catchword: if it happened</meta>

---

# [Right Page]

<page-num>14</page-num>

...dictated by the love of others. Now the words of Blanquerna pleased the knight, and Blanquerna told him that he would experience a type of thinking that he had never fled from in all his life. Long did the knight ponder if, in his warfare, he had possessed charity, justice, and a clear conscience. He remembered an enmity <note>A personal grudge or feud.</note> that he held against his enemy, and how he had placed his hope in auguries and not in God. Pride and vainglory had lived in his heart under the guise of Fortitude. When the knight had considered many of these things and recognized his failures, he then arose and praised God, the Lord, for showing him his failings and for guarding his future.

Then Blanquerna said at these words: "Blessed be God, who has given me the fortitude by which I have conquered my enemies in my heart! Never have I won a battle so profitable or so agreeable. For the enemies I have conquered in my heart—conquering the enemies in the heart of this knight—has been a long time coming." <note>Blanquerna views the conversion of the knight's soul as a greater "battle" than any physical combat.</note> For such a battle is very difficult and hard-fought, but with Charity, Justice, Patience, Humility, and Fortitude, one can conquer and overcome ill will, pride, wickedness, and malice. While the knight spoke these words, Blanquerna took leave of the knight to continue his journey in valor and praise.

Blanquerna went on, searching for a suitable place 

<column-break/>

to pray and retreat. As he went, he found a minstrel <note>original: "juglar"; a traveling entertainer who often relied on the patronage of nobles.</note> who appeared very poorly dressed, and his face signified the poverty of his heart. Blanquerna asked the minstrel for what reason his face showed such misery and sadness. 

"Sir," said the minstrel, "I have come from a court where there were knights and noble barons of this land. In that court, I thought I would find Valor <note>term: Valor; in this context, it means both the virtue of the nobles and the generous rewards/recognition they should give to those who praise virtue.</note> even though I have very poor clothing. I thought they would reward the responses that I have long given, but they are enemies of Valor. And because of this, I left that place, and no valor was shown to me. Never in that court was I rewarded for valor, nor by any of those who should uphold it. They have passed away in their vanity and falsehood. I shall compose a new song <note>original: "sirventes"; a type of satirical poem or song used by troubadours to criticize or mock.</note> in which I shall speak ill of their 'valor' and of those who support them."

"Fair friend," said Blanquerna, "before you fail in this, you must have knowledge of what Valor truly is, and of those who are its servants, so that your words may follow the truth."

"Sir," said the minstrel, "for a long time I have had knowledge of valor, and I have always followed and praised it, yet it never helped my poverty, nor through it could I see a future among these wretched people."

"Friend," said Blanquerna, "if valor were what you say, it would necessarily follow that it should be praised; for if it did not do so, it would not be valor. But it may be that what you call 'valor' is actually 'devalue' <note>A lack of value or worthlessness.</note>, wickedness, and a great failing."

<meta>This page continues the allegorical dialogue from the previous page, featuring Blaquerna and the Emperor. The text shifts from a theoretical discussion of Worth to a narrative scene where the Emperor expresses a desire for spiritual reform and a move toward the eremitic life, followed by a tragic exemplum (moral story) about a shepherd and a wolf.</meta>

# Page 16

The incarnation of the Son of God, which is of such great <term>Worth</term> <note>original: "ualor," a central concept in Llull’s philosophy referring to moral and spiritual excellence</note>, has demonstrated to the faithful who have failings that they should possess the goodness of those things which through Him acquired value. For whoever wishes to exalt their worth in value and in the honor of the Lord must faithfully pray. There are men who want God to value them so that they may be well honored by others; then there are men who love worthiness for the sake of the honor that comes from it. For if one does not act rightly, then that honor is devalued and cannot sustain itself. 

Therefore, let the <term>Worth</term> of our words and the morality of our eyes be filled with the grief that comes to the heart, for it is a great loss. Let them fear the theme that <term>Blaquerna</term> <note>The protagonist of Ramon Llull’s 1283 novel, "Evast e Blaquerna," who seeks spiritual perfection</note> speaks of regarding Worth—which alone possesses value—and these words are from the charity of God and the spirit of wisdom. And through this, a man comes to recognize his failings and his works, so that he may reach fulfillment. One who does not turn toward devaluation will not fall into decadence; for worth cannot be possessed correctly if one does not fear it in their own honoring. Today, the justice of God will not befriend the enemies of Worth. For this reason, a person should follow Blaquerna toward worthiness with prayers and devotion, for Blaquerna is always of a courageous spirit and a helper to Worth. In this way, a man guards his heritage and his soul by running to the Emperor.

<column-break/>

Such a <term>Minstrel</term> <note>original: "iuglar"</note> is the most displeasing and unknown among those who love stolen worth. What would be the words that the Worth of our time would fear? They are sent by the enemy. And all those who are officials of the Emperor and the Minstrel listen to the words that Blaquerna says regarding the Worth of our Lord. 

And if it happens that one is constrained by some will, let him keep a moral mind so that he does not decline in worth. Therefore, the <term>Emperor</term> <note>original: "lempadoz"</note> recognizes his foolishness and gives himself over to Blaquerna and his way of life. Blaquerna’s way of life is one of chastity, and he goes about as a hermit so that in his heart he might have the honor of fleeing the world and its enemies of Worth. 

The humility of God and His pity appeared to the Emperor. Through the mercy of God, he felt a great memory of his sins and became emboldened by these words. "O, I am the only one to blame who, for the sake of following Worth, has followed a path that leads to the loss of what is truly valuable! I have spent all the days of my life following that which has no worth." Then the man recognized his actions and the need for order. And because of this, Blaquerna made a profession that he would no longer be a person who lacks Worth. Rather, he would recover his possession of the arms of spirit which he had lost for a long time. And therefore, he recognized that he should honor Worth in an orderly way. And being filled with this Worth, he should return to his honor, no longer fearing the world, but rejoicing in God. The Emperor, with these words, went to the place where Blaquerna was staying.

---

<margin>Note in the margin: Here Blaquerna tells the Emperor that it is necessary for him to return to the provinces where he used to be.</margin> Beneath a beautiful tree, Blaquerna received the Emperor and the Minstrel very amiably. And the Emperor said these words to Blaquerna. "Blaquerna," said the Emperor, "blessed be the hour that I met you in this world." 

<margin>Note: Of the departure</margin> "Regarding my departure from your agreeable person, it is necessary for me to order my life differently. I must return to the Empire and take a wife, and I must attend to giving to men who love Worth, so that they may be honored. According to the first of these commands, I wish to give this Minstrel a great deal of gold and silver to be distributed by my hand, so that he may record the worthiness in the courts that is being slandered. For many have gained a penalty of worthlessness in all the places they have gone. I desire that no man be praised except for the sake of being a better servant of Worth. And I do not want more sons; I want to spend the time that remains to me with God. In your hermitage, I want to learn how I can have God and Worth through you, so that God may be remembered by me, and so that you may be my companion and my lord."

And a story is told of what happened in the evening, when Blaquerna was traveling. He met a shepherd who was guarding a great flock of beasts. This shepherd had a son of <note>original: "xxiii ans," twenty-three years</note>. Because of the great love the shepherd had for his son, he was very happy. But it happened that the shepherd fell

<column-break/>

asleep, as is the custom of those who are tired. He moved away from the place where his father was sleeping, and a wolf came to the flock and snatched the child and took him. Rising up, the shepherd saw that the child was struggling and that the wolf had taken him and was resisting. The shepherd, with his dogs, chased the wolf, but before he could reach him, the wolf had devoured and killed his son, and had eaten his heart. 

When the shepherd reached the place where he found his son dead, he was distraught and said these words: "Alas, wretched sinner that I am! I have been so evil! My son is dead, and I am the cause of his death! For I loved him so much that my heart is now burdened with sadness and painful grief, and all the horrors of life have softened my heart. Above all, I am desolate; I recognize I am desolate. Above all, I am full of the burning fire of grief. It is so strong that I cannot move my eyes or my heart. Before, I was full of joy, but now I am guilty of this death!" 

As he said these words and wept over his son, he saw the child as a reflection of himself. He saw his love for his son in the death he suffered. "I no longer have any hope in my heart from this moment on. My heart is dead and will not move. Oh, life is moved by love, and now there is no thought of comfort for me! I have proven the pain that burns with love and the heavy grief that follows."

<summary>This page features the Emperor's spiritual conversion after meeting Blaquerna and an allegorical story of a shepherd whose negligence leads to his son being killed by a wolf, illustrating the dangers of spiritual slumber.</summary>
<keywords>Blaquerna, Ramon Llull, Worth, Emperor, Minstrel, repentance, shepherd, wolf, allegory, spiritual negligence</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative from page 16, where a shepherd has just suffered the loss of his family to a wolf. Blanquerna (spelled here as Blaquina) intervenes, moving from a physical struggle with the beast to a deep theological lesson on the nature of grief, the hierarchy of love, and the restoration of the "sen" (rational soul or right mind).</meta>

The songs, plaints, and weeping were so great and full of suffering, through the lamentation of this event, that they would have overcome any heart. <term>Blanquerna’s</term> <note>original: "blaquina"</note> voice was so [gentle] that it moved the heart to its depths. Blanquerna came to that place where the shepherd was weeping and lamenting his son, whom he was kissing and embracing with great fervor. Blanquerna wished to comfort the shepherd, but the shepherd made no sign that he even saw or noticed him, nor did he hear the words, so disturbed was he by the anguish and the pain he was enduring. Blanquerna looked upon the wolf that had killed <margin>the son</margin> and seen the death it had caused, and he thought that he must catch the wolf. Because the shepherd desired only to die and found no consolation, Blanquerna took a mace that the shepherd carried and went toward the wolf swiftly. And as a man moved by piety and for the love of the child, he pursued it. The wolf wanted to flee, but the dog held it long enough for Blanquerna to kill the wolf. Then he said to the shepherd: "Dead is our enemy, through whose death our <term>Justice</term> <note>In Llull's thought, Justice is not just legal, but the divine order that restores balance</note> may turn into consolation."

<column-break/>

From lament to consolation, he began to explain the event, giving wise words to the shepherd. "O son of Henry <note>original: "fil enricu," possibly a specific reference or a generic address</note>, you who live blinded by the love of your son, why do you not lament the damage that your own soul receives from such an unruly grief? You would find lightness in the death of that which you loved so much. Your son is dead; the wolf killed him, and it killed your wife too." The shepherd thought his wife was there, and he believed Blanquerna spoke the truth, and he thought he should not weep but rather embrace the grief he had taken on. While the shepherd thought on this, Blanquerna spoke these words to him: "If your wife and son are dead, you should find consolation in your weeping." Blanquerna replied: "Know truly that your wife was this wolf." The shepherd followed Blanquerna to the place where the wolf lay dead. "This is your wife," said Blanquerna. O, how the shepherd’s heart marveled at these words, and he thought either he had lost his mind or the wolf truly was his wife. But Blanquerna saw that the memory of the shepherd was beginning to return and restore itself to its natural state. As his understanding began to clear, Blanquerna led the shepherd back to the place where his son lay dead, and the man began to wail and tremble with a grief so profound it seemed he would lose his sanity again. The shepherd marveled at the sorrow Blanquerna showed, and the more he marveled, the more he recovered the clarity of mind he had lost; for the shepherd regained his senses and realized it was the wolf of the woods and not his wife. From this joy came

<column-break/>

restraint and a spiritual pride. He came to Blanquerna, holding his son <margin>who was</margin> dead and wailing. "Lord Father, what is this? Why do you weep for my son? Give me back my son, and save me from the sorrow in which I used to dwell." Blanquerna replied to the shepherd: "It is the custom of this land that when a man undergoes loss, he should weep and lament. But you should lament more because of the great love you had for your son. Since this cause brings you such great bitterness, if you wish to weep according to the custom of our land, I will show you the way and the manner by which you may lament and weep for the death of your son as is fitting, and according to how our faith dictates for a mortal death." The shepherd said: "Father, your words are pleasing to me. I pray you to tell me the manner and the custom of our land by which I should weep and lament, so that the death of my son may be even more felt and vulnerable to me." Blanquerna said to the shepherd: "So that you may know the manner in which you may feel this great grief, it is necessary that you first know how to sing of <term>Prudence</term>, <term>Fortitude</term>, and <term>Justice</term>. I require you to swear to follow what you are commanded." The shepherd said: "I will do everything you say, provided you show me the way to have such contrition and sorrow that it may heal the lack of consolation I have for my son."

<column-break/>

Blanquerna asked the shepherd to swear which he loved more: God or his son. The shepherd replied and said that he had more love for his son than for God. Blanquerna said: "You have fallen into error, for a man who loves any thing more than God commits a sin. And <term>Justice</term> is that thing which punishes whoever loves less than God that which should be loved. It is because of your excessive love for your son that God has permitted this mercy through his death. It is the <term>Wisdom of God</term> that from now on you shall love God above all things, so that you may have <term>Prudence</term> in your soul, through which you may have <term>Fortitude</term> against the path in which your heart was trapped. Thus you shall have hope in God and wait for the glory of God." The shepherd began to remember and understand the words Blanquerna gave him. And the more he thought, the more he felt uplifted and unburdened, and he went as if his strength had tripled. At this, he marveled and said to Blanquerna: "The more I remember your words, Father, the more Justice is in me and the more I feel comforted. Where is the Justice that your words have multiplied in me?" Blanquerna replied by asking the shepherd to speak truthfully: before his son was dead, which did he love more, joy or sadness? The shepherd replied that he loved joy more. Blanquerna said: "If your son is dead and you now love sadness more than joy, then your son's death has become a giver of joy, for through this sadness you have returned to God."

<summary>Blanquerna comforts a grieving shepherd by killing the wolf that destroyed his family, subsequently using the tragedy to teach a lesson on "disordered love" and the necessity of loving God above all earthly attachments. Through the virtues of Prudence, Fortitude, and Justice, the shepherd's rational mind is restored, and his grief is transformed into spiritual understanding.</summary>
<keywords>Blanquerna, shepherd, wolf, allegory, Divine Justice, Prudence, Fortitude, hierarchy of love, grief, Ramon Llull, Medieval Catalan literature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Blanquerna and the grieving shepherd. Blanquerna transitions from the specific tragedy of the shepherd’s lost family to a broader philosophical and theological discourse on the nature of "Fortitudo" (Fortitude or Strength), moving from physical force to the superior strength of the spirit.</meta>

...should be a notable [lesson]; you must not give [sadness] such dominion that it starves the soul. Instead, meet sadness with patience and joy. If you did so before, you must do so much more strongly now, for your love for your son should be greater now than when your son was alive.

**Blanquerna** <note>original: "Blaquina"</note> said to the shepherd: "And how could it be that my son is more against my soul, since he did not wish to kill me?" Blanquerna responded that through **patience**, one can find comfort in the great joy of everything that **Divine Justice** <term>Justice: In Llull's philosophy, one of the "Dignities" or attributes of God; it represents the perfect ordering of the universe.</term> performs. This brings gladness to the **heart** <note>original: "coratge," which in Old Catalan means both "heart" and "courage"</note> where **fortitude** resides, standing against [adversity]. One finds joy in true prudence and humility, receiving high wisdom. Just as a knight bridles his horse against great physical force, a man must turn toward the spiritual. This is in accord with the celestial and spiritual life for all time, which is everlasting. 

Long would be the recording of the words Blanquerna said to the shepherd to comfort him for the death of his [wife] <note>The OCR reads "marem" (mother), but the context suggests the shepherd's wife/the child's mother.</note>, through which Blanquerna led the shepherd out of sadness and brought him to consolation and joy. The shepherd, receiving those words, said: "My soul is gladdened from now on, in harmony with its Creator. I now possess the virtues I did not used to have, and I would not know how to stray. My son is a great prize; he is received by his Lord in His glory. My soul will fly to Him forever, having the power of my Lord God." 

When the shepherd had said these words and was transformed, he took his son and kissed him, and placed him upon his neck, blessing and praising God, saying that the profit he gained through death was greater—through the **virtue of love**—than the loss he feared. For through love, he recovered the love of his [Creator].

<column-break/>

Blanquerna and the shepherd remained together. Pleasantly, Blanquerna spoke to the shepherd so that he would always remain in this remembrance, consolation, and patience. The shepherd asked how he could console his wife for the death of their son, for her grief was entirely human. The shepherd asked Blanquerna for the grace of knowing how he should comfort his wife in the same manner that Blanquerna had comforted him. Blanquerna told the shepherd that he should explain to his wife that the love of their son, and the death of one so innocent, would bring her to a state where she would find herself in the praise of God, and thus she would have true consolation. He would go to her and show her this.

### Of Fortitude, Part 1

In the forest where Blanquerna was traveling, there was a very strong castle belonging to a **knight**. On the occasion of a feast at the castle, many people of high status had gathered, and there was much display of arms. There was a knight there, proud and arrogant in love, who ignored the moral customs of all those staying in that region. This knight waited all alone on his horse, dressed in the armor he was accustomed to wearing, near a castle belonging to a lady who loved her daughter very much. 

It happened that the knight found the **maiden** <note>original: "donzella"</note> outside the castle gates with other maidens. He seized her by the neck of his horse and took her by force from the other maidens, carrying her into the deep forest. Great was the cry and the clamor raised against the knight so that they might take the 

<column-break/>

maiden back from him. While the knight was carrying her away, the maiden wept and cried out very loudly. Blanquerna, hearing her, followed the knight. The knight stopped to struggle with her; his horse stumbled and he lost his grip on the maiden as he headed toward his castle. 

Blanquerna, walking through the forest, came upon the scene. He saw the maiden weeping and crying, and Blanquerna [confronted] the knight. But Blanquerna considered that his own physical power was weak compared to the power of the knight. Therefore, he helped the maiden with **Fortitude**, which is spiritual strength, and he gave the knight this **exemplum** <term>Exemplum: A moralizing story or illustrative anecdote used to emphasize a point in a sermon or philosophical text.</term>:

"A certain good man, learned in philosophy, theology, and other sciences, had the devotion to give himself up to the **Saracens** <note>The medieval term for Muslims.</note> for the sake of the Holy Catholic faith, so that he might destroy the errors of the Saracens and that the name of God might be exalted and blessed. As is known, this holy man went into the land of the Saracens and demonstrated the truth of our Law [Christianity] and destroyed the law of Muhammad as much as he could. When this was reported to the King of the Saracens, he became very indignant against the holy man and had him brought before him.

The King spoke these words: 'How have you dared to despise my commands and my lordship? Do you not see that I have so much power that I can torture you? And what pride or power do you have that you have despised my great power?' 

'Lord,' said the Christian, 'it is true that your physical power can overcome and force my body. But the strength of my **heart** <margin>The soul can transform the strength of my heart</margin> cannot be seen or forced by the strength of your heart, nor by the strength that is in the hearts of all the men of your faith. For strength of heart is more noble and greater than physical strength. Therefore, I do not fear the physical force you have in your person or in your kingdom. I am prepared to die with all the powers of my soul against all the weapons in your entire kingdom.'" 

The King was greatly amazed by the words of this 

<column-break/>

man. The Christian explained the source of his courage, by which he surpassed all the external forces and all the guards the Saracen had in his land. 

"Lord," said the Christian, "so great a cause is the **Incarnation** of the Son of God and the **Passion** He sustained to save us, that it is a powerful cause that drives out all falsehood. Because of this, I am inflamed with the fire of Fortitude in my heart, more so than any natural strength. Not all the men you have possess the fortitude that I have through my Creator. And this is because all of you are in error..."

<summary>Blanquerna uses the theological concept of Fortitude to transition from the shepherd's personal grief to a story about a maiden's rescue and a Christian's brave confrontation with a Saracen king. The text argues that spiritual strength (strength of heart) is far superior to physical force.</summary>
<keywords>Fortitude, Blanquerna, Divine Justice, courage, knight and maiden, Christian and Saracen, spiritual strength vs physical power, Ramon Llull</keywords>

in strength and resistance. Of Penance.

remembering, and as he went, he was a creator

entered, and it was the ninth hour, when he found

dead horse, crying loudly and showing in his
countenance great sadness. Blame
asked the squire why he was full of grief.
The squire responded: "Sir, I cry
because a lord with whom I was,
whom I had served, has turned me

envious of disordered habits

near here in an abbey <note>original: "abadia" - a monastery or abbey</note> in which the monk

there to do penance. But the penance
in which he stays is like the penance

squire, "What is the penance of the wolf?"
"Lord," said the squire, "I have heard it told
concerning God, that a wolf entered a fold
where there were many sheep and killed them;
on the following morning, when the
shepherd entered, he caught him
and found many of the sheep dead. He had
great malice against the shepherd who had not
guarded the flock that night.
And the shepherd being dead, he [the wolf]
wailed for the death of the shepherd and of the
sheep—the wolf who had killed the shepherd."

    *Self-Correction during drafting:* The OCR says "seglar" and "senglar". In Old Catalan, *senglar* is a wild boar. The story of the fox and the boar is a common fable.

    *Parrot story:* The parrot tries to warn a "foolish one" (*asina*) that the lamp isn't fire. The parrot gets caught. Lesson: don't try to correct those who won't listen/don't want doctrine.

    *Fox and Boar story:* The boar sharpens his tusks to fight a lion. The fox asks why. Lesson: preparation for spiritual battle.

    *Fox and Chickens story:* Blanquerna serves Narpan a fox. Narpan is horrified because foxes eat chickens (which he likes/keeps). Blanquerna points out that Narpan’s lifestyle (eating delicacies) is as destructive to his soul/penance as a fox is to chickens.

**Folio 20v**

rather he wants the foolish sparrow. Heart, have heart; of that which falls, it would be fitting that he did penance for the harm he had given the sparrow. He found her in the place where Narpan died, in a <unclear>minha</unclear> <note>possibly a cell or small dwelling</note> where Narpan had died, who belonged to the sparrow to whom he had shown the way to sit and go to do penance. He should strike out his whole testament through his will, for in a similar manner, the lord with whom I have stayed a long time would repent; for he finds a sinful man in the world and kills him: you have made him dead because they recover. And in this monastery where he ate so much, his will was in great vanity, from which he gave a bad example to all the monks of that monastery: dead from the <unclear>nā</unclear> <note>possibly "wrath" or "injury"</note> that comes. 

"Lord," said Blanquerna, "it seems to me that you did well to come to the monastery." He stayed for a time with Narpan to see if he could heal him with good men. The squire replied that if you stay with him, harm would come to you just as it did to the parrot <note>original: "papagay"</note>. Blanquerna asked him to tell the example of the parrot. 

"Lord," said the squire, "in a <unclear>fra</unclear> <note>perhaps a field or clearing</note> it happened that a foolish woman <note>original: "asina," literally a she-ass, but often used to mean a foolish person</note> was lodging; she found a lantern. She thought it was fire; she blew on the lantern so that the fire would spread. A parrot was in a tree and, seeing the foolish woman, saw what the lantern was and said: 'Alas, how much you labor in vain!' The parrot had a body that did not wish to labor in the correction of those who do not want doctrine. The parrot descended from the tree and placed himself between the foolish woman and the light, and the woman 

<column-break/>

caught him. The parrot died. It is a sign that if you wish to correct those who do not receive correction, the desire to do so becomes corrupted against some; they are called contrary sons." 

Blanquerna said, "Keep your merit in the <unclear>educall</unclear> <note>teaching or upbringing</note> that the fox <note>original: "uolp"</note> tells to the wild boar <note>original: "fĕglar/senglar"</note>." The squire begged Blanquerna to tell him that example. 

Blanquerna said: "A fox was going through a forest and found a wild boar who was sharpening his tusks because he wanted to fight a lion. The fox asked the boar what he was doing, and the boar recounted his courage. The fox told the boar that he did not have the tools with which he could fight the lion, for the lion had killed many of his kind, and it seemed that the lion would have the better of the battle. The lion came to fight with the wild boar, and having retreated, the boar made good use of his weapons. In a similar way, you must make good use of your weapons against Narpan, for he would be conquered with the help of the cardinal virtues <note>original: "ortunals uertuts" - referring to the four moral virtues: Prudence, Justice, Fortitude, and Temperance</note>." 

When the squire had heard the example, he took leave of Blanquerna, believing that Blanquerna was constant in the path that could lead to the monastery. The bad example of Narpan, who failed in his penance and singing, made him go to the monastery where they found the Narpan of whom the squire had spoken. Blanquerna said to Narpan: "I wish to stay a year or more with you, lord."

---

**Folio 21r**

"Lord," said Blanquerna, "I come in the <unclear>p̄ p̄sa feria</unclear> <note>likely "presence of the fair" or a specific holy day</note> and have become a monk; I will stay with the lord in all the prayer he raises, so that he can take relief, for you have asked me about my journey. I do not know what the state of this monastery is in which you stay." 

Narpan replied to Blanquerna: "I am in this monastery to do penance for the sins I have committed in the world, from which I have fled. And I have made a pact with the master to be the least among them; and another master says that if I do not wish to stay with him, I must do his will, for I labor in such a way that it makes me repent." 

"Lord," said Blanquerna, "if you are in penance, I wish you to be as one who is truly penitent. If this is so, staying with you for a year would be a benefit to me." For when Blanquerna recognized the repentance, Narpan behaved according to his own will, for which Narpan conceived a liking in his heart. Blanquerna recognized the <unclear>colūn af</unclear> <note>perhaps "column" or "fault"</note> of Narpan. 

On another day, Narpan told Blanquerna that he should bring one of those hens that he held in the enclosure, and that he should cook it for Blanquerna to eat. He put his hand in the coop and pulled out a hen with its throat cut. But the fox was sitting in the corner of that hand. Blanquerna took the fox and asked him for the flesh of the hen he had killed. When Narpan went to the table, Blanquerna placed the dead fox before Narpan. He was marveled; Narpan looked 

<column-break/>

at why he did not bring the poultry but instead brought the fox, which was in a wretched state. 

"Lord," said Blanquerna, "you have my chickens, and they have such a mortal enemy as the fox. And because you love your chickens, you should know that his nature is to kill them. Many times, Narpan, Blanquerna has killed a hundred." 

Narpan asked why he confessed that he had killed the fox and had not brought the hen. 

"Lord," said Blanquerna, "in this matter, the fox is like your own contrary desires toward the chickens. If you have the chickens, you must guard them, as they are your protection. And because you are obligated to flee the world according to the form of penance, if you eat the fox with the others and the delicate foods that you used to eat, such penance is false." 

Therefore, Narpan promised that he would not eat in that way, and he agreed with Blanquerna. At night, when he wanted to go to his place, Blanquerna had laid out a rough bed of straw and branches. Narpan said to Blanquerna: "Why have you not made the place according to how I am accustomed?" 

Blanquerna replied that this place was made according to the work of penance, and that in no other way should a man make his bed. Narpan was a man who did not want to make the place as he was accustomed to doing. Narpan expected that Blanquerna would kneel and serve him as he was accustomed to being served. But Blanquerna did not wish to humiliate his mission for all those who do penance. That night, Narpan stayed in the place he had.

<summary>Blanquerna uses animal parables—the parrot, the boar, and the fox—to critique the superficial penance of Narpan, eventually confronting him for wanting delicate food and soft beds while claiming to live a life of religious austerity.</summary>
<keywords>Blanquerna, Narpan, penance, allegory, monastery, fox, parrot, wild boar, austerity, Ramon Llull, 13th century</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Blanquerna's stay at the monastery with Narpan, following the previous discussions on true penance and the hypocrisy of living in luxury while claiming to be a hermit.</meta>

A woman who loved Blanquerna had once enlightened the eyes of her soul <note>Catalan: "lamuat los mells de s’arma", likely meaning she had received spiritual illumination through his teachings</note>, so that from then on and for the rest of her life she desired to depart from this world and die here in love, so that she might be present before God with Blanquerna. She was helped by the Abbot and all the monks to remain in God's grace for a time. However, because she saw the great height of the words spoken by Narpan and Blanquerna, Narpan and Blanquerna remained all alone in Narpan's chamber. Blanquerna did not forget his purpose or his prayers. He went out from his hermitage and approached Narpan to ask if he would release him from the promise <note>original: "catge", referring to a pledge or formal agreement</note> he had made to stay there, to which he was bound for one year. And since he had already recognized the failings of his conduct, he wished to return the letter <note>original: "carta", the written contract of their agreement</note> so that he might be absolved of his covenant.

It greatly displeased Narpan when he understood that Blanquerna wanted to depart from the monastery, and he began to weep and said these words to Blanquerna: "The light of my soul is lost! My hope is turned to anger. My monks are waiting for a confession of my deeds—how can I hide my wicked name? For if I am now without the one who loves me, I will surely fall into the sea of death, for I remain in such great ignorance and feel myself so guilty." Narpan begged that the agreement be upheld and that Blanquerna fulfill his promise. He spoke of how he would not have the strength of the falcon <note>An allegory for the soul's flight toward God</note> if he were left in his falseness. Blanquerna, seeing that Narpan was sincere in his grief, put on his humble garments once more. "I must stay in this monastery," he said, "to plant seeds for the Lord and for all the community that has been so kind to me."

<column-break/>

"It behooves me to worship in the temple and to strive for the improvement of my soul," he said. And he remained in that monastery, for they were very devoted. In hearing the words of Narpan, which he spoke to prove his spirit, Blanquerna was moved to great devotion and weeping. With great affection and spiritual hunger, he proposed to stay there for that whole year with Narpan, so that he might be an example of life to all the monks of that community. And so it was. Blanquerna confirmed to Narpan the promise he had made to him, and they both dressed in hairshirts <note>original: "uestit ala cin cestíua", referring to the rough penitential clothing worn by ascetics</note>. During that year in the monastery, they gave glory to the love of God and performed penance. This pleased Narpan, the Abbot, and the whole community, for Blanquerna had returned to his first fervor.

->And here, in the presence of those watching him,<-

Blanquerna and Narpan spent their time in penance. When Blanquerna sang with the monks in the church, he did so with an elevated heart. He responded to the liturgy with a voice that echoed through the monastery. Soon the Abbot and all the monks requested that Blanquerna officially become a monk <note>term: <term>monge</term> - a member of a religious order living under vows of poverty, chastity, and obedience</note>, for they saw he was a great master of theology <note>original: "theologia", the study of the nature of God and religious belief</note> and the other sciences, which he knew well. But Blanquerna preferred to serve as a humble monk, for he wished to practice the humility he had learned as a hermit. On a feast day, they wanted to praise him, but he would not accept it; instead, he chose to remain quiet because he did not wish to speak the language of the world, but only the language of the spirit. He felt a great shame for his past words when he realized their failings. When the Abbot and the whole community were in the Chapter House <note>term: <term>capitol</term> - the room where monks met daily to discuss business and hear a chapter of the monastic rule</note>, Blanquerna and Narpan entered the room.

<summary>Blanquerna agrees to stay at the monastery for a year to guide Narpan in true penance, eventually being recognized by the monks for his profound knowledge of theology and his deep humility.</summary>
<keywords>Blanquerna, Narpan, penance, monastery, Abbot, theology, Chapter House, spiritual illumination, falcon allegory, hairshirt</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Blanquerna's internal struggle and his role as a spiritual teacher within the monastery. Following the previous page's discussion on penance and the nature of the soul, Blanquerna here addresses a sick monk, explains the relationship between the intellect and the physical body, and ultimately prepares to return to his life as a contemplative hermit.</meta>

He said to the sick man that the intellect was a nobler cause than many physical things, and that the body should be more obedient to the intellect than the intellect to the body. For the intellect is provided for itself, which intellect, through divine power, is miraculous. It is contained within the <term>Leptis</term> <note>Likely referring to a subtle substance or a specific medieval anatomical/philosophical term for the vessel of the spirit.</note> in such a way that it demonstrates a great degree of movement. Naturally, they are forms just as the three elements are, those which are invisible; otherwise, they would be physical movements, which are not seen except only as the form of the light in which the first form is composed. This form exists secretly in its natural state in many works, all the more to reach the power, the holiness, and the will of God, being a source of peace. If this were not in any way from God, he would not demonstrate that his power is above natural power. 

These words and others Blanquerna said to the sick man, who then received the body of Jesus Christ <note>original: "cor'p de ihu x’t"</note>. To the faithful Christian, God gave health, having guarded him from the error in which he had been. God moved the soul of the sick man. The Abbot and the monks were fearful at the words they were accustomed to hearing from Blanquerna. Blanquerna said to them: "I give you an example and a sign that God wants us to be mindful when in company. For if you were not with us, this soul which today has passed from this world would have been lost; God in his <term>disnatio</term> <note>Divine providence or dispensation.</note> would have been hidden from us by the ignorance that is in us, so that we would not have known how to say the words that you have heard said. And so it happens at times that God brings such cases so that a man may be taken by the will of God." 

<column-break/>

"Not only for the profit of the soul of the dead man, but so that all of us might have a change of heart through right reason, if we find ourselves in a similar case." 

Blanquerna answered: "There is a way by which a man can be healed of error, which is by receiving the body of Jesus Christ with knowledge of oneself. For God wants that which is obedient to him, especially because the imagination often wants to cause a man to fear falsely through appearances, rather than trusting the work of divine power. And in this way, you will lose the fear that comes from ignorance and the lack of science." 

Then Blanquerna answered more strongly concerning following those who live in error, [saying it is better] to live with intellect and faith than with reason alone. And in passing through this vision, he comforted their hearts, for his words were to them as words of life for the company. 

### Of Counsel. Chapter 50.

The lover <note>original: "Liqueryu" (The seeker or lover of God).</note> considered very firmly how to love that which he sought within himself, for it seemed to happen in the repentance of the dead man. While Blanquerna was meditating in this manner, the Abbot and all the monks prayed that he would speak more words to them. Blanquerna said, in uttering these words: "Amiable, amiable friend! Do not offer to the power of the <term>st ubo’</term> <note>Possibly a corrupted form of 'stablished' or a specific monastic rank.</note> as Abbot and all the convent. And set your understanding, which God gives with intelligence through grace, even better than comfort in his belief; for to love with light and fear is more equal to having knowledge of what you believe. For the will appears more strongly when one exalts the love of God and his saints. Therefore, if you give yourselves as monks, I give myself 

<column-break/>

to you, that I might be your companion. And let me be secured by your mercy, and all yours. And if I give myself, it is only on the condition that you grant me your mercy alone." 

Blanquerna spoke in a figure of speech to the contemplative man who stays all alone for the love of God, and not by the fortune of any other, but by himself alone. "In a true manner, I will be in prayer, to know through the conscience how much I am to love God’s will. Even while being under divine rule, I will sharpen my understanding so that it may be equal to all. And my conscience makes me remember the Chapter of God, where I am an image that loves to learn with authenticity; for they do not have before them..." 

In uttering these things, Blanquerna said to the Lord Abbot and all the others that in all his thoughts he was [seeking] to be before God, and he prayed that he might be transformed into another. For he confessed himself... many times the lover began to keep vigil. The Abbot and all the others prayed to God that this would be his right reason, that Blanquerna’s fame be retained and their rights be saved. 

Heartily, he entered into that night under the feelings that Blanquerna had entered into. For in his mind, another thing was kept in all holiness, for he renounced everything to return to his solitude, according to what he had previously known and understood. The understanding understands more clearly because it is a double retention. At another time, after the meal, Blanquerna was in his prayer in a naked state of mind, remembering in his soul the truth... and in his feelings. Afterwards, he began to love. 

<column-break/>

Blanquerna, holy in prayer, weeping and praying to his spirit throughout the night, so that his mind would not be more weary. That night he was able to keep vigil and consider. And if his eyes did not want to stay awake, he would move so that he could return to his meditation. And in his prayer, the imagination began to act with the understanding so that he could rise. 

Blanquerna looked up to see the sky and the stars so that he might increase his reason and feeling toward the high and affectionate will. He prayed to God that it would please Him to make him remember how he was enlightened by these works... and through the will of God, it happened that he who was a monk, because he considered that virtue... in the monastery... 

Blanquerna was firm in his purpose, remembering that one should live and consider in that manner. He had perceived that conception... and moved so that the greatness of the truth would not be naked. He placed himself in consolation and in that poverty that comes from being a hermit... showing that his life in the monastery seemed to him... When Blanquerna had perceived this knowledge, he went to the Chapter house where the Abbot and all the convent were, who were speaking together about the life of Blanquerna. Blanquerna knelt before the Abbot and all the convent.

**Column 1**

He reproached and corrected him. No thing should exist between the Prior <note>The second-in-command of the monastery, responsible for internal discipline.</note> and the poor man at the end, which in the mind exalts nobility; so that the vine... of our sisters... the Prior went joyfully up the hill, for it was fitting for him to go to the window. Now the [faith] was multiplied in that monastery through that which Blanquerna taught; for Blanquerna, as a master, was the cause that the arrow <note>A metaphor for spiritual longing or the "dart of love."</note> of this world was understood. Frequently, Blanquerna would pray and contemplate, in which he did not judge himself to be [worthy], but rather a servant who studied. In this work, he labored with great mercy. 

The Abbot and all the convent desired that Blanquerna perform this [office]. And another monk, who was a fellow master, [concurred] that Blanquerna should go and rule. Blanquerna, newly ordained as a priest, received this with great honor and justice, because he did not seek it for himself, but rather spiritually. He sang a lament, a new song, without any [vanity]. Blanquerna was elected [to this office]. Day and night in the church, Blanquerna [served] the church and stayed there; he returned to sing mass and [cared for] the food of the soul. He came to the altar with hunger and thirst for the spirit, and the people were moved because Blanquerna's prayer was not common, nor was the judgment he had in his contemplation. He did not tire his soul, but rather found joy in the presence of God.

<column-break/>

After **Compline** <term>Compline: the final prayer service of the day, held before retiring for the night</term>, Blanquerna remained in [the church] until it was full [of light] for his soul. He prayed fervently to God, and Blanquerna [was moved] by what he considered. While Blanquerna was in this thought, he fell into a sleep in which, while waking, he had confidence. Through the influence of the great light that he had while he was in [prayer], he saw a book in his dream that was flaming. Saint Michael, Saint Gabriel, and Saint Raphael <note>The three archangels often associated with protection, revelation, and healing.</note> appeared to Blanquerna, and that vision returned to him in his sleep and was revealed to him. 

When the time came, Blanquerna rang the bell for **Matins** <term>Matins: the first prayer service of the day, traditionally held in the middle of the night or very early morning</term> and spoke to the monks, and they understood what had happened that night. While Blanquerna remembered these things, he returned to the altar for the mass. He was filled with wonder at the light he had seen. An angel stood at the side of the altar and showed him a book... Blanquerna was amazed by this vision. He did not wish to [leave] but rather to remain in that prayer until the [service] was finished. Blanquerna remembered the [promises] and the light which [guided] him in his necessity, providing him with strength and dignity. The angels presented him with understanding and a comprehension of the divinity of God, through which he was able to follow the path of salvation.

<meta>This page continues the description of Blanquerna’s spiritual development within the monastery. Having previously established himself as a model of devotion, the narrative now shifts toward his internal struggle with divine virtues and his eventual elevation to the rank of Abbot.</meta>

**Column 1**

...in several ways together with virtue. Fortitude <note>original: *fortitudo*</note> consists of strengthening the heart against the imagination when it leaps or denies, for the imagination often acts in a disorderly way. Because one thing can be perceived through various shifting appearances, Blanquerna was aided by all these virtues in the midst of the doubt in which he was placed. This refers to the state of mind of one who dies to the world <note>A common medieval trope for monastic life—being "dead" to worldly desires.</note> as he was accustomed to doing. 

It happened one day that Blanquerna was in the church all alone, considering the fear of God with a full heart. He felt a sudden spiritual trembling at first, as he contemplated within himself. Though he felt this fear, his heart remained steadfast. This fear was felt throughout the whole church; he felt enveloped by a great light. In that moment, he remembered that his heart was strengthened by fortitude <note>original: *fortitudo*</note>, for fortitude is a virtue that resides in the soul. Through this, Blanquerna remembered the power of God, which he preferred above all else in the church, remaining there alone with his desire and the company of his thoughts. Blanquerna was in prayer, and he began to remember the place within the power of God where he was situated. He considered the power of God and moved forward with great strength.

**In** many ways Blanquerna was prepared in his devotion. It happened that while he was prepared, he remembered the virtue of Faith, according to that virtue which serves to mortify <note>In a religious sense, "mortifying" means subduing one's physical desires or ego.</note> the flesh so as not to offend God. In this virtue, he took care that

<column-break/>

Blanquerna responded through the memory he had of the other virtues. Blanquerna was immersed in the virtues, and within him was found Temperance <note>original: *temperantia*</note>, through which he balanced his soul. He felt the light of God, which guided his whole soul. He felt the great perfection of the Justice of God <note>original: *iustitia*</note>. In this state lived Blanquerna, a servant of God, with great honor and joy. While the other monks were sleeping, God took away fear from Blanquerna and filled him with light. 

The monks were gathered in a dignified and confident manner. They were moved by the great constancy of Blanquerna, and all the people of that region spoke of the abundance of his spiritual fruits. They saw that he was a true servant of God, who lived in unity of faith. In that world, he was a mirror of God's grace.

### In what manner Blanquerna was elected Abbot

The Lord Abbot <note>The head of the monastery.</note>, reaching an age where he no longer possessed his health, and seeing the needs of the monastery, came into the chapter house <note>The room where monks gathered for business and daily readings.</note> with the whole community. He spoke to them: "For a long time, I have been your superior. I have held the office of major monk. But now, I perceive that I am failing. Among us has come a person who is most strong and worthy to be our father and protector. It is necessary for the order that we provide for a successor. I ask that you elect an Abbot, and may God's mercy guide your counsel." Everyone praised Blanquerna and said

<column-break/>

that a great favor should be done for the Lord Abbot. It was found that everyone agreed that Blanquerna should take on the labor and the work that he had long avoided. They sang a hymn of thanks for the help that had been given to them. In the chapter house, where the monks were gathered, they said that it was fitting for a younger man to serve as Abbot, one who was inspired by the spirit and who could lead the monks and care for their souls. 

Blanquerna was then elected as Abbot. The news of his election was received with great joy. When they told Blanquerna, he showed a humble countenance, for this choice was made by the common will of the brothers. He was told that he should signify his acceptance so that the election could be finalized. It was said that he was the most saintly person and would be a benefit to all. He was full of grace, more so than the others. They argued that it was not contrary to God's will that he be the Abbot, and they commanded that he should not refuse the office. They trusted that he would follow the rule <note>The "Rule" refers to the specific set of laws governing the monks' daily life, likely the Rule of Saint Benedict.</note> better than anyone else. Blanquerna was found by the electors to be the most suitable to be elected Abbot, and they trusted that he would lead with mercy and contemplation. He was the one who was

<column-break/>

best and closest to God. Between the contemplative life <note>A life focused on prayer and meditation.</note> and the active life <note>A life focused on work and administrative duties.</note>, one must choose the best path to serve the monastery. They intended that his election as Abbot would allow his contemplation to be the cause of even greater active service, as he had shown in the past. 

Then the cellarer <note>The monk in charge of the monastery's food and supplies.</note> spoke to Blanquerna, saying that if he was elected, he should not refuse the burden, for the monastery needed him. He spoke of the austerity and the discipline required in the monastery. He insisted that he had been chosen and that he should not refuse. Blanquerna responded to what was said, saying that he feared the responsibility. He spoke with humility and asked if there were not others better suited to be elected. However, if the community and the spirit were in agreement, and if it was for the common good, he would not resist. He responded that he would take up the labor. He feared that he was not strong enough for the election, but he put his trust in God. Blanquerna prayed and asked that this decision be for the best. The Abbot and the community were strengthened in their resolve, and the election was finalized.

<meta>This page follows Blanquerna’s spiritual exercises and his leadership within the monastery. It describes a transition from temporal concerns to a state of spiritual "fruition" or divine enjoyment, guided by the personification of the Queen (representing the Virgin Mary or Divine Grace).</meta>

...to separate oneself from temporal things <note>The text uses "carmenar," literally to card wool, here used metaphorically to untangle or strip away worldly attachments.</note> so that they do not hinder the soul from convening in its spiritual and celestial form. Through this, the Queen <note>Likely referring to the Virgin Mary or a personification of Divine Grace.</note>, because of the fruition <term>fruition: the theological concept of the soul’s joyful enjoyment of God</term> in which she existed, began to lift herself up to contemplate God and to consider the Passion of our Lord. For this reason, she said these prayers:

"Third Friend, who is the Holy Mother, stay with me in the grace of fruition in which they wished to place me in this Solomon <note>original: "saloma"; possibly referring to the Song of Solomon or a place of peace</note> where she is. Mother, why have you not helped me in the fruition into which I have fallen?"

While the Queen of our Lord offered this prayer in that state, through our Lady, Fortitude <note>original: "fortitudo"</note> came to her through these prayers. My heart is made such that it does not fear the worst: neither through envy nor through what I feel. The Prince is Obedient, for the movement of the heart is governed according to how the understanding shows it. Justice <note>original: "justicia"</note> also acts in these matters.

While Fortitude spoke in this way, Blanquerna <note>The OCR reads "p s y na r aua," likely a corruption of Blanquerna.</note> retired from all the holy monks to be apart, having the desire in his heart to finish his prayers. Blanquerna then set about to govern his own person. He governed his habits so that his person, the Passion, Justice, and his mind remained with me in a way they were not accustomed to before. The Queen desired, through Constancy <note>original: "carstancia"</note>, that our Lord receive much honor from this grace. He loved it so much that he could no longer contemplate God as he was accustomed to, for his mind was so full of grace. For this reason, they made him bow for a long time in this manner.

<column-break/>

In her own way, the Queen remained for a long time dejected <note>original: "abatut"</note>. Oh, what a desire for consolation and contemplation before dying and going to the grace where she was! The monk who used to be the Abbot <note>Referring to Blanquerna's predecessor or a change in roles.</note> came to him, and with him, he refreshed his person and consoled his soul by contemplating God. In that manner, the Abbot **Blanquerna** <note>OCR: "Blaquina"</note> acted as Abbot.

The Queen... they gave him the book which begins with many "Ave Marias." The monks felt a great reverence, and soon they considered Blanquerna’s body... our Lady... in a new manner. Blanquerna considered how the divine power came to him, and he wished to go throughout the monastery and enter a private room. The monk who was in that room saluted our Lady in a great manner. He did not follow the usual customs of the convent <note>The monastic community as a whole.</note> because he was occupied with saluting and contemplating.

The Abbot Blanquerna went to the room... in the Chapter house <term>Chapter house: the room where monks meet for business and to hear a "chapter" of the Rule</term> was the Abbot with all the convent. He spoke many holy words. Because the mealtime had passed, he received... while he was doing this, he maintained his state of mind as he considered God through human nature. For this reason, it was fitting to honor him. "Who among us," he asked, "wants to salute our Lady?"

Those who wished to do so remained in the room and did not go to eat. This was done by the monks who did not want to lose the spiritual state they found in that room. Thus the Abbot said that every monk who had a soul should be in this state.

...greeting Our Lady, who is the Mother and the blessing. In her greeting, he is comforted. He who feels his salvation in our Mother... Our Lady is the aid. Prayer is a very high light and is illuminated. She is the one who removes evil and failure. Greetings and Amen. Lady, do not let us fall into any other thing. Our Lady gives to those who greet her. "Her greeting is my consolation, my company, my comfort, and my everything." And it was a great pleasure for the monk. Because he failed so much in greeting and contemplating Our Lady, many times he went to greet Our Lady with the monk to please her. And the other, who contemplated Our Lady, remained there more strongly. When it was all done, the monk saw that many monks in that monastery followed Our Lady most certainly. Great were the <term>alms: charitable gifts of food or money given to the poor</term> <note>original: "almona"</note> and the labor done in that monastery for the poor who came. Because of the fame of this, many people ran to this monastery for the alms of bread and legumes. And many poor people were there for the alms. Then it happened that the <term>cellarer: the monk responsible for the monastery's food and drink supplies</term> <note>original: "cellarier"</note> entered into the great needs that were in the monastery. He noted that the alms they were giving could not last long, for the convent did not have enough grain for the alms. The cellarer and the others who gave the alms were worried, for they had so little grain left that they feared the time would come when the convent would run out of supplies.

<meta>This page continues the narrative from the previous folio, where the monastery faced a severe shortage of wheat for alms.
