<page-num>32</page-num>

**Full of grace** <note>original: "gratia plena"</note>. And for this reason, the granary keeper <term>granier: the monk in charge of the monastery's grain stores</term> trusted in Our Lady, who is always full. The harvest of wheat <note>original: "blat"</note> flourished while he served in that land. With total consideration, the Abbot reflected on what the brother granary keeper had said. For our Lady had more devotion in "Full of Grace." And for this, he stayed in a place in the monastery, an oratory which he named "Full of Grace." He kept that chamber empty so that I, the brother, could at all times spend my hunger <note>The text suggests "famea," perhaps meaning a spiritual hunger or fasting</note> adoring and contemplating Our Lady, full of grace. For now, the saint is in "Full of Grace," and it is my devout wish to see his Father. Oh Lady! For all the monks considered the grace of which she was full. And the young girl <note>likely a reference to the Virgin as a young girl</note> and the holy path that was in that brother varied. Sometimes the monks would recount with the brother their primary devotion, through which they were identified as monks in their devotion. Through these, they were consoled and gladdened. For this monastery was illuminated by that brother and by the "Ave Maria" <note>The "Hail Mary" prayer</note>. And many times the monk of "Ave Maria" and the brother of "Full of Grace" would visit one another. The monk speaking of "Ave Maria" and the brother of "Full of Grace" found pleasure in the holy company of both, which provided a long example to all the monks and to all those like them that I could tell you about. The Abbot Blanquerna <note>The protagonist of Ramon Llull's novel</note> began to weep and to contemplate Our Lady when the great needs of the poor—whom he had to support through the monastery—had very strongly clouded his thoughts in distress. For he already felt a sense of peace because he saw the Abbot go and return to the brother of "Full of Grace."

<column-break/>

He was alone, and he wished to see in what manner he contemplated Our Lady. While the Abbot entered into the chamber where the brother was, he found the brother kneeling before the image of Our Lady. And in praying, he said these words: "You are the hand <note>original: "mana"</note> of piety, which is the fulfillment of all that is in you. And for you is the remembrance and the kindling of the will of my soul. Because the world cannot fulfill that which you fulfill. And for this reason, because you are most full, for your Son who willed to be in the world, let the world not be so corrupted. You are full of grace so that we might recover the grace that we had lost. In you is full our hope, charity, virtue, prudence, fortitude, temperance. We are filled because you are full. Full is the grace of those who remember and love you. Through you, those who are loved are received without failing. Full of grace, you were the fruit of God, God and man, when the Angel Gabriel <note>The archangel of the Annunciation</note> gave his greeting. On that day, the root of grace, you were the mirror of the vineyard and the faithful finish of truth, with will and merit. Your Son, God, is full, eternal and infinite. In the fullness of grace is power, wisdom, and will. This fullness makes us pray today toward your fullness in this world, so that with your 'Full of Grace' you may be the hand that fills our soul and mind with charity, so that we may be able to fully love and know you. And fulfill our end in our Lord, in the grace of our treasures, full of pleasure, where the smallest things are filled with your hand's grace." According to these words and many others, the brother contemplated Our Lady, praising her name and her motherhood.

***

<meta>Translation of the left-facing page (Folio 31v) provided for context and continuity of the miracle story.</meta>

The granary keeper <note>original: "granier"</note> went to work on what the cellarer <term>cellerier: the monk in charge of provisions and the cellar</term> had told him. He went to the granaries where he was to know the truth of whether the wheat was in such small quantity as the cellarer said. While the Abbot and the cellarer returned, the Almoner <term>almosneher: the monk in charge of distributing alms</term> passed by the granary. He saw the place that he loved above all others, where he honored Our Lady Mary alone. In all that granary, there was no more than one measure of wheat, which greatly displeased the Abbot. When he saw there was so little wheat, he felt great pain, for he ought to have distributed the alms. This pain was born of his charity. Seeing this, the granary keeper asked the Abbot why he wept. The Abbot replied: "The death of the poor who live by me is approaching, if this almsgiving, which is customary in the monastery, must be hidden because of the failure of the wheat." The granary keeper, trusting in Our Lady, then said to the Abbot: "I have enough wheat for this whole year." Do not doubt this, for our Lady, who is full of grace, has given us security. The Abbot responded: "So much is true and sufficient as our trust; we would be guilty if we refused her and stopped our almsgiving." The Abbot returned to the monastery very joyfully and ordered all alms to be given as was customary. For as long as people came for alms, all the wheat in the granaries and all the stores remained, and they received wheat from the land in that granary where the granary keeper had placed his trust in Our Lady. Then a message came to the granary keeper that the wheat was as sufficient as he had promised. The granary keeper opened the storage...

<column-break/>

to the monastery: coming from the wheat that was in the enclosed land. When the granary keeper opened the store, he found the land placed as if he had praised it: all the wheat was there. With the joy of the closed storehouse and the harvest they found, it was all apparent. The granary keeper said the "Hail Mary" <note>original: "aue mant," likely Ave Maria</note> as he was accustomed to do. While the grain was being lifted by the men, he who had praised the storehouse saw it new with wheat. He did not keep it full for himself, but because he had placed his trust in the Abbot. While the granary keeper thought on this, he heard that Our Lady was "Full of Grace." For he saw that if Our Lady were full of grace, then the granary must be full of wheat. At this, the Abbot sent for the granary keeper to bring him some wheat, for a traveler from Saxony had arrived. The granary keeper trusted in Our Lady, and opening the storehouse again, he found it full of wheat. The granary keeper remembered how Our Lady was "Full of Grace." In all that he found, the granary keeper saw the storehouse full of wheat every time he opened it. And that storehouse sufficed for the convent and for all the alms until the new wheat arrived. The Abbot and the monks praised and honored Our Lady, who had willed to remember their necessity. One evening, the granary keeper was at the monastery for the office of Sext <note>The monastic prayer service for the sixth hour (noon)</note>. The Abbot asked him how it happened that everything had been so sufficient. The granary keeper told the Abbot that among the other words in the Hail Mary, there is "Full of Grace"...

Blanquerna seeks to recapture his lost fervor, distracted by the administrative duties of the abbey.</meta>

### The Abbot’s Devotion to the Fullness of Grace

The Abbot <note>The protagonist, Blanquerna</note> was so moved because he turned with such joy... he marveled that he could be so devout. It was enough to recognize that through the fulfillment of Our Lady <note>The Virgin Mary</note> being full, the justice of redemption was joined. The Son <note>Jesus Christ</note> was there where the Abbot said: "I salute you, Holy Spirit, for the grace <note>original: "gia," an abbreviation for "gratia"</note> full of Our Lady." 

The brother replied to the Abbot: "Indeed, if they knew any causes for which they could die honoring Our Lady. If you wish to show me how full Our Lady is of great and revealing grace, by this I shall set myself to fulfill my understanding." With this, the joy grew with more love and knowledge; and through that encompassing knowledge, his will was pleased to love and deliver that fullness of grace.

When the Abbot saw the replenishment of grace in Our Lady—which was in the Son—he remembered that he entered into a state of redemption as if in a fire, and he spoke these words. The first part that the Abbot felt was a longing for such a fulfillment of devotion as this Son. 

Abbot Blanquerna knelt <note>original: "geneller"</note> to the brother of "Full of Grace," praying that he would teach him how he might return to the devotion in which he used to dwell, which he had lost through the business of the abbey <note>original: "negosis dela abadia"</note>. The brother wept and Blanquerna wept; and the one looked at the other with a loving countenance. Neither knew how to speak to the other, for love overwhelmed them; but each followed the other in his heart. 

The image of Our Lady, and the representation of her Son, and her will: these were held without fear. The Son went into the garden <note>likely referring to the Garden of Gethsemane or the imagery of the Rose</note> in which he hung, so that no one might be raised up who did not serve Him. Now they wept and returned to their prayers before they could speak. So much did the Abbot remain on his knees.

<column-break/>

### The Significance of Divine Grace

He did not doubt these words: Our Lady was full of grace when the Son died. That grace signified the grace with which the saints are full of God in this world; even so, one finds and demonstrates how the Son of Our Lady is distant <note>The text here contrasts the earthly presence with divine transcendence</note> in that "Full of Grace" of Our Lady. Light was desired in the world in great fullness. 

Abbot Blanquerna wept for the faults of the world. Blanquerna wept with these words: "O Lady, to know and love you! I give myself so much to believe that at the hour she was full of grace, I should remember it most fixedly; in which, in this prayer, she was able to imagine her Son." He begged to be filled with so much grace that it would make us love and please the Son; "Oh Savior: do not hide your grace from me, for all your lovers receive action from us." 

In that state, as if in a signified recognition, he began to sing. And he felt the guilt pass away so that he could sing once more. "Brother," said the Abbot, "help me to pray and weep." They were there a long time until the Queen <note>The Virgin Mary</note>, whom he loved so much, was loved with such certain grace in this world that the Son was placed in order. The Abbot and the brother wept together, and after their weeping, they departed from one another. 

Abbot Blanquerna went into his chamber so that the devotion in which he used to dwell might be maintained, and for this reason, he sought to retain the pleasure of contemplating Our Lady in her city of grace <note>original: "cib[tat] de gia plena"</note>.

It was seen that Abbot Blanquerna held for himself a granary keeper <term>granier: the monk in charge of provisions</term> who was upright and distinguished on the bank of the great river. That hill was of such a nature that one could see the brother who wanted to gaze upon the land. The other man approached... that man who in grace recognized a monk... through these words: "For a long time I have known this hill... and for the love of the one who through this world became a pilgrim and a family... telling you that you could not climb it." It was agreed that he would guard the hill.

...to change the song into a contest... The **Tree** responded regarding the salutation of God: "The thirst of God," and how the Angel **Gabriel** saluted a lady, **Holy Mary**. And among the other words, he said: "The Lord is with you" <note>original: "d’niſ t’eala," likely a corruption of the Latin "Dominus tecum"</note>. Through this salutation, fear was given to me, as if saying: "Behold, the Lord of Heaven and Earth is with you, so that His laws may be in your heart, so that your works may be pleasing to Him."

When the **Tree** had spoken these words, the **Pilgrim** felt himself overcome and conquered by the logic, and said: "Lord Tree, give me the rule of the monk <note>original: "moneiher"</note> through which I may dwell in a hermitage <note>original: "eztuiana"</note> on another peak, away from the world." The Tree granted the Pilgrim what he asked: to live on that high mountain where he wished to stay to contemplate the Lord. "I give you a mountain in your realm... but you shall have no cell or chapel in that hermitage." But the Pilgrim did not want this, for he feared that through the vanity of pride or worldly fame, people would come to him and disturb his holy life or mortify his devotion.

The Pilgrim spoke these things, and the Tree showed him the rule and the manner of how to be a servant of God according to the form of this salutation. "The Lord, the King of Angels and of all that is, chose a woman—the best work that any creature could receive—for God wished to come into a woman’s human nature. No nobler work could God perform." The Pilgrim replied: "Lord, God by nature gives to a woman a place in His nature... more

<column-break/>

strongly to exalt nature in our human form." And for this reason, the Lord was with that Lady when the Angel said: "The Lord is with you" <note>original: "d’niſ t’eai," for "Dominus tecum"</note>, more than with any other. After this, he began to return to his order... through this salutation and many others... the rule of action to enter into the manner of how to contemplate God and Our Lady.

The Pilgrim remained a long time in that place in penance and a harsh life, contemplating God and Our Lady. In an abundance of great devotion, he exalted his understanding and love, and religion... <note>The text here describes a state of mystical transport where the pilgrim's devotion exceeds his outward knowledge</note>. So great was the devotion of the hermit that many came to see his holy life and his church, and the words he spoke were like a mystery.

It happened that while the Abbot <note>Blanquerna</note>, who loved the Truth and hated the Lie, was traveling, he came to that forest where the monk we have mentioned was staying. In his way, he met a **Knight** in full armor <note>original: "cauallier garnit"</note> who loved seeking adventure for the sake of his lady. This knight had removed his helmet and was resting because of the great heat; his horse was grazing on the grass near a spring. The knight was singing a *new song* in that place,

---

criticizing the base ones <note>original: "auoluays," referring to those who do not understand true love</note> who speak of love but do not know it. He praised that lady whom the knight loved. The Tree, hearing the song and the words, began to speak to the knight. And the Tree asked the knight about his thoughts, and said: "Knight, this lady is of a love that makes one love that which is pleasing and agreeable. It seems to me that you are enamored with a lady, according to what your song signifies. This lady you praise above all other ladies: tell me, if there were another lady better, more noble, and more precious than yours, would you love her more than the one you love now?"

The knight praised his lady’s character and worth. "Even if there were a lady more noble and precious than the one I love, it would be a betrayal to change my love, if I did not already love the best among all ladies. He who does not love the best does not understand the art of love." When the knight had said this, the Tree asked him: "Knight, tell me, why do you carry arms?" The knight responded: "So that I may defend my body against those who wish to offend me." The Tree said to the knight: "Do you have any arms with which to defend the lady whom you love?" The knight replied: "Listen,

<column-break/>

it would be a great misfortune if another lady were better and more worthy of praise than mine." The Tree said: "In a more noble manner, my Lady is praised, and she is more precious and her love is more pleasing... a worthy spirit of praise is my Lady compared to yours. And therefore, she is more nobly loved by a knight than your lady is by you."

This did not please the knight, what the Tree was saying. He said that if the Tree were a knight, he would challenge him <note>original: "lo tendzia," meaning he would hold him to account in a duel</note> for the words he had spoken; for by force of arms, he would prove that his lady is better and more precious than any other. "Lord," said the Tree, "know that I am armed spiritually, through which I often defeat the wicked. Would you like to fight with me with such arms to see which is better and more worthy? Do not be afraid, for I feel myself strengthened to prove to you that my Lady is better than yours."

A great contention arose between the Tree and the Knight as to which lady was better loved. They agreed to each praise their lady to see which one would be judged better. The Tree wanted the knight to go first. The knight praised his lady with these words: "My lady is so beautiful and gracious that love has made me conquer and overcome many knights. Many times I have been brought to the point of death for her, and I have suffered much to serve her. I search the whole world for her... how much more should you be her servant!"

<meta>This page continues the allegorical debate from Matfre Ermengaud’s 'Breviari d’Amor' (Breviary of Love). Following the previous page's discussion on the "Fullness of Grace," the text here transitions into a dialogue between a Monk and a Knight regarding the nature of the "Best Lady"—the Virgin Mary—and how courtly devotion to her differs from earthly, sinful love.</meta>

# The Breviary of Love

### The Monk’s Discourse on the Heavenly City

<columns>

"This," says the **monk**, "is what Jerusalem signifies: divine consolation <note>original: 'divina cons', likely 'divina consolatio'</note> which is better and more pious. It holds a sign of great truths for us to sing and affirm with great support. It tells of a **Lady** who is radiated with light, whom you shall see and hear if you have the tongue to recount it." To the **knight** who had praised his own lady, the monk replies that his own Lady is far better. 

"Now you shall hear her praised, for my Lady is so superior that she is better than all those who have ever been. My heart rejoices, for I do not see her with physical light, but through this spiritual light. For without fail, thousands of knights would wish to see her glory if they could. I speak of the praise of the Virgin <note>original: 'panilla', likely 'paraula' (word)</note> that the **Angel Gabriel** spoke to my Lady. Through those words, she became the daughter of God, the chosen one, **Mary**, who is above all other women."

Note that there is no greater joy than the joy found in her, for God the Father took human flesh within her. Because she is full of grace, he chose to dwell in her womb as a man. That **Lady** is the **Mother of God**. It is said that she who is the Mother is better than all other crowned women. And if a man has the grace to love her, he is better than all others. Therefore, this person is more blessed than any other. I tell you, this Lady is the head of all virginal grace.

The knight, having heard this praise of the feminine, then spoke. Now the knight is cited in the praise that he addresses to the monk:

<column-break/>

"My mind is full of grace," said the **knight**, considering the one true **Love** that he has for the **Lady** who is human. "But I am now in doubt, for if that love is a **mortal sin** <term>mortal sin: a grave sin that separates the soul from God’s grace</term>, I am in peril of falling. I cannot see how I can escape the infernal fire, nor how I can play or rejoice with celestial glory. My mind is changed." 

The knight considers this with many sighs, wondering if he must remain in this state of earthly love. "But let us speak of that 'Better Lady.' If I loved her and were loved in return, it would be a great fortune to stay all my days on this earth loving her. She is the goal." 

The monk, who is seated by his Lady, knows her to be better than all other women. "My love will have no end, nor will it bring me shame or vileness. It makes me better to serve this Better Lady. My desire will not fail or change for this love."

The knight spoke these words of the monk: "Lord Monk," said the knight, "if it is true that the Lady you love would not suffer me to love her, then to love her would be my death. All the days of my life I would be in a state of denial <note>original: 'reneguetat'</note>. I ask for the honor of her favor." 

The monk then spoke to the knight: "To love the Lady who is the best and most pleasing, one must be a devoted lover."

<margin>
One after the other, they do not deny the knight’s desire.
</margin>

<column-break/>

He promises his love to her. And if she is worthy of having honor above all other ladies, the knight has a great desire to serve her with words of truth. He will not tremble, nor will he act falsely like a <term>joglar</term> <note>a wandering performer or minstrel</note>, but will speak in truth of the Lady who is above all women. 

"In this way, I wish to honor my Lady. This is the manner in which I will enter the combat for her, as a knight who is not a servant to any other lady, but only to this one." 

When the knight had spoken these words, he proceeded to speak further. Then it became a matter of a new rule that the knight would take up: to hold the will of God. The knight sought to be true and upright. He went to the court of the **King** <note>In this allegory, the King often represents God or a high authority of Love</note>. When he was in the presence of the King, he spoke with the will of the King. 

When the knight was before the King, he spoke these words: "Lord, I am a lover of the Lady who is better than all others, whom all should respect. I seek no other honor. I follow the path of the King to see the honor that all should give to the Lady from whom human flesh was made." 

The knight was newly entered into this service. The King asked the knight if he would perform his duty to God, and stay in the grace of the Mother of Mercy.

<column-break/>

...that she was full of grace, being the Mother of the King of Glory. For this reason, the knight wished to make her praise known. The King asked who would deny this Lady the honor the knight spoke of. The knight responded to the King, saying that the greatest honor any lady could have is in the mind of God, and that he would fight all the world to defend the honor due to his Lady. 

But since he did not know the scriptures <note>original: 'l’escrepturas'</note> well, he did not wish to answer the King's difficult questions. Instead, he gathered all the knights together. The King asked the knight what he would do now. The knight replied that he would lose his life or suffer a bad death before he would let his Lady’s honor be stained. 

The King signaled for the knight to prepare for a contest. Many other knights were there through the grace of the King. When all the knights were in the field, the knight who served his Lady was ready. He looked upon his armor and his horse. 

The knight fought with great courage. The King saw that all the others were wrong. It is a wonder that the knight, through his love, was not defeated. This shows that the knight who has love in his heart cannot be conquered, for love gives him the strength to win the battle.

</columns>

## When the work was brought to the grange where
they were in company, as soon as he moved, the **Abbot** recalled the news of the **knight** who had been captivated <note>original: 'arrobat', meaning snatched or robbed of his senses by love</note> at the fountain, singing of love. And he recalled the words that the knight used to say regarding the **Rule** <note>The monastic Rule of life</note> to which they are bound. He considered all those words which he recognized from the Abbot's account of the knight. And he remembered the words from the **Hail Mary** <note>original: 'aue maria'</note>, specifically: "Blessed are you among women" <note>original: 'benedictu tu i muliere'</note>. And when the bishop <note>original: 'lanesque' / 'lauesque'</note> had considered the language, he said to the Abbot that he had joined the rule.

## Blessed be the divine light
of piety and grace that has moved this sinful man to submit himself entirely to this fruit of the [tree], the fruit that was in the womb of **Our Lady**. He offered that fruit to that monastery with all the joy of his heart and with all the joy of his soul, which was pleasing

and with great devotion near the royal city. A **monk** was heard to be in the company of those honoring **Our Lady**, and he set himself to keep watch. The monk saw the years... <note>The OCR is fragmentary here</note>... and he put his love in this... so that he recovered the joy which he had previously lost.

## And for this reason and confidence, he prayed
to find the way by which he might judge the honor of the fruit that **Our Lady** had in her womb through the grace of the **Holy Spirit**. And in all truth, the bishop feared... <note>Text becomes difficult to decipher</note>... to confess how to joyfully show the fruit and the sign of **Our Lady**. It happened then that in that territory there was a cleric who... <note>fragmentary mention of a place or person called 'malo guaf'</note>. And then in the presence of **Christ**, that island is considered a noble and holy place.

And they do this so that they may praise, through the general [rule], the fruit of **Our Lady**. Solely intending to honor that saint... fruit... that love and this establishment are known throughout Christendom.

## The bishop was very pleased
with that establishment and greatly praised the devotion of the king and of this lord, who wished to be martyrs... and then the bishop said...

with a heart to populate that place... and a very loyal monastery. And then he was given the apostolic [letter] of the chapter of that bishop... so that he could be a wall against captivity. ## But there was great concern from the
bishop who used to be in his company. The monk remembered the **Hail Mary**... praying "Holy Mary, pray for us" <note>original: 'oza pnb'</note>... for all Christians... in that prayer to the honor of **Our Lady**. The monk considered how many priests there are who [neglect] the prayer... in the [glories?]. And the monk proceeded to say to the Abbot: "How does the 'fruit' come to the people who are in the mountains and the forests?" For they do not come to the church. And therefore they fear the rule and justice... that there should be a prayer from the shepherds to whom they say "Holy Mary, pray for us." For in that place he recognized and considered that they are all alone

and there is no one to guide them. He considered how a man swears by the sign of the man in **Our Lady**. Our confidence is in multiplying love anew in loving **Our Lady**. Now the Abbot was pleased by this new devotion, commanding that the monk should not fail in honoring **Our Lady**. He went to the monastery to provide and to stay with the [monks] with a willing heart... so that a monk of that monastery should be the leader of the shepherds. And that prayer had the name "Holy Mary, Pray for Us." All the convent was pleased with that establishment. The monk who used to be... taught the shepherds "Holy Mary, pray for us." And he explained to them through that prayer the love of virtue. The Abbot said that in this knowledge, the desire is a great concord... for the will loves that which the understanding perceives. And therefore it is most perfect... to the shepherds... who are people more suited to understand by reason... and thus they will love the honor of **Our Lady** if they understand the necessary and just words...

<margin>The truth and the doctrine that the monk asks for</margin>

## For the understanding accepts a knot of that
which the [prophet?] carries. The understanding commends it to the **memory**, and understands the law... because a man [hears] the sermon and the word in a subtle way. The **memory** cannot return <note>retrieve/recall</note> all that the understanding commends to it. Therefore there is ignorance and failure of recognition in that firm reason.

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the spiritual merit of the Virgin Mary. <margin>May his words be pleasing</margin>

# On the Grace of the Virgin and the Virtue of Words

A thing that is made beautiful and very good, for many ladies become much more pleasing when they possess good and better manners <note>original: 'māña', referring to conduct or courtly breeding</note>. From them, the will of a person becomes amiable. Through this, one can come to know how to speak the best words, for you know that all things are necessary to bring us to the honored God through words of prayer.

## The Monk’s Devotion to the Glorious Son

He sings and shows the manners of one who admires that immortal... which the monk holds in our name. This is the manner of fishing <note>possibly an allegory for "fishing for souls"</note> or contemplating the worthy one. O monk, you are asked to appease... through these words: "Holy Mary... your glorious Son, to whom you pray for us more than other saints." If you are more willing to pray for us than other saints, it is because we are not worthy on our own to be heard in such songs, if we do not pray to you, if your Son does not save us, and if we do not pray to be obligated to contemplate you in all our honor. And now we give you honors, so that you might remember us. Look upon us with your merciful eyes in this time, and remember our procession and our gathering. For through our failures, we often forget the Passion that touches you, even if we do not see it as it truly was. But you bear it for us, so that we may be saved. If you do not cease to pray for us and all your servants, it will be as it should be.

O virtuous queen, love us with the affection you have for your honored Son, in those places where there is much suffering and where the way is difficult. Through this path, the Son of God, who was once born and was finally crucified for those who are certain of his reign, became our eternal King. You are full of grace <note>original: 'plēna de gracia'</note> and of favor, and it is believed in heaven that you were conceived to pray for us and for other saints.

Singing together, we offer honor and begin to trust more strongly in you. We entrust ourselves to you, for you are the one who pardons our faults. O Queen of Grace, look upon us... pray for us, for we remember your piety and your noble song. If you do not ignore us, pray to your glorious Son; may your glorious Son not cease his prayers. In that love which we used to have... remember us, for we are commanded to the favor of **Saint John**, the noble son and witness to the monk, according to the manner in which you have been accustomed to hear and contemplate. We praise you here on earth and in heaven where we wish to return. If God grants that we return to him, we shall be humble in our prayer to the honored and exalted one, and we shall remember to pray and to love, for faith shall be our guide.

<column-break/>

# The Encounter in the Valley of the Shepherds

To you, we would bring great joy, which the monk cannot love and praise enough. The companions come and stay in that place where the light shines. Now the monk sings... for the people... the teachings of my Lady, who prays for the just and the righteous. And so it was that the monk came into a valley where he saw a soul... and a woman who had performed such a famous deed. 

The monk came into that valley, and it was a place that pleased him. He greeted the woman kindly, and the monk spoke to the woman. She explained that a shepherd <note>original: 'p-at-r' or 'p-it-x', interpreted as 'pastor'</note> had commanded her, and it was a fortunate thing... The monk behaved with justice and relieved her, according to the custom he had with the other shepherds with whom he worked. While eating with the shepherds, the monk remained with the woman and asked her for her story. 

## The Faith of the Shepherd's Wife

The woman told him how she was the wife of a shepherd, and how she lived with that shepherd in poverty. She told him how she did not act falsely, and how she had kept her mantle <note>possibly a symbol of her marriage vow or her dignity</note>. She had no reason to fear because of the great love she had for him. They ate together in that time and place.

The monk greeted her again with kind words... and the woman told him of her adventure: that the shepherd lived with his woman in that place. The woman took... and the shepherd returned. He did not care for... but our Lady made it so that all those who were there... In this place, the name of the woman was known to be just, and she prayed to the shepherds... so that her name would not be forgotten. "Holy Mother," <note>original: 'Scā m-rt-', likely 'Sancta Mater'</note> she said, "I was not worthy that my name should be known in this place." 

The shepherd said to the monk that it would be a noble thing to pray for her. The monk replied and said that the Mass was a fortunate thing for those who pray, and that they were all her children. Those who believe in these deeds... The monk trusted these words, and the woman... The monk was certain that no one would suffer there. For the glorious Son of our Lady... and this woman had not... she had not received... but she had her husband. The monk said to the woman that if she confessed, our Lady would not let her die. The woman replied that she would not lie about what had been done... for she had great faith in her heart.

# Here begins the third book, which is about the prelacy
## In what manner Abbot Blanquerna was made a bishop. Chapter 68.

When the Archbishop of Rheims <note>original: 'remi'</note> reached an advanced age and was in the city with all the canons, they gathered in the chapter house <term>chapter: the governing body of a cathedral or monastery</term> to ordain how they should elect a pastor. And some of the said canons asked the archbishop which of them all he thought should be chosen to be bishop, for the previous bishop had renounced the office and wished to die by the sea <note>likely referring to a desire for a hermit's life of contemplation by the coast</note>. It seemed to him that this should be requested and debated within the chapter.

The canons agreed with what the canon said. He who used to be bishop went to the chapter and, in the presence of everyone, said they should elect Abbot Blanquerna as bishop, for he knew no man so worthy as him for such an office. And if they demurred regarding the bishop <unclear>d'm</unclear> they should elect a bishop according to the custom of the place and the canon law. He did not wish to displease <unclear>d'm</unclear> because he knew the one who used to be bishop. And he turned to the abbot of the canons, for they were all regular canons <term>regular canons: priests living in a community under a religious rule, such as that of St. Augustine</term>. And when <unclear>d'm</unclear> his status was considered, Blanquerna was [to be] bishop so that he would not have to leave his life as a regular canon. The abbot and the regular canons agreed that <unclear>d'm</unclear> Blanquerna should be bishop. They did not want him to be just any canon, for the way of his art <note>referring to Llull's "Art," a philosophical system of finding truth</note> was the art <unclear>d'm</unclear> that existed before... with the canons who wished to live according to the rule. And almost all the canons wanted Blanquerna to be bishop; they elected Abbot Blanquerna as bishop. However, one canon opposed this and elected

<column-break/>

another as bishop, so much so that a great discord arose among the canons for a day, and they were deeply divided. The canons came to Abbot Blanquerna and told him that he had been elected bishop, and that they had never seen a man like him. And they prayed for him everywhere, swearing by the Holy Spirit <note>original: 'ſan ſps'</note> that he should accept the bishopric and go to Rome to be confirmed [by the Pope]. 

Abbot Blanquerna, hearing this and seeing that he would need help and <unclear>d'm</unclear> to seek relics for this bishopric, said that in no way would he be bishop; he was displeased with the canons because the Abbot did not want to be bishop. This was also said by the others of the city's clergy. The other candidate went to Rome to prepare himself to be bishop and to be confirmed. But already the majority of the canons were against that other canon, and they prayed that the Lord Apostle <note>original: 'ſenhor apoſtoli', a medieval title for the Pope</note> would command Abbot Blanquerna to be bishop. 

The Lord Apostle spoke these words regarding the appearance of simony <term>simony: the sin of buying or selling sacred things or church offices</term> that exists when one office is traded for another <unclear>d'm</unclear>. And it seemed to him that Abbot Blanquerna, who did not wish to be bishop and withdrew himself, not wanting to [rule] the great city, was the one truly worthy of being bishop. Therefore, the Apostle, seeing that they truly wanted Blanquerna to be bishop, issued the mandate that he should be bishop, whether he willed it or not, and this pleased Blanquerna. And everyone praised the command of the Apostle. A monk went to the Apostle to accept this, saying that he who used to be bishop had come to the Abbot to choose him <unclear>d'm</unclear> for the city, and it seemed he [the previous bishop] renounced his...

## The Canon of the Affliction of the Soul

...troubles, anxieties, and afflictions. He desired to be the first to proclaim to all: that which is sustained should be for His love. And it happened one day that a muleteer <note>original: "menatò:" or "menador," a driver of pack animals</note> led a mule... and he noticed that the mule was very thin and weak. The canon said to the muleteer: "Why do you beat this mule so harshly?" The muleteer replied that the mule did not want to carry its load nor do its work unless it was given bread and straw. When the muleteer had spoken of this affliction, the canon said in the presence of many: "Look at these <unclear>hardships</unclear>; to carry a load without love or greatness is not a worthy thing... as is first said in the Gospel: his fidelity... and giving to the poor person is not recognized with justice." And it happened one day in the square... the Canon of Affliction. And the archdeacon, walking through the square, came to him. He was healthy and well-fed. He saw the canon who looked as though he were a <unclear>corpse</unclear> or a dying man because he would not eat more than was necessary. And when the archdeacon, who was a great man and a great eater by nature, saw this, the canon asked the archdeacon which was more profitable to the soul: the affliction of the body or the abundance of grace? Which God gives first to all those who, for love, sustain... he did not have... and the archdeacon...

<column-break/>

...without giving any response. The lord of the city went out and traveled toward another city. He walked five miles. He encountered two religious men <note>monks or friars</note> who were arguing; for one did not want to go by one path, and the other pleaded for a different path. The one who did not want to go by the first path was afraid because he had to pass through a narrow and dangerous passage. He was so afraid that he wanted to turn back or find another way. When the Canon of Affliction understood what they were arguing about, he rebuked very strongly the one who was afraid. "This is done to prove the word of God. For He says in our doctrine: if I am dead so that I may be saved... for this reason we should not fear." This was said to prove the justice of God. When the canon had finished, he entered the city where he was going. He met a man who gave a penny <note>original: "dinar"</note> to a poor man so that people would say he was a good man; and he said that he had done enough during the famine. The canon and that man disputed for a long time whether that gift was truly for God or not. The man argued that out of love he had given from his own wealth. The canon said that alms were not truly alms if they were not done according to <unclear>the spirit</unclear>. For if a man loves to sin or follows his gluttony, it is fitting that his hunger or his woman be punished. The canon sought to convince him of this. When the canon was in the city, he found a great number of poor people who went through the city...

<column-break/>

...seeking certain alms for the love of God. All the poor gathered around the canon. The canon went along with the poor, suffering hunger with them. One day it happened that a merchant sent for the canon and the poor to come and eat until they were satisfied. When the canon and the poor came to eat, they felt a stronger hunger than before they had arrived. Many were amazed at this, seeing that they had nothing to eat. He ordered that they be given more to eat. After this, the canon said: "We have eaten well, by the mercy of God and yours; for the hunger we endure is for the love of God, so that from this salvific blessing we may be satisfied." And so the canon took the opportunity to be hungry to punish himself for his sins and to give an example to the people of how they should hold to afflictions, and how they should remember the hunger that Jesus Christ had in the desert, and the thirst He sustained on the cross. And the canon did not love only physical hunger for the sake of love, but he also loved spiritual hunger, so that others would have a hunger for virtue, for charity, and for other goodness, so that they might be satisfied in the next life. Of Mercy. [Chapter] 68.

## The Canon of Mercy

He preached mercy <note>original: "misericordia"</note> every day. And it happened one day that a man, according to justice, was to receive death. This man had a wife and five small children. He was a man who worked at his trade. While the man was being led...

<summary>This page describes the roles of the Canon of Affliction and the Canon of Mercy, using allegorical stories about a thin mule, a fearful traveler, and a famine to illustrate the spiritual value of physical suffering and true charity. The text emphasizes that bodily hardship should be a reflection of Christ's suffering and a means to attain spiritual virtue.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the "Canon of Mercy," a character from Ramon Llull’s *Blanquerna* who embodies the virtue of compassion in the public sphere, often interacting with the legal and royal systems to advocate for the poor.</meta>

<columns>2</columns>
<header>Prose</header>

<margin>
The purpose of speaking
</margin>

...the knowledge given to the monk <note>This refers to the "Canon of Mercy" introduced in this section of the text.</note> concerning a man’s sentence, and he follows what must follow because of it. Thus, as the King pronounced his sentence, speaking of the penalty for the offense, this man of whom we speak—the Canon—went to the King and spoke these words: "Mercy, says the Lord Jesus." <note>original: "mifericordia diu en ihas fenfeyre"</note> This was said to the King to serve your <insert>purpose</insert> and to show you the time is right to be merciful, so that you do not follow a path where the piety that God gave you in the Gospel is lost. For it says: "Blessed are those who have mercy, for they shall obtain mercy." <note>A translation of the Latin Beatitude: *Beati misericordes, quoniam ipsi misericordiam consequentur.*</note> And if you do not grant it, you shall see that mercy will not be given to you. And as I am a sinner in need of mercy, I stand in your presence to pray that you show mercy regarding this sentence which holds you in its power. The King placed his trust in the words the Canon spoke to him regarding eternal mercy <note>original: "mifericordia aeterna"</note>, so that he would not act against his faith. And at these words of the Canon, he spoke these words: "Lord, have mercy on me." He was moved so that he would not act wrongly, nor be burdened by a lack of mercy in his faith. He spoke in deep thought, and you must choose the better path. The Canon responded and said that as a man begins to work more as a servant of God, he finds the better and more noble way, and becomes accustomed to it. This is said so that you may see how often the souls of sinners are denied mercy. This prince was prepared and represented on earth, saying it was his usual practice. For this reason, I have spoken of the cause of earthly judgment and the souls denied mercy because of the way they were judged. For so long a time

<column-break/>

did the Canon speak to the King, and with such great pleading for mercy, that the King overturned the judgment of the one who had been sentenced to death. The Canon said it was necessary that no light of truth be hidden, so that it might shine through him as he performed his duties. And so the Canon acted according to his office, coming many times to the square where the court <note>original: "cort"</note> was held to offer reasons and help to those who could not afford a lawyer. He stood with the poor and helped in cases where they had no one to speak for them. The Canon rebuked those who falsely conducted lawsuits <note>original: "plats," meaning legal pleas or trials</note>, including the lawyers, officials, and clerks. He saw many failings and many deceptions in the lawsuits they managed. The King recognized this through the case of a poor man <note>original: "paget," likely a peasant or smallholder</note> from whom a field had been taken. This peasant had been to the court many times, and he was seen going there often. He tried to go to the King’s palace <note>original: "pilat," variant of "palau"</note> to show the injury done to him, but he could not get close enough to speak. He waited until he could speak with the King, for he was starving and in God's hands. He returned again and again, begging for help. He took his plea for mercy to the Canon and told him of the necessity of his case. But as he could not speak with the King, the Canon, who was in the square, reasoned with the people and went up to those who were in the square listening. In that square, no one spoke without mercy. And he saw the man who

<column-break/>

<page-num>45</page-num>

brought the women to say that they would stand in prayer with one voice. The monk told them and prayed that they would join him in mercy. They went to the gate of the King’s palace and entered, crying out together, "Mercy! Mercy!" The King was marveled by this and asked why they were crying out. The porter at the gate asked the Canon why he was shouting. "Lord," said the Canon, "Our Lord Jesus Christ <note>original: "ihu x," abbreviation for Ihesu Christ</note> followed love and mercy for the poor. And it is certain that you are given the duty to act with mercy. It may seem to you that men only require justice, but you should be ashamed." The King, moved by the words of this Canon, responded to him. And it was established that any man could enter the palace gate if he was recommended by the Canon. Thus the court was improved, and cases were represented to the King so he could see them. The other officials were punished because they had not been truthful. Then a peasant <note>original: "paget"</note> came before the Canon and told a lie about a debt, swearing by a bull <note>original: "buon," referring to an ox or beast of burden</note> that he did not owe anything to a certain man, though he did. He came to the market to trade with another. And in the middle of the market, the owner of the bull caught the peasant in his lie. The peasant cried to the Canon for mercy, asking that he be given time so that he might pay. The honest man who owned the bull complained that he should not suffer for the other's lie, as his wife and children depended on it.

<column-break/>

The Canon pleaded for mercy, asking for a pardon from the magistrate <note>original: "veguer"</note> and the creditor <note>original: "crapitier"</note>, but it was of no use. The peasant was to be taken away. The Canon of Mercy, seeing no other way to help the peasant, took the income <note>original: "renda"</note> from his own church office <note>original: "canongia"</note>. He went to the one who was taking the peasant away and the honest man who had been wronged. He took the bull and the peasant through the middle of the market and along the road. On that path, he met someone who wanted to give himself as security for that bull. If they did not find another way, he would go to prison <note>original: "piger"</note> himself as security for the bull. While the Canon and the prisoner <note>original: "piger"</note> were leading the bull along the road, they met the King, who was coming from Mass. The Canon asked if the other man wanted to go to prison for the bull. The King was greatly marveled by these words that the Canon spoke. He asked the Canon to explain what those words signified. The Canon said: "God gives himself as security for this man. In the eyes of God, mercy is worth more than the thing itself. Therefore, the mercy of God is given to the one who gives of himself. I would rather go to prison myself if it would save the bull and give it back to its owner." This greatly pleased the King and all the others. The words the Canon spoke concerning God's mercy revealed the deception and falsehood of those who lack mercy. And the King ordered that they

...so that he might resemble the demons who were of him, and they would have to advance on his behalf. **T**his Blanquerna <note>The protagonist of Ramon Llull’s novel, now serving as a Bishop.</note> wanted to have the son by his side, and it happened that he went to serve as a prelate <note>term: "prelate" (high-ranking church official)</note>, the son of Evast, by way of the Bishop who desired this appearance; and the King of the land did not wish to forbid him, for he intended to establish and put peace in the end to that which was in war and in trouble. And it happened on a day that the Bishop went to the vespers <note>original: "uesyas" — evening prayer service</note>, at which all the people gathered at the cathedral, in which he heard much from all the poor who were there at the first hour. And it happened that he named the vespers. A great number of youths came to him, performing acts of labor; those who saw the vespers of pity <note>likely referring to the "Vespers of Mercy"</note> were stirred in their souls. At that point, the Bishop was blinded by all the harm that the wicked ones had done to the orphans. He desired that the orphans be protected and loved, and he turned his back on the evil will and the urban vanity. The Bishop held many disciples to have them dispute with all the uninitiated at the synagogue <note>original: "synagoga"</note>, taking them by their deeds toward the end of faith. He heard them and saw that God was with those who sought to help the orphans. At once, many Jews <note>original: "unicus" — likely a corruption of "jueus"</note> were baptized into the holy Roman faith. And the Bishop gave a part of the land's revenue, which belonged to God, to make for them much alms. There were some youths who were like these orphans; they had no protector. In a way that they might play without their misfortune, in those corners lived two knights who loved one another. But a change came upon them; they began to hate each other and fell into Discord. And they were riding upon a mountain, and it seemed

<column-break/>

that war was beginning among the knights, for they wished each other ill and felt great fear. No man could be found who could bring peace between them. The Bishop Blanquerna commanded them to come to his land. He spoke to one of the knights and gave him a horse, and granted him his portion of the Bishop’s revenue, which he had won through a morning service <note>original: "matetrino" — likely referring to revenues associated with the Matins service</note>. On the other day, the Bishop called the other knight and gave him a horse, and for his part of the Bishop’s revenue, he also gave him a morning service. And neither of them was worth a single morning service. And the Bishop told them time and again that since the knights had heard this, he gave everything to the Bishop’s revenue; for in fact, he held them before the Bishop was anointed in the power of the Bishopric. And he went to the Bishopric for the two of them to swear that they would cease their division and come together in all parts. When all the knights and the Bishop had agreed throughout the whole area, the Bishop granted the truth to one knight, and the other part he gave to the other knight. And he made concord and peace between them in that day. From then on, they were to be in peace. One of them was a man of great age, and he had an orphan whom he had taken in as his own. This man was deceiving his wife with another woman; he left his wife for a vile woman whom he loved very much. The Bishop, whom no material thing could corrupt, with the power he had, sought to heal the evil woman who held him. At once, the Bishop sent a messenger <note>original: "m'erfatge"</note> to the woman and prayed her to part from him, and promised to give her a good life if she followed his command. The woman responded that she was pregnant and did not know if she would see the light of day or not, and thus she had no rest.

In truth, by the message that the Bishop sent her, he told her that he held her in such regard that he would provide for her if she did not remain with the man. And the Bishop went to the morning service <note>original: "matetrino"</note>, a protector to the woman, and gave her a place. He saw through the Spirit that the Bishop already had a plan, and because the woman did not want to return to sin, he gave her a dowry. And he departed, and thus she remained in the house of the Church. He loved the will that desired to do the work of God. In that city, there was a merchant who had entered and had much money, seeking to live in peace. That merchant once fell into a great illness, and death approached him. He returned a great truth: that he wished to divide his goods, and he did not know how to do it. For fear that he would be cheated, he prayed to Bishop Blanquerna that he, who had the light, should see to it that his goods were distributed. The Bishop feared that if he asked for his share, it would be seen as fraud. There was a scholar <note>term: "scholar" (a student or cleric in training)</note> who was the Bishop's relative, and he was sent to help. It was seen that the Bishop's way was to command the holy way so that he would be brought to the side of God. And the man was in his bed, and in that prayer he saw the scholar who brought him the news. He felt comforted by the scholar, for he was his friend. When the Bishop had gathered all the people, he commanded the truth of love and fear, and the Bishop went to his friend... <note>The text here begins to condense into symbolic or fragmented abbreviations</note> ...because they had much. And the man who made it to the Bishop in peace gave everything, and the scholar was moved to devotion.

counsel that he feared as a great rewarder: he was a great saint, and he remained at the table <note>original: "al me" — likely 'al mes' or 'al mesa'</note> if in truth it was that he doubted he was a saint, for he did not regard him as he should; and he was frightened. They brought the said fool, and truly with empty hands they led him before the King; and they brought to him the words that he had said. <column-break/>

They told him that their deeds would show him what they must be; and the Canon told them that an apostle must be persecuted for justice; and he left them in that place. And the Canon went throughout all the lands; and he believed all this that they did with their teeth. And he was persecuted and tormented many times by those who resisted him; and by the false liars, he did much good in all that land. And the Canon went and departed. For in the multiplication of the believers, and from the preaching of the Canon, God our noble Savior multiplied grace through the merits of the Canon; and when...

**A**ccording to what was had yesterday, he was accustomed to the path he saw: what the command of the brothel <note>original: "putanē" — referring to the 'prostitution' or the house of ill-repute, a frequent site of moral reform in the text.</note> was and how, so that the man's tongue might be seen there if there were a thousand causes that made him improve; and the merit in the continuation when he was sensed according to the command that he could achieve. And it was seen that while he was in guilt throughout such a manner of the brothel... and the man saw and passed by that way; he said that in the morning he had to go quickly; and now he went with less of it; and they wished that he should be seen from every side.

...the <term>Bishop</term> <note>original: "lauesque"</note> alone, saying that men have the custom of in no way assisting at the Mass, neither the <term>Pope</term> <note>original: "papa"</note> nor doing any other things. And all the ceremonies being alone, ten questions were put to him. These are they: <term>Whether</term> a Christian is at fault for the ignorance of the faithful person who dies in ignorance of the Catholic faith. <term>Whether</term> it is... to judge or blame through <unclear>fear</unclear>, or that Catholics, in swearing an oath of fellowship, have a manner with the infidels—infidels who should not be avoided—and put the Catholics into error. <term>Whether</term> a Christian is at fault because the <term>Saracens</term> <note>original: "sarraies" — a medieval term for Muslims</note> do not hold the right faith... in Jesus Christ... <term>Whether</term> the successor of the Christians is to be understood as a young Christian. <term>Whether</term> faith is of value if the articles [of faith] cannot be understood. <term>Whether</term>—and what is the principal reason—that man is a believer. <term>Whether</term> one ought to do justice over the bishops and archbishops for the sake of <term>Christ</term> <note>original: "xpst"</note>... <term>Whether</term> it is greater to provide or to give justice in the land of the Church. Why do we complain if justice is held? It shall be clearly seen. <term>Whether</term> the Holy Church should first seek to sanctify the world and the Christian princes. <term>Whether</term>

what is the most noble work that the first can do for the poor people. <term>Before</term> the Bishop marveled at the five questions said above. The Bishop did not wish to carry out the five questions, but placed them in the court of Rome; two questions were placed before the Pope and the Cardinals, so that he might solve them and that they might do what was fitting with the solution. The Bishop, in the manner of his protection, attempted to touch everything... for the jester wished to fail him in that which he reproached him... that he was a fool... He had great pleasure, one and the other. And the <term>jester</term> <note>original: "toglar/joglar" — a minstrel or performer who often provided social commentary or "holy folly."</note> asked the Bishop that another in that court maintain valor; while he was there, the Bishop promised him... the jester came into the consistory where the Cardinals were, one with the other, for the purpose of... and the jester began to say... and the... those who are in this court where the world is ruled, that there is no other... these have more of songs <note>original: "canons"</note> than of the souls of all men. And he passes the fault and blame to those who rule—for which they shall have more [blame] in the other world by reason of the ruling they do in this world below—of those who have such long... in this world. <term>Greatly</term> did it affect the Cardinals, for they were very strongly moved by the words the jester would say. <term>And</term> the Cardinal recognized to his companions that it was a jester who had spoken words of blame to him. For this reason, many said the Cardinal had... and for the other things that they did, the Cardinals were unwilling that the Bishop blame them... and they wished that he be... <term>And</term> they said, "We are creators... and he gave us so much courage." And they wished to blame... in the... for clearly they do not wish blame. And they said these [words]

<column-break/>

around the world, there be great discord and accord... through the <term>Apostolic One</term> <note>The Pope</note>. And if it be that... a great fault if not... to err in the world, following the path of the Father which God has given. To... and to... the world. and he be known as unworthy... because... he exalts himself by his own will... the noble... the apostolic name... and he wants... the apostolic power. <summary>The Bishop presents ten theological and ethical questions to the Papal court in Rome, covering topics from the salvation of the ignorant to the justice of the Church. <meta>This page continues the narrative of the prelate’s visit to the Papal court. Following the legal and theological questions posed on the previous page, we now encounter a dramatic, allegorical performance by Ramon the Fool and the Minstrel of Valor, intended to critique the corruption and lack of devotion within the high Church hierarchy.</meta>

...a greater company. One day, the <term>Pope</term> <note>original: "l’apostoli" — a common medieval term for the Pope, referring to his role as the successor to the Apostles</note> summoned all the cardinals and held a great assembly on that day. They were eating a great meal together. And a man dressed as a <term>fool</term> <note>original: "foll" — in medieval literature, the "holy fool" was often used as a character who could speak truth to power</note> came before them... suddenly, in the space of another moment, all was silent. He led a dog and went to salute the Lord [the Pope] before his closed circle of cardinals and the entire court. The Lord [the Pope] rebuked him; however, he stood upright and spoke these words: "I am Ramon the Fool, who comes by the command of the Emperor to enter this place of your ministry and to observe your ways." When he had said this, he presented a <term>sparrowhawk</term> <note>original: "peratner" — a bird of prey used in falconry, here serving as an allegory</note> and then he began to pluck it, feather by feather, several times. Afterward, he did other things: he bound it with a cord and beat it; at another time, he plucked its wings. He treated the hawk so that its skin was torn. The dog began to bark and fled, and the hawk was left in a savage state before the Pope. When Ramon the Fool had finished plucking and mistreating the bird, he did so several times. And every time he was called to account, he remained firm in his purpose. Ramon the Fool said to the Pope and all his testament: "What do you say of this? For I have come to serve you all, and I ask what is signified by what I do to the sparrowhawk and the dog here before us?" The Lord [the Pope] said: "Ramon the Fool, it was your heart's intent to show us... for we have seen your performance... tell us now about the <term>Passion of God</term> <note>original: "passió de dieu"</note>, Jesus Christ, and of the nobility of God, who is able to grant honor to the small."

<column-break/>

"I do not wish to have a manner of loving by force, but by the force of great love, because Jesus's heart loves us; honor him for the <term>Passion</term> <note>The suffering and death of Jesus</note> and the death he loved, and for the <term>Incarnation</term> <note>The belief that God became human in Jesus</note> which he took to his heart. I have come to your court to show through my performance what occurs in my vision. The **sparrowhawk** signifies those men who do not love, but seek only to be sustained and maintained by the order of our court, without submitting or serving. And so men take them in and give to them, yet they receive only grief, trouble, and passion, and they are like wild things. The **dog** signifies the men who are like barking dogs; they have no love for the order of the court because God is not in them. When one does not satisfy their greed, they bark and bring trouble. They are the men who go to court for worldly things; they are unpleasant and disagreeable." When Ramon the Fool had finished his explanation and had answered the Pope, the **Minstrel of Valor** <note>original: "lo joglar de valor"</note> sang a song with much reasoned argument concerning the honor of value. He spoke these words to the Lord [the Pope]: "The heart of a lord should have love and valor in it, and he should give that valor to those in his heart. And if there is a lord who loves truly, he should see that value is honored even in his enemy, for he loves the honor of value itself." The Minstrel of Valor said to the Pope: "What does this signify? It signifies that I ask that you, Lord, appoint two cardinals: one who seeks out value..."

<summary>Ramon the Fool performs an allegory using a dog and a sparrowhawk to criticize the selfish and greedy members of the Papal court. He is followed by the Minstrel of Valor, who counsels the Pope on the true nature of love and the importance of recognizing worth in others.</summary>
<keywords>Ramon Llull, Blanquerna, Papal court, allegory, sparrowhawk, Minstrel of Valor, Church corruption, Passion of Christ</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the scene where Ramon the Fool allegorically mistreated a sparrowhawk to demonstrate the neglect of the Church. The Pope ("the Apostoli") now responds by convening the cardinals to establish new, rigorous ordinances for the reform of the papal court, structured around the verses of the "Gloria in Excelsis" prayer.</meta>

...with a first <note>The text begins mid-sentence, likely describing the virtues or ranks of the attendants.</note> joy, and the other who remains with truth, humility, and the **fortitude of Christ** <note>original: "ffortitudo Christ"</note>. The regular cleric had spoken the small <term>chapters</term> <note>original: "capitoli" — here referring to the specific rules or sections of ecclesiastical law</note>; it was said to the canon of Plora that it was not done in a way that many should withdraw from the gifts which are in this court. By this order, the honor that befits my lord ceases. It pleased the <term>Pope</term> <note>original: "la postoli" — the "Apostolic One," a standard medieval title for the Pope</note> that there be honor: that the value in the court not be diminished by the cardinals. He commanded them to be worthy: that all the cardinals should be against [vices]. And to maintain the letter they had made, to which they promised they would not fail him, and they wished to help maintain the power they had among the cardinals. And there, the Chamberlain and the cardinals told the Pope that they were prepared to confirm and order all things so that justice might be restored in the new law. In prayer, they remembered the great charge they had to honor the passion of the Son of God. The Pope spoke these words: fifteen were the cardinals who are appointed as companions so they can preserve and help this oath in the name of Jesus Christ. The division of the **Glory in the Highest** <note>original: "gloria i excelsis"</note> was made in fifteen parts. And the first part was given to the first in dignity. All was done so that a part was given to each cardinal according to who was first in the dignity of the office, and according to the parts that followed one another. And each part was an office by which each house was ruled in honor to

<column-break/>

maintain the court, so that in the court of Christ he may be honored throughout the whole world. Since the cardinals rule by what the Pope said, and the Pope rules for the **Glory of God in the Highest** <note>original: "gloria i excelsis d’s."</note>, let there be more [devotion] in the cardinalate in the present court. And in the spirit, they took the oath, the cardinals, according to what was prayed. To each part they assigned a [duty], and each cardinal in that part [performed] it by the prayer of the *Glory in the Highest*. In the presence of the Pope, after the examination before our lord the Pope, the cardinals held that they should have an equal rule, so that from what descends from one to the other, it is ensured that no person is [neglected], according to what reason shows and the rule regulates. And for their ministry, so that they might have grace to save the soul, he gave a certain ordinance. He commanded that they perform it perfectly so that it does not burden them, for no office was [greater], and that by prayer the cardinalate is a holy thing. And he ordered that the Pope and the cardinals should not waste the money of the Church. This <term>establishment</term> <note>original: "establiment" — a formal decree or legislative act</note> was confirmed by the Pope among them all. And a promise and sacrament were made, and they were made holy officials. He responded that if any cardinal should go against the decree said above, their responses were assigned to other pages in the <term>rubric</term> <note>original: "rrubci" — the section of a manuscript written in red ink containing instructions or headings</note>. And if they were not in this [order] for a long time, they should not have any <term>prebend</term> <note>original: "prelcnda" — a portion of the revenues of a cathedral or collegiate church granted to a canon</note>. He wished to order the decree mentioned above, for it is very [necessary].

---

They did this to preserve it, so that this evil does not remain through discord, nor that it [spreads] against other nations when they are in court. Let them not live in poverty nor with a bad example, nor speak ill of the Pope nor of his companions. Instead, let them have greater devotion to love and serve God through the holy life and conduct of the Pope and his companions. Because the ordinances and decrees mentioned above are to ensure that from the verses of the *Gloria* and from the supreme [authority] that the archbishops and other prelates have to defend the rights of the Holy Church, the offices of the cardinals are sustained by the *Glory in the Highest*. Now provisions are created for all the offices held by the bishops, archbishops, and prelates; let this suffice for the cardinal to rule in the office, to be held by the Pope and the cardinals. Every week, the Pope shall hold a <term>chapter</term> <note>original: "capitol" — a formal meeting of the clergy for business and correction</note> with the cardinals alone, so that each might correct the other if they are religious; and each must confess his failings in the sight of the others. This decree was put in order so that each cardinal holds a chapter one day a week with the officials. And in this, it was ordered that the cardinal hold a chapter with the scribes one day a week, and arrange other matters for the others mentioned before, as was seen fit. Another decree the Pope and the cardinals ordered, which was very necessary, was this: that throughout the world

<column-break/>

there are sworn messengers who should send letters or messages regarding the state of the land, so that if any strange occurrence or great change of state should happen, or if there were a need for [intervention], they should immediately treat that which was for the remedy of it. In this, the Pope **Bladina** <note>original: "bladina" — likely an allegorical name for a reformed or "pure" Pope (from *blanc*, white)</note> ordered his court to take effect. And of the tenth ordinance, how he was in the court, I do not wish to recount it at length, nor the things he had said of the *Glory in the Highest*. 

In the council, the brief was read by Pope Bladina with his cardinals, so that more [good] works were done in the Church known to God in the heavens. And for this, the Pope said to the cardinals who followed him that if they had this desire of [true faith], they should act in such a manner that they could return the people to the true intention of what the offices and sciences are, to give grace to God. For the world has come into such failure that hardly one man in a thousand has the right intention for the cause for which he was created, nor for the office in which he serves. While the Pope was leading the cardinals, in the presence of the king and the cardinals, a messenger arrived with a letter from the [Sultan] of Babylon <note>original: "iuhiloma" — likely a corruption or variant of Babylon, often used in medieval texts to refer to the Sultan of Egypt or the Saracen east</note>. In that letter many words were written, regarding all the kings and Christian princes and how they [controlled] the land and sea, and the maintenance of their prophet **Muhammad** <note>original: "mafomer"</note>. These [eastern powers] had been [strong], but now they did not wish to have...

<summary>Pope Bladina and the cardinals establish a new ecclesiastical structure where the fifteen cardinals are assigned duties corresponding to the fifteen parts of the "Gloria in Excelsis." The text also details the requirement for weekly disciplinary meetings (chapters) and the creation of a global network of messengers to report on world events, ending with the arrival of a letter from the Sultan of Babylon.</summary>
