<meta>This page continues the narrative of Church reform from page 51. The Pope (referred to as the "Apostolic One") and his cardinals are organizing the administration of the Church and planning for the conversion of non-Christians through the study of languages, a central theme in Ramon Llull's work.</meta>

...love of **Jesus Christ** <note>original: "ihu xpt"</note>, the Pope <note>original: "cels apostols"</note> who took his turn, and those who first converted the world. In this letter, the Christians do not love their founders as much, though they conquered their land; God did not wish for them to possess it because they acted against His nature. This reader appeared to be quite exalted among the apostles; the letters of the apostles were weak in others. And the Prince of the Christians <note>The Pope</note> encouraged them to have the will of Christians in the words they have heard. The Fool <note>original: "lo sol" — a transcription of "lo fol," the character representing Ramon Llull himself</note> said these words. And furthermore, it seems to me that it is quite secluded and lost, because in the places where they are destroyed, they are loved. The Minstrel of Valor <note>original: "lo ioglur de valor" — an allegorical character representing moral praise</note> said that there is great dishonor and no value in that place where the Son of God and the apostles were; many hands of grace were gathered there, more than in any other place in this world. After these words, a messenger came who brought news of a death. A King of the Christians <note>original: "rei crishtian"</note> had died a bad death. When these words were said, the Minstrel of Valor said: "What use was the humility of **Jesus Christ** to him if he was not loved?" The people, when they wish to suffer the Passion <note>The suffering of Christ</note>, if they lose one hand, it is a great grief; but the Christians feel a greater grief when they lose the sun. Two cardinals <note>original: ".ij. cardenals"</note> cautioned themselves to speak of their love, and they spoke of the election, that they were all chosen in disagreement. Regarding the Passion, the Fool said to the cardinals that those words were most pleasing...

<column-break/>

...the Pope <note>original: "papa"</note> began to order the life of the Holy Mother Church, providing an example. And he prayed to God to grant him devotion. He sent for the masters of the religious orders: the Master of the **Temple** and the Master of the **Hospital** <note>The Knights Templar and the Knights Hospitaller, the primary military orders of the Crusades</note>, to discuss how to order the affairs of these gifts. When everyone had arrived and stood before the Pope, the cardinals and the Fool began to run toward the Friend and the Beloved <note>Llull's central mystical concept: the soul (the Friend) and God (the Beloved)</note>, for their praise and their sighs were the signs of love; their minds were purified. "This is no small matter," said the Minstrel, "signifying what is loved and what is done." And no worldly value followed from these signs. The Fool said: "Eternal scripture has written the names of the lovers and the beloved in a book. And it remains for me to wish that the lovers be in this book." And in that book was written the name of the Beloved. It was said that a lover should be cooked with the fire of love, and his hands washed with the tears of his eyes, and then he should be crucified. And the Fool, for love, had tied himself to his Beloved. The medicine of love, with which you are healed, with which you remain eternal. Without all these things, the lover is not worthy to be written in this book. After these words, the sixth of the cardinals and the religious [orders] praised the Church of God. And it was ordained that all the religious who performed loving deeds should be assigned to learn **languages** <note>original: "lenguirges"</note> and that there should be holy visits by their hands throughout all the world. And that missions <note>original: "messiof"</note> should be instructed for the poor, according to the arrangement of the monastery that is on the **island of Mallorca** <note>original: "laila de malcorca" — This refers to Miramar, the language school founded by Llull in 1276</note>. At the third wish, it pleased the others, and the lovers, that the apostles should die for the nation of the infidels <note>original: "isiels" — referring to non-Christians</note>, staying among them to learn their language and so that they might learn ours, and that by being among them, they might preach to them in their own tongue. And those who acted as apostles should ensure the essence of the **Holy Catholic Faith** <note>original: "s[an]ct[a] fe cattolica"</note> was given entirely. And in the end, God was praised. And when they were in their faith... seventeen servants who followed these holy ways so that they might first spread the testament through the world. And it happened that a youth who was among the infidels of **Alexandria** and **Greece** <note>original: "alixandria e de gregia"</note> received grace so that these religious might preach in our faith, and that their will might be one with our religious orders. And through this union, they corrected things that were against the Holy Faith. The Pope and the masters of our religious orders told the religious to take care and send a certain quantity of their own resources so that the language might be learned in **France** <note>original: "françf"</note> and other places. The religious were told: "I do not know if you are aware that many are losing the faith."

<column-break/>

...and that no one is compelled to read the law of God. From here, the masters and the friars who are in Christian lands ask that someone be assigned to hear and understand them. And in time, they shall create apostles. And if this does not follow, it shall be approved by the winds of the Holy Church, so that they may be made into apostles. And let that man be honored above others. And this one shall invite the others, and they shall make more efforts to understand the truth. And it was seen that the others had the same goal. When the Pope had said these words, the Chamberlain <note>original: "camariere" — the official in charge of the papal treasury</note> said that if this was established, the friars and the **Saracens** <note>original: "farrazinf"</note> who are in the lands would gain in other ways, and the income of the Holy Church would decrease. Then the Fool said to the Chamberlain that a man loved a woman and told her that he loved her more than any other woman. And the woman asked why he loved her more than others. He replied: <margin>because she is the most beautiful.</margin> And because that man followed her and won that part, the woman said that if another were more beautiful, he would love her more. This signifies that he loved her for her outward appearance. The Minstrel of Valor said that if there were anything better than God, the Chamberlain would love it more than God. It is a great thing for the glory of God and valor, regardless of the income or the labor that the saints perform for the glory of God.

<summary>The Pope and his court establish the first missionary language schools, specifically mentioning a monastery in Mallorca. The narrative uses an allegory of a man's shallow love to critique officials who prioritize Church income over divine glory.</summary>

...seeds and honors <note>original: "semes et ozar"</note>. For through all the kingdoms his fame resounded, fleeing throughout the world—a glory in which he stood alone. While the Cardinal was in that city, a message came to him from Rome through a spy, saying that he should return to Rome for his own safety. He was fearful in his heart because of certain individuals who were in a great verbal dispute <note>original: "gra uerbal-la"</note> at the court with one another. The Cardinal arrived in Rome; he effectively sent a message to the jealous man <note>original: "pauato"; referencing the man from the previous page</note> and made him understand that he should keep secret what he told him. He did this so that the Cardinal could make him believe that he was the only one in that place whom he recognized and favored, especially to the jealous man with whom he had a contract. For the man was a greedy man <note>original: "iome auar"</note>, and he believed that the Cardinal would send him all the riches of the world for his own use. And the Cardinal mentioned that he was doing everything for him. In the same way, it was confessed that he went from one to the other, so that by means of this, everything the Cardinal mentioned would be saved. And one and the other gave thanks, feeling that the Cardinal did not wish to speak to anyone else out of love. Thus, he made himself agreeable to one and the other. In this manner, the Cardinal held many other offices for the people, so that he might do his own will, and so that he might appear to be a mediator for their salvation. And so, in everything the Cardinal did and maintained, no one in the country could deny him, and all men recognized the Cardinal for the way he treated and managed the people.

<column-break/>

Through such a court, where all was pleasing and the stories were shared, the Cardinal held his high office. This pleased us, as there appeared at the court many business matters regarding the faithful who were pleading. The Cardinal was a safe haven, and his counsel there provided rule and doctrine; he settled the disputes of all those who were in tribulation. Because the Cardinal performed these services for the people in such a pleasing way before God, the people, who were often idle, received a share and a blessing from the Cardinal’s mind. Finally, the Cardinal remained in the city of Lausanne <note>original: "lauzana"; this could refer to the city or be a play on the word for "praise"</note> to be praised. 
Henry <note>original: "herry"</note>, the Cardinal of Lausanne, arrived at the court of Rome. And because of the state of the religious orders, he sent his envoys <note>original: "pauatos"; here used perhaps as messengers or attendants</note> throughout the landscape. He in his person performed many works to give the word of God and the work that he had put into practice. This was an assault on other persons of divinity, because of the work through which he had lived in love. And it happened, at the time when the Cardinal was praising God in another place, that he encountered a cleric and another person—a good woman—who were both praising God. They were seeking to help everyone. The Cardinal of Lausanne, through the strength of God, began to reason with them, recounting the praise of the deeds they performed for God. Then came the good woman, who was the mother of a son of whom it was said that he should only serve at the altar of God. She lived in a state of poverty <note>original: "pueto"</note>, and her husband was dead. She was very pleasing and she corrected those things that were enclosed within her ears as best as she could. And because

<column-break/>

of the correction that the good woman made to the one and the other, she was highly valued. In their language <note>The text likely refers here to the vernacular vs. Latin</note>, there were many common customs in that city. And everyone feared her because she was so firm in her rule and knowledge. She watched their actions certainly, and their failures. 
The cleric was a man of great prayer <note>original: "pauatocre"; likely a variant of "pauatoso" used here in a religious sense of "zealous"</note>. He behaved with great joy, for he was a man of prayer and love. He was an acceptable man who lived in a way that pleased his soul, and he governed it because he loved it. At various times, the cleric was seen to love and give alms to the poor. He visited the dwellings of the religious, and he did so often. He was loved by the clerics, whom he corrected for the failings they were seen to commit. He did not fear to speak the truth to any cleric who came before him to incite this state of mind. And in correcting them, he praised God; and in praising God, he corrected them. That cleric had a custom of going every day through the cemeteries <note>original: "carnunas"; places of the flesh/dead</note> to the altar, with a censer in his hand <note>original: "en sen ser"</note>. He went with the clergy who came there to receive correction. In this way, he came to the glories <note>shrines or chapels</note> in which he stayed in prayer, desiring to speak to God and to correct the clerics in the church when he saw them commit a thousand failings. For this reason, he made the churches more pleasing.
The other brother, who was a layman, also went through the cemeteries to the altar. And when he found any layman who was not right in his conduct, or who was excessive in his speech, or in whatever manner he was behaving, he corrected him. He would cast blame upon the person, and when they were corrected, he would praise them. He held

<column-break/>

that God should be held in high regard by those he loved to correct. And because of the audacity he had to undertake these things, those who heard him feared him, especially those who were in error. Great was the light <note>original: "lotz"</note> that this man brought to the city. And so it was that the good woman, the cleric, and the layman were all involved in these matters. 
Then a question <term>Questio<gloss>A formal scholarly or theological debate common in medieval universities and courts</gloss></term> arose among them: which of them all praised God better? Each of the parties maintained their side, as they were all praised by God in the city. Each of the parties came before the Cardinal and presented the question. This pleased the Cardinal greatly, and he brought the matter to Rome so that the Pope might provide a solution at the altar of Rome. One was the cleric, and the other was the layman, both of whom in a similar manner sought God. They wished for a stable rule within the city of Rome for all time. 
And when the Cardinal was in Rome, he brought the question before the Pope and the Cardinals. They were all amazed by how much they heard from him and the other Cardinals. They prayed that he would be protected from heresy and that he would be given faith. They said that he wished to perform a deed of great wonder and to show humility for the faith he held. This was to discourage those who lived in pride <note>original: "sup’bia"</note> and followed those vanities. The Cardinal, who had supervised the question, prayed that the solution might be understood in that dispute. For by the way of God, and by correcting the vices at the court of Rome, the hunger for faith would be satisfied. This pleased the Cardinal very much.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Cardinal in Rome, transitioning from his personal diplomatic maneuvers to a series of formal spiritual debates or "questions" involving a merchant and several symbolic prizes—a horse and a golden cup. so that he was able to give that horse <note>original: "palafte," likely a scribal variant of "palafre," a riding horse</note> and that cup, which he took so that he might show himself as one who honors God. Meanwhile, as the merchant went about his business, it greatly pleased the people of that city, who had a great desire to possess the horse and the cup of the other, which was worth much money. And they proposed many diverse ways of honoring God. In an election of many, one canon was made bishop, and the other envied him because he himself was not bishop; yet the bishop who won the election honored God because he found himself in a city of such great virtue, and all because of them <note>the electors</note>. And there was a great dispute beforehand regarding the "Question" <term>Questio: A formal academic or theological debate common in medieval universities</term> of who gave God the greater blessing: the question of who offered God more blessings, whether it was the man to whom God granted fame and wealth in this world to honor God, or rather that man who is unfortunate and poor, yet recognizes that it is through God's will that he has his suffering <note>original: "royzalt," likely relating to hardship or trial</note>, and thus honors God?

**F**urthermore, it was asked regarding one born blind, who had never seen, and the man who became blind <note>original: "temeu’e dieu s de paacrias," likely referring to an acquired affliction</note>—the man who was born blind honors God in his providence, and the other honors God when he regains his sight; just as the guilty man honors God when he is pardoned, or the man who is healthy and honors God. And then there are the lepers <note>original: "meselt"</note> and the rulers, and the others who, through everything that God gives them, honor Him. Then the question is asked: who honors God more fervently? Next, it was asked concerning a man and a woman: the question of who should honor Him more.

<column-break/>

The man, because he did not see the end... or the woman, because she was not a man and was more subjugated <note>original: "iugada," meaning judged or placed under authority</note> in this world than the man? **A**nother question was raised concerning a lady and a king... and who would have more fear of God? This question was between a lady and her son: for the son honored God because He had given him his nature and birth, and the mother honored God because He had given her the son. **A** judge <note>original: "Giur"</note> honored God because He had given him his people, and the people honored God because He had granted them their prince; and because of this, there is a question as to who seeks God better. **A**ll these questions and many others were recognized and entrusted to the merchant, so that he might take the horse and the golden cup to the one who could present the best "Question"—that which most strongly signified who honors God best. **A**nd the merchant, having these questions of love and work, went with his horse and the golden cup and came to Rome to the Cardinal to honor God. He begged the Cardinal to help him resolve these questions through a more refined discourse <note>original: "d’un mielt dit af"</note>, so that he might know the manner in which one might best honor God. Thus, he recognized that through the horse and the cup, he could reward the one who brought the best question. The Cardinal, along with the merchant, went into the Consistory <term>Consistory: A formal meeting of the Pope and his Cardinals</term> before the Pope and the other Cardinals to solve the questions. And after they were certain, the Pope and the Cardinals sat, for these questions had been debated for a long time so that they would not be forgotten, and so that they might be truly remembered. This record <note>original: "estample"</note> was then recorded for the hearing and the honor of God, so that it might be applied according to the custom of the law.

<meta>This page continues the narrative from page 57 regarding the Cardinal's efforts in Rome to promote the honoring of God through symbolic prizes and public acts. It transitions from the theological "Questions" to the practical application of these lessons by the character Blaquerna, who has now become Pope.</meta>

God. The Cardinal’s conclusion was of greater utility. And for this reason, he established a decree that at the gate of the city of Rome there should be a **marble palfrey** <note>original: "palafit," usually a riding horse (palfrey), but here described as a statue or monument</note>, and a man remaining there who held a cup. And upon the pedestal were to be inscribed the verses of the love of Jesus. And another time, while the merchant went about his business, he stayed behind. One day, while the Cardinal was walking, having with him a man of God and a youth before him, they came to a riverbank where there was a great crowd. There was a rogue <note>original: "arlot," a person of low station or a beggar</note> who was begging with a bowl. And the rogue was speaking villainous words against Jesus Christ and Our Lady, because he had nothing to eat.

The Cardinal dismounted from his horse <note>original: "palafit"</note> and gave it to the rogue who was blaspheming God, because he possessed nothing and yet did not cease his blasphemy. This action was reported to the Apostle <note>The Pope is often referred to as "the Apostle" in this period</note> and the other Cardinals, to whom it was said to be an act of madness. One day, a master of theology was singing Mass, and he had been speaking for a long time about how a scholar should be treated by his judge. The Cardinal said, reflecting: "Since there are so many rogues and vagabonds who speak ill and blaspheme God, Our Lady, and the saints of glory, I desire that they be treated with justice, yet also with mercy." He sought to correct their malice, for those who act against God’s blessing are under a curse.

The Cardinal spoke these strong words to his companions, and it was recorded as a testament. He spoke of how he punished the unwilling. He said that it is better that a man does not deny the name of his beloved. The fool asked: "Since they are so worthy for their beloved, to whom they show such love, why are they in such a state of misery?" He was answered that it was time for them to rest from the labor they suffered for their beloved. Concerning these words, a lawyer spoke to the Pope, seeking to fulfill his mandate to protect a prince who was deserting the Church. The Cardinal of Elena said: "Love has made it so that the Pope’s cause is not denied, for people blaspheme and dishonor God more because of the pride of antiquity."

**Blaquerna**, as Pope, was walking one day through the squares of Rome. He went into a hovel <note>original: "ratteria," a place for the poor or a low tavern</note> and heard someone say, "Blessed be the name of God." The Pope asked how it could be that a rogue held God in such high regard. A knight of the city of Rome replied: "Holy Father, a rogue here was given a horse by the Cardinal of Elena, and it has become the custom that when the rogues hear those words spoken by the Cardinal—that everything he does is for the love of God—their hearts are changed." The knight told how he himself had changed his ways because of this.

<column-break/>

Because he heard the name of God, all the rogues and vagabonds of this city now honor the name of God. And that rogue, through the custom he started, has caused the people to use good words. It pleased the Apostle <note>the Pope</note> to hear the words the rogue spoke, and he said that if the rogue's life was once judged as foul, it was now glorious. The Pope wondered at how much honor could be produced by making rogues and vagabonds hold God in high regard, even more so than those in high estates.

"Holy Father," said the rogue, "in that place where the name of God is most dishonored, that is where people should most strive to honor it." The Apostle understood the words of the rogue and said it was a very necessary thing that the name of God be held in honor among those people who are sinners, who usually show no honor to God’s name.

After the Pope had spoken these words, a knight came to him and said: "Holy man, I have been a man of arms, and I have spent my life in hearing Mass and destroying the enemies of that Name. If it pleases you, let us not be like those who are near death and have lived evilly, but let us speak words of truth to God, so that men who are dying may be more confirmed in the faith." He spoke of the joy of God and that they should die with the name of God on their lips. The Pope <unclear>granted him justice... in his palace... those words... which he said... regarding the death of that man... and all the earth... which went to that place... and it was a very pious thing... and the rest...</unclear>

<margin>He restored them, and this move made them...</margin>

<unclear>to work... and the name... and they said... the rogues... and the mercy... of the men who were dying... in the city... the name of God... and the justice... and the words of God... they were held... and the death... and the life... he died... of death... and the life... and the death... of the earth... and the city... and the words... and the life...</unclear>

The <unclear>last words... of the cardinal... very strongly... and the life... of the forest... and the world... and the city... and the forest... and the life... and all the people... of God... and the life... and the city... and the names... and the words... and the life... and the death... and the name of God... and the life...</unclear>

The text emphasizes that even the most "vile" members of society can be brought to piety through mercy and shocking acts of generosity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the journey of the Cardinal and his companions as they explore different forms of devotion and encounter various perspectives on faith, following the Cardinal's act of charity to the beggar on the previous page.</meta>

### On the Nature of Worship

**Column 1**

That he did not believe was common among the people. While **the Understanding** <note>original: "l'entenāl," personified as a character or faculty of the mind traveling with the Cardinal</note> was approaching a point of consideration regarding what is said of the soul, in one place where he passed, there was a church where a poor man was asking for light <note>likely asking for candles or alms to buy oil for the lamps</note>. He used to enter the Mass to beg for the alms that people gave him. In that church, there was a very great abbey, and they lived there very nobly. In that church, a **pilgrim** <note>original: "pelegri"</note> was kneeling, praising God with great Christian devotion. The people held him in high regard as he prayed in the church. Meanwhile, the man who was traveling with the Cardinal entered the church and approached the pilgrim who was on his pilgrimage. The Cardinal asked the pilgrim why he was worshiping the sign that represents the image of Jesus Christ.

"Lord," said the pilgrim, "it is the custom in the Holy Land that people worship idols, and in this time in which I live, I see many who worship idols and saints." He continued, "And the Jews reproach us Christians because we worship images. And because an image carved in wood is closer in figure to an idol than a flat image <note>The text refers to the medieval debate over icons: whether a three-dimensional statue was "more like an idol" than a two-dimensional painting</note>, it signifies that flat images are more suitable than those in the round. For this reason, it is the custom of some to remove all carved images that are upon the altar, lest they resemble idols."

While the Cardinal and the pilgrim were in this conversation, a man who had come from a long distance approached the altar; and there, he began to pray to God very fervently. The Cardinal perceived that in that man there was a great love for God or words of...

<column-break/>

**Column 2**

...love for the Beloved, whom he worshiped so much that he did not focus on anything else. He wished to know the manner in which he worshiped God. That man said: "It is often the custom of those full of the love of the world that they worship God for worldly glory, and because it brings them wealth, and many times they worship Him so that He might grant their temporal desires. But one should worship in a different way, more securely regarding the necessities that a man has, focusing on the height that is in God." Because he was a great mediator, he wished to consider the points and the loves of the saints. And in the villages, the people were so full of wonder at a **palmer** <note>a pilgrim who has returned from the Holy Land carrying a palm branch</note> and they gave him gold and honors and dignities. Because of his height and his perfection, they held the man in such honor and love, and showed reverence to the prayer.

<margin>
At this point, much grace was received by the Cardinal, the pilgrim, and the man.
</margin>

Wishing to find the best way that a man can have in worshiping God, they returned to the **Canticle of Rome** <note>original: "acantar de ròma," possibly referring to the Roman liturgy or the Papal court</note> so that he might represent to the Apostle <note>The Pope</note> the various rites as is said above. While they were walking and talking together about the manner in which a man should worship God, they encountered a **Jew**. He who was approaching was very weary from a long journey he had made. In his face, he bore a sign of great despair and great sorrow. The Cardinal asked the Jew why he walked so sadly and so constrained.

"Lord," said the Jew, "I am in a great prison of thought from which I cannot escape."

---

It weighed heavily upon his soul, for it brings no pleasure to God to love death. The Cardinal wished to know the thought in which the Jew was trapped. And the Jew said to him, according to these words: "Lord," said the Jew, "I remember when it pleased God to believe that we were His people, chosen above all other peoples. We were enslaved at various times: once for four hundred years <note>referring to the bondage in Egypt</note>, and another for seventy years <note>referring to the Babylonian Captivity</note>. In all those times, we were held because we were holy. But now, we have been in this state for two hundred years, and we do not know why. And I have heard it said that we are blamed for the death of Christ, for which we are in enmity." And he felt like a man wounded, signifying that we are in enmity because we do not worship **Jesus Christ**.

**The Understanding** <note>the faculty of reason/personified character</note> put this question to the Jew. It was a question about how to firmly love God. One man had a way of worshiping through the mountains and the plains—that is, worshiping God in the herbs, plants, and the things he sought, which is the way of the Christians. Another knew how to worship God in that which God does above nature, such as in the **miracles** that God performed: creating the world from nothing, and raising the dead, and in the great works that nature cannot

<column-break/>

**Column 2 (continued)**

perform. The third worshiped God in that which God is in Himself, and in that which God is outside of creation. He asked which of these worshiped God more perfectly. One worshiped God in the ways said before.

<margin>
Who worships God more strongly?
</margin>

"Lord," said the Jew, "the one who worships God for what God is in Himself worships more strongly than those who worship according to the other manners mentioned. For he worships Him for only one reason." And then **Understanding** said that he should remain in that love.

And the **Philosophers** are those who see God through the works that are shown in natural causes. And the **Jews** are those who worship God in the miracles—the works that God performs above nature. But the time has come when the Jews ask for miracles and do not acknowledge God in that which He is, or in what He does above nature to demonstrate His truth. And the **Christians** acknowledge what God is in Himself: the great work that nature cannot perform, regarding the **Father** who is God, the

<margin>
the Son who is God
</margin>

**Son** who is God, and the **Holy Spirit** who is God. Yet they are but one God. In that unity, God took upon Himself **human nature**, in which there is but one person. And because He has been and continues to be, and died for you, He came to you to save you, having suffered death; for otherwise you would not have been redeemed.

<summary>This page explores different modes of worship through a dialogue between a Cardinal, a pilgrim, and a Jew. It categorizes worship into three types: the philosophical (through nature), the Jewish (through miracles), and the Christian (through the Trinity and the Incarnation).</summary>

### On True Worship and the Vanity of Riches

**Column 1**

It is pointed out to us if there is a certain agreement that Christians went to the saints. It is said that the city of Nineveh is greater <note>A biblical reference to the size and repentance of Nineveh</note> in dark forests, if not for the reason that it was to be submitted to the sun through an act of mercy and humility, as the Cardinal Bishop had said to him. You, who are reported to have come to Jericho to learn, see that in those synagogues of the Jews they adore God on the Sabbath days according to the likeness of what they have heard said. And along that way, the Pope <note>original: "la pstoli," referring to the Apostolic Lord or Pope</note> granted his privilege to the Cardinal, seeing that the Pope’s officials were to grant him his vision. It seemed a wonderful thing to hold to the Pope's authority. And I adjure you that they would do nothing to repent toward the Pope, in which term the Jews would do what he himself intended to say—that it would be seen what a Christian had recognized of the Catholic faith. This truth delivers him from the errors of Muhammad <note>original: "mahomet"</note> in which there was folly. And because the truth would clarify, for a duplicate cannot be proven by words passed in a new light. If one suffers more than another, then the faith is manifested as a Christian in the faith that he replicates. With this, the Pope’s letter was said to be probable; for if it were so, he would become a Christian. He would already adore Jesus Christ as they say, and would turn his whole heart to the Church of Rome. And so all would become worshippers of Jesus Christ. Various letters were given as a pledge from the Pope and to the Cardinals. There was a Gentile <note>original: "gentil," a person not yet converted to Christianity</note> who had traveled a long way...

<column-break/>

**Column 2**

...part. And he had nothing to gain. In that city, there was a great redemption, for there were princes who worshipped the sun. The sun, and the stars, and the birds, and the wind. Those people of that region are many; they are tall and black, and they have no law. And it was known that for a long time it was fitting that one cause alone should be worshipped, and that it should be more noble than any of those things they were worshipping. Because that city did not know what was happening, it was thought that they were in service of the enemy <note>referring to the devil or paganism</note>. What cause was it that should truly be worshipped? For a long time, this man, this messenger, sought it out. And with diligence, he sought the people with whom he did business, to see if he could find knowledge of that cause which ought to be worshipped above all things. And they made it certain. There were Jews, but they did not want to show him what thing could be the one worthy of being worshipped above all things. And a messenger, the one who came to the Pope, and the Pope immediately sent the articles of the faith. And they were opened, through which these things were demonstrated as probable. And through the brothers who had learned well, they did what was necessary to bring a message to the Pope. And that following, by the great power of God, the King was converted, along with a great number of his people. And with the messenger of the Pope, they went to that same island where the Gentile had come. And there he demonstrated the necessary reasons...

<column-break/>

**Column 3**

...those people of that city worshipped idols in which they were made to believe. The saints, knowing the change that was among those people, were solicitous through the mission. When the archpriests entered into their lives, they tempted many of those people to be baptized with great joy and with relics. And a book was delivered, and the Pope took possession with many plates and relics. And others argued that they did everything clearly so that they could understand. The Cardinal treated that profession of faith and recounted that there were many lands in the world where God was not known nor worshipped. And he marveled greatly how God was worshipped by these kings. While the Cardinal continued in this way, he frequently saw the great efficiency of the silversmiths <note>original: "argēters"</note>. They were selling what remained of their work, such as engraved items, basins, pitchers, and cutters, making them of gold and silver. The Cardinal was very wise. The Cardinal looked at them and asked why they had brought these jewels. It was said that they were made for the city as he had ordered. Those mentioned did his bidding. But he who owned the jewels was much scandalized, thinking the profession was lost because the Cardinal had ordered his executors to do this. He wondered if the city was being deceived... the Cardinal from then on had a thought that followed his profession...

<column-break/>

**Column 4**

...which was all adorned <note>original: "cenada," perhaps girded or decorated</note> with gold and silver and precious stones. And she cast a light like the natural colors that are placed within the images in which you see the truth. The Cardinal knelt before the lady, without it seeming that he wished to worship her, and said that she was similar to Saint Thecla <note>original: "dycla"; St. Thecla was a legendary saint of the early Church, often associated with beauty and miraculous light</note>. And after kneeling to her, the lady and all those with her were greatly amazed. For the Pope heard of this profession and was very troubled, asking for what reason the Cardinal had accepted the profession or had knelt before the lady. And the Cardinal immediately told him that he had been thinking how many lands in the world there are where God was not worshipped. And since the boxes of gold and silver and the royal jewels of that place were so many... he had ordered that those jewels be distributed <note>original: "d s t r n i d a s," likely "destruïdes" (destroyed) or "distribuïdes" (distributed/sold)</note>. And because of this, the Pope was moved to decree that from now on none of those jewels should be in any city or place, so that they should not be worshipped. According to the lady’s will, he then established that no woman who was born and named should dress in the likeness of a sacred image <note>original: "pprsefsio"</note>, nor should she enter into the church during the singing of the Mass.

### Column 1

It appeared in a place that he prepared in which the forest was provided <note>The text uses "emamana," likely meaning prepared or provided with resources</note> with great delight. And it happened that an evil remnant of winter <note>original: "hiu͛g"</note>, as they say, brought rain to that place where the king was. And the king was in his tent; for even though he was a powerful king with many garments, he could not protect his person from the cold or the foul weather that was in that place. Alas! It stripped him of his humanity. He had his people and his poor ones, whom he could not defend from the bad weather. 

When the Cardinal had spoken this example, he turned to the exposition of these words. The king, the Pope <note>original: "piſtolı," referring to the Apostolic Lord</note>, the cardinals, and the prelates of the Holy Church who have no poverty are like religious men; and the men who joined from various languages appeared at that inquiry, and they understood it. And let the pious be there in the interpretation. 

Saint, this custom would be pleasing to the will of God for all those who want and ought to do it; and we do not know well how the will of God in this world is served. Those who die under the heretics <note>original: "aritihaſ," likely referring to heretics or perhaps a specific group like the Arians</note> who are in that place are satiated, and he nourishes those who are his enemies; to those who have ignorance of the state for various reasons, and those who, through the greed in which they are associated, the prince does many cares to their persons, and to their servants and their daughters, to permit many denials of the art of the heretics, and they renounce it. And for this, they are not in the state in which they are by the will of God. 

When the prelate had heard these words, he had in his eyes a vision where it seemed that in that place, the prince who fought with another prince in a certain manner, if they were pious, would be constrained by it. So that they were made to move toward other heretics who were fighting with the faithful; and according to the language of those people, they would not swear falsely, and they would participate with them. And the prelates heard these things to hold the prelate so that he could make them understand the will of God. 

And in great will, the Cardinal spoke of the will of God, so that he might better understand how to glorify God. He had his confidence that through various ways they have the will of God. And it happened one day that the Cardinal was walking through the streets of Rome, and passed by the prelate where there were many people. And he asked the men who had gathered in the square what will it was that made them love so diligently. And each one responded that the will they had was focused on temporal business, and thus they went about diligently. And because of this, the Cardinal found a man in the square who searched for the cries of various servants, their understanding being to finish God's work by wanting to provide for the business of this world. And so that they might understand God, they procured it so that in the will of God... that the will they had for business was changed. That man then took the Cardinal; at times they were the men who were there, who would have given their will to serve the will of God. And the Cardinal made a faithful helper who had suffered for the matters of...

### Column 2

...to help or to fulfill the will of God to see them. He spoke of eighteen articles <note>referring to points of faith or law</note> regarding what he commanded them to do in the **Ten Commandments**, and that they should glorify the will of God. For he who seeks to have fear, if he wishes to love and wishes to give the most high mountain <note>original: "alpin," likely used here metaphorically for something great or high</note> to this man who was saying to the brothers these things that glorified the will of God, which had made them men and heirs. For if he wished the will of God, he would not have taken that mountain, but would have taken our nature, and would have had the highest will. And those men [followed] the heirs. 

When the Cardinal had finished, a man who served him came to speak. That man told the Cardinal that he would live. When the scribe went to tell how he had served the will of God, he gave him these words of danger to say. 

The Cardinal understood that it is very necessary to love the will of God above all things. He saw that a man had pity for the beggars and for the religious. And that by doing this, he received them in the churches; and in those places he found those who remember God and love the will of God, which he commands in the **Gospels** <note>original: "auangelıſ"</note>: that they should pray for the poor. And he turned and did it. That man has beauty through his actions. 

And it happened one day that two clerics <note>original: "dergues"</note> in the country said that the Cardinal was a beloved of God. And then it was heard that God our Lord, who commands the poor and the faithful, that God does not love the highest mountain <note>metaphor for pride</note>. That man was much scandalized by what those two clerics had said to him. He said that God's command goes to all, and is shared among the people and the churches. And because God wanted that man to be loved, he prayed that those who are in the churches should take care to help for love, and that no one should say that he is superior unless he is worthy and follows the will of God. 

While he was in this state, the two clerics came again to the court of the Pope <note>original: "pısolı"</note>, and the Cardinal said: "If you have helped that one for a long time, see if you have not served the will of God in going through the paths to beg for the will of God." And the man gave for the love of God. And he told them not to be in the quest for the love of God, for he had that will in his soul; and that for the pleasure of it he should take care that they do not want the will of God, and do not give for their own will but for the will of God. That he had helped for the love of God so that he might know the will of God. And from those who take the alms that man commands them, from which they understand those who are lovers for the love of God. That helper made much effort in his faith, for he had to beg for his life and spend his days in the church contemplating the will of God. that which is contrary to his nature. And where is this custom now purified in which a man lives? The philosophers and the great ones <note>The text mentions "gnat nu eitheo," likely a corruption of "great theologians" or a similar Latinate Greek term</note> say that there is a beginning... so many means of understanding. To the senses, it is given that one cannot do that which is by path... according to his will, working above nature. This pleased that universal one, and the philosophers know much of its provenance. A greedy man who always knows himself to be a greedy man. After these words, the Cardinal went with the King and took the grain, for he did not know how to motivate his son’s tears. And this was according to what was customary in that land. Full of joy, the princes who were there showed him their sons, so that he might govern the people with his virtue. This pleased the King, following what the Cardinal had said, and he was obedient to his command. Inasmuch as the Cardinal desired to multiply [knowledge] and to leave his table, he went to the chapter of the religious orders. And when he went to the lectures on Law, he saw that everyone was going there to adorn their reputations. And God was multiplied among men so that they might know and love Him. It happened once that a son of a count went by his own will to study Law. And the Cardinal, who was going to Valence for the General Chapter of the <term>Preachers</term> <note>The Order of Preachers, commonly known as the Dominicans</note>, met this son of the count. Since the paths of their journey were joined, it pleased him to speak of the love of Holy <term>Theology</term>, which is more necessary to a cleric than Law. At this point, the son of the count took this to heart and applied himself to Theology, in which science he became a master. Now the Cardinal went to the school of the Preachers to hear the lesson of Theology. At that time, there was a great school of Law where there were many students dressed in ecclesiastical habit. He went forward to another school full of <note>original: "dactalitas," likely referring to those pursuing doctorates or specific doctrines</note> who were in ecclesiastical habit. And the Cardinal made a mandate to those students of Law, because

<column-break/>

in the city, there was too much secularity. And for this reason, that city wanted to be secular. They established that they should not stay in the tower; there, those who knew the Law were admonished to have love for God above all. And he especially made that mandate because the beginning was among the lawyers who learned the science of Laws without <note>original: "dactalitas," doctoral authority or spiritual doctrine</note>. For this reason, he wanted them to strive for a different lesson. After this, the Cardinal entered the school of the Word of God, which is called Theology. In all this school, there was only one student, the son of a religious man. And for this reason, the Cardinal went to a certain place that had a great reputation for its fame, and because he loved the doctrine found there, which showed the divine holiness. And for this reason, the Cardinal gave a mandate that everyone should go there. Once it happened that from the schools of Law, some sinners were made, and they ended up diminished. And they came to this place to learn Theology. In this great school, they learned from the letters of Holy Church. And for this reason, they left the Law. Hearing this, I rejoiced in the Law of Theology. By this mandate, many lords and the Cardinal himself were seen to attain holiness. Because in holiness one finds true knowledge, just as the man who is ingenious with God understands everything. To these four graces and the consummation of love, he said:

> God, heavenly King, God Father almighty. <note>original Latin: "deus rey celestis dens super o i potens"</note>

In the consistory, the Cardinal was before God, and spoke quickly to the

<column-break/>

other cardinals, saying that he wished to see and recognize what the power of God had taught everyone in this world, so that his voice might recommend his manner. And it happened one day that the <term>Apostolic Lord</term> <note>The Pope</note> wanted to send messengers to the land called India. And there, in essence, was a king named Ariha. And the Pope ordered that king to send him some religious men from those lands, so that they might show their language and their ways to the Latin speakers. And that a priest should be sent there, so that with the Latin priests they might stay in their kingdom and bring them to the faith. While the Pope was dictating these letters, the <term>amariane</term> <note>Possibly a chamberlain or high official</note> said that when people return, they should keep their own language and show the Latin ways to those people. And it was then that in a short time he would send a message. The Cardinal was very displeased with what the official had said. And this was fulfilled once when he was traveling on a road toward a black lagoon where a great number of <term>demons</term> <note>original: "dmoq"</note> were watching a man who had drowned in the water. He asked those men why that man had not gone to the bank of the water to cross from one part to the other as a man should. And he had a good view of the bank. They replied that when they raised this man from that side... And for this reason, the Cardinal strongly reprimanded the official, saying that by his words he was delaying what the Pope wanted from the religious men of the Church.

<column-break/>

To use such power cannot return <term>Lucifer</term> <note>original: "sifer"</note> if he is damned in that state, to the state of salvation, for he has passed from the present life to the eternal one. And I say that he had suffered long because he had nowhere else to go. It happened one day that he fell into a very grievous continuous fever. The Cardinal was very displeased with the medicine. And his physician told him that he should praise God if he wished to be cured of the torment that troubled him. The Cardinal replied that he had no fear of losing the fever, for that power belongs only to God, who through His power over nature can do everything He wills. And I say that God's power is above the power of nature. And for this reason, God could make the first man from nothing, and cause a virgin woman to conceive, for this is a divine work. And the power that rules the stars and the celestial bodies is conquered and overcome by the supreme power. Truly, his son said to him... For these times confirmed the virtues in their hearts, and they overcame and surpassed the infidels who did not believe that a virgin could conceive, nor that God could become man, nor rise again. Since the power of God is as great in creating man from nothing, which is a power above nature, the Cardinal said to the Pope: "Do you hold that power is finite or infinite?" The Pope replied and said that it must have the greatest power that exists, because it has the help of God. And as there is

<meta>This page continues the narrative from page 63, where the Cardinal was teaching students of Law and Theology. It moves into a deeper theological debate regarding the nature of truth, falsehood, and the hierarchy of spiritual power over physical force.</meta>

# Page 64

To be-falsehood is to not love God, and it begins with non-being. And through these words, the Cardinal demonstrated to those who tolerate falsehood that it exists in the world because truth is often hidden; for he wished that truth should reside in God. And the <term>Pope</term> <note>original: "apostoli," a common medieval term for the Pope</note> responded that this was not because falsehood had any inherent power, but rather it was through a lack of <term>charity</term> <note>meaning divine love or courageous compassion</note> that one does not wish to show the truth. And for this reason, falsehood maintains itself only through a lack of light. And it follows that falsehood exists because people do not dare to speak or to cherish the truth in any pronouncement without fear. And truly, when the word of God is not preached to the <term>infidels</term> <note>non-Christians, likely referring to Muslims or Jews in this context</note>, they are not without excuse, for the people of the earth are kept in ignorance. And the Cardinal turned to secular matters and placed before the Christian princes a plan so that, by the force of arms, the truth might be guided and protected; and all those who do not serve the truth were criticized by the saints who wished to preach the word of God. He desired that the Church should have no fear of any infidel who does not submit himself, so that the <term>Acts of the Apostles</term> might restore the truth of the Catholic faith. So great was the power of the Holy Spirit that the infidels of that land were struck with fear when they heard the preaching. And they came into this state—to stop and reflect—as they were suffering because the Christians, in their community, preached and conferred with the infidels. And it happened that a king <unclear>fat m i m o it</unclear> throughout the land did not want his subjects to be converted or to see preaching in his land. And a <term>Friar</term> <note>original: "fran re," likely a Franciscan</note> who had been driven from their land came to the Cardinal to speak of God,

<column-break/>

saying that they could not stay in that land because the <term>Saracens</term> <note>original: "fanatins," likely a corruption of "sarraïns"</note> did not want them there. Now the Cardinal says that they had turned back the power of the will, which is stronger and more noble than physical power. And it seemed to him that he should speak to the Pope, saying these words: "Holy Pope," said the Cardinal, "through the <unclear>j ar ene</unclear> brought by the love of God, physical power is obligated to submit itself to spiritual power. If kings act with physical power against the power of our Master, let it be known that it is a folly; instead, one should show and pray upon the Passion of God. And through this, the primary stone of the <unclear>gremial</unclear> and the merit that is hidden shall be revealed to the infidels, and through common prayer, physical power shall be overcome by the spiritual." Thus it is signified that through the power of God, the Lord comes spiritually. And it is revealed that He is in all creatures according to what is represented in the Sacred Scripture. The Cardinal was fervent and held that the power of God was the pillar <note>original: "culon nar"</note> that sustains all. He spoke of how the power of God is like a table for a man to hold for the world, so that he is not confused by these mature or ill-placed evils, nor by the winds from below, nor by any other power through which the grace of the divine power is lost. To another official, the Cardinal spoke of the power of God and held that He could do all things, since nothing was made without Him. To the official, he asked what works he could do by his own means,

<column-break/>

for he was lost and did not know the path he should follow. And he began to describe what this land was like. In another way, it is seen that no evil or falsehood comes from His power, for many things can be done that seem like a shadow, yet in the city, one does all that one can. To an official, he said that the saints, in the time of God, had a regular and active life in this lineage. And another time, he showed how human officials and their pride are diminished by their own prayers. Another time, he said to a man who was prideful: there was no man who was able to create even a single leaf of a tree <note>A common theological argument: man can build, but only God can create life</note>, nor a feather, nor anything else; and yet some have great fame. And the Cardinal and his official spoke to God, for when a man loves Him, he is freed from the weight of worldly power. He turned to confessing through the disciples, and a man was sent by the Cardinal who was obedient to the will of God. And from the Cardinal, he received much merit. And because of this, having fled from falsehood and appearances, he turned to the Cardinal with divine love, saying: "Jesus Christ is my guide."

Great was the devotion that the book says the Cardinal had for the person of the Son of God, who is the light of humanity. Because of the devotion he had for the Son of God, the Pope said these words to the Cardinal: "As we have been made apostles and messengers, and you are a Cardinal, we have

<column-break/>

received authority over all the arts to speak and promote God." And for this reason, it is worthy to remember the obligation to go throughout the world to show the truth of the faith, according to the manner in which the Son of God was sent into the world to save the human race through His death. Thus, the Pope ordered this business: he requested messengers and asked for men of merit to go forth. Those who are chosen should go throughout the world to show the total truth of the world, and to turn hearts away from favor toward others, and instead toward the Son of God, loving Him throughout the world. And they should go to recount the good fortune of what was done for the love of us in the world. It pleased the Pope to entrust this task to the Cardinal. And he performed the virtue of what was requested. And so was fulfilled the figure of the Emperors of Rome, who were sent to govern all the world; so many messengers did he send out to show the truth of the world. In this figure, one can see the increase of God's glory. And the Lord of Rome <note>The Pope</note> and the health of the faith were preserved, so that all might be submitted to the Catholic faith. He sent them out to the world in twelve parts, making them messengers who should love the truth above all the gold and wealth of the world. And it happened that a messenger of the Cardinal went with great speed and obedience. And he performed his duty with such diligence that in twelve days he had come to Rome with that news.

As the emperors, according to what is written in the chronicles <note>Historical records of the deeds of kings and emperors</note>, took such pleasure in the Romans that they spoke all their words together, so for more than a hundred years they have strived, above all with their money and their members, to sustain their gold and their treasure. And for this reason, they were very prompt in protecting what they used here, so that at some time the Romans might keep their path and hold themselves toward their goal, following more what is of the flesh. The Cardinal held the <term>Pope</term> <note>original: "apostoli"; the standard Old Catalan term for the Bishop of Rome</note> and the Cardinals in great esteem regarding the news and novelties that came to him every day from various parts of the world.

It happened one day that a man <unclear>pome proque</unclear> who was <unclear>dimie' se fetuha</unclear> and was newly recovered by the Cardinal's part, came with the other Romans. When he performed the <term>office for the dead</term> <note>original: "morte de .v. ans"; likely a five-year anniversary mass</note> here, the man did not fail to divine and recount what happened to the Cardinal, who kept that manner in mind in the registry. Here, the man, because he lived by the alliance and that establishment which recounted news, did not hold himself as a Roman. The master soul <unclear>mulla ca' na</unclear> who was helping him was pleased that from the house he had newly recovered the son of the Cardinal, who said: "God, Lamb of God, Son of the Father" <note>original Latin: "deus agnus dei filius patris"</note>.

A **Chapter** <note>A formal assembly of members of a religious order or clergy to manage affairs</note> was then held with the other high officials in the narrow places, and this point was discussed. There was a rule that those of the <term>religious orders</term> who were in France were governed by a Chapter in the city of <unclear>r' eonon</unclear> every year.

<column-break/>

the way of Ferran in religion <unclear>t' en si mĩ al</unclear>. And for this reason, it is a necessary cause for us to have to rule the laity, so that a Chapter comes in such a form that it is just and pleasing to God. For if a **General Chapter** <note>original: "capitol g' rial"; a supreme assembly of a religious order</note> or imperial one does not rule, the state of the world cannot be perfect.

And so that this might be so, in whatever house or place <unclear>co' pũtiuo</unclear> it was agreed by all the Cardinals that the Pope should act with the Cardinals to obtain a Chapter in this form. It was ordained that every year a Chapter should be held, and that they should act in this <unclear>occ' enten ue fq' t</unclear>, and that at the Chapter it should be decided who is worthy of being punished for the failures they have committed throughout the year.

Afterward, it was established that every year an **Archbishop** should hold a Chapter with his **Bishops**, and that those who are accused in these Chapters should be punished by their Archbishop. Furthermore, the Pope ordained through the Cardinals that in one part of the world the Archbishops of one quarter should come to one place, and those of the other quarter to another. And this should be done so that the Cardinals might hold Chapters for them. There was one Cardinal for each corner <note>likely referring to the four quarters of the world</note>, and the Cardinals were set above the Archbishops for this establishment. They ordained that the Pope should hold a Chapter with the Cardinals once every year, and that those in whom failure was found should be punished without delay.

An establishment was made regarding those who were over the Pope <note>referring to a council or collective oversight</note>, and that once a year there should be a Chapter, and if there was any failure, it should be punished. And then they ordained that every five years they should hold a **Council** <note>original: "cõmili"; an ecumenical or provincial assembly of the Church</note> where all the Bishops should come. The Pope or the Cardinals <unclear>calf cuos</unclear> do not wish to see a way of defending themselves except through the law, by which they are governed.

<meta>This page continues the moral and legal instructions directed by the Cardinal figure. While the previous page focused on the institutional reforms of the Roman courts and the payment of judges, this page shifts toward the spiritual and moral justifications for such laws, using allegorical stories and dialogues to illustrate virtues like humility and patience.</meta>

**Column 1**

### 1.2. How the soul is disinherited and deprived of its kingdom without any right to hold it.

And its cries made the **king** weep, and he showed a sign of **great mercy** <note>original: "grā misericordia"</note> toward that poor soul. While standing around the garden with his retinue, he took pity on those people who were so prideful that they sought to kill through advantage. 

While the king was consoling himself and weeping, the **Cardinal** <note>The "Cardinal" acts as the voice of moral authority and legal wisdom throughout this text.</note> spoke to the king, asking which thing was more pleasing to him: to judge or to give a gentle response. The king replied that a gentle visit was better. The Cardinal told him that it was better to be humble and pleasing to the king than to be prideful. He should think on why he wept and where his love was settled; for he who is cast out ought to weep because of his unjust pride.

After this comparison, another follows—that is, a fable <note>original: "afab'"; likely a moral parable or exemplum</note> about how much more it is worth to be loved by **God** for showing **humility** than to be praised by people for worldly things. I tell you, poor ones, that the Cardinal said it is good to help the poor. If God gives you weight in this world, and the kingdom is taken from me, from then on the soul prophesies **patience**, **spiritual humility**, and bright singing, for such was the **will of God**. 

It pleased the king, and those who had served him were restored to that which they had been disinherited from. And from that court came a truth that shall be told in this chapter: that truth was a man of vision.

<column-break/>

**Column 2**

And of **holy life** and the same supreme **justice**. In his duties, they wished to separate him from our Lord, who grants rewards to his children. That man, who was of great value, asked for counsel on what was worth more: to be greedy or to have **patience**, **humility**, and **youth**. Or whether it was better to have a capital <note>original: "upitol"; likely referring to a head or source of wealth</note> even if it was small. According to what the court allows, the Cardinal concluded by departing from that manner of speaking previously mentioned.

They followed what was said regarding the bishop who feared for his faith; and furthermore, because of his youth, they wanted him to look to the bishop for something better: he should have not greed, but rather **patience**, **fortitude**, and **humility** as his signs. 

Afterward, in the singing, the soul became weary, and it manifested itself to someone whose name does not appear to be of the kingdom because they are **apostles**. And the Cardinal passed through the gate toward the sea, where they wanted to test him according to what was said before, so that he could judge in such a way that he might be pleasing to God.

On another occasion, a man who made comparisons asked which thing was more necessary: to raise his son in **good customs** or to leave him an inheritance of **riches**. Which was better: to leave him riches that are badly gained, or to let his son be poor and holy, following the path of the father and praising God in his childhood? While this man was considering this...

<column-break/>

**Column 3**

...others were judged by the mother. A man would rather accept **death** in his **poverty** than that which was said before. Many people are comforted by youth in their sensuality, and then they hear it at the end. The love that does not recognize its own... a son... a scholar from **Rome**. That man had no other son and received no other reward. And for the love of his son, he was in great sorrow.

The Cardinal came to see that man who was in sorrow and said to him: "Amiable son," said the Cardinal, "which is of greater utility: for a man to **praise God** in this world, or to have the wealth that he desires?" The father replied that to have knowledge and high **patience** was better, for patience gives victory to those who suffer in this manner; it is a treasure that gives pleasure without passion.

"My son," said the Cardinal, "I have seen you, and therefore I want to guide you toward having patience." And he explained the ways in which he had moved toward God because he had a son to give back. The other is in patience, for he called it by that name. Even if the son were not dead, a man should increase his learning so that he is prepared to receive the vision of **grace**.

Through the square in **Rome**, there was a rich man who went to sell at a good market...

<column-break/>

**Column 4**

...and spoke many things, leading them to the street. From within the crowd, he went to sit with his peers through the square. There he saw another man who went about in fine clothing, and it was very adorned with colors and other things. That man asked which thing was worth more: a woman who shows herself as a **divine lady**, or one who is plain but acts with **humility**. 

This man had already seen those things through the square mentioned before. Those who followed him asked that the man be honored for the words he spoke. There was no patience or love for the Cardinal there, from the divine... the king was told that they were loved, and he governed them with **justice**. 

On another occasion, one asked which was worth more: to sing or to pray? While he was going through the square, the men who were there said that he should speak the truth. He said that he would not blame them for what they did... in this world... and especially to those who do not blame others... for it is better to be poor than to be prideful.

**Column 1**

It is believed, according to a story, that a brother <note>original: "fratres"; likely a friar or monk</note> who had acquired learning went to the land of the Saracens <note>original: "farrans"; a medieval term for Muslims</note> to prove the **incarnation** and **passion** of the **Son of God**. He returned to a state of cold devotion, singing and returning with his companion toward death, which ended his fears. He desired only to love, and he lived as one who only seeks the welfare of the people. While he served, he met a will that moved him forward with an age that walked toward death; he wished to die either as one who is sick or as a martyr. He fled death when it was appropriate, yet he put himself in danger; he went further than he was told. He fled dying, for in death a man is made more like the vermin <note>original: "uchmet uer meit"; likely referring to the lowliness of the grave</note> that the Son of God took upon Himself in His human nature. While this man walked thus, the fruit that was born of his lineage appeared as an old man who leads one away from the temptation of carnal delight, for the more he hastened, the more he was truthful. In his final deeds, he moved the reason and the force of the contrary will. Or, in the summer, those who are accustomed to delight fear it. Truly, the religious man, because of the delight he had left behind, was in great belief, present at that moment to be content with the Son of God, who in the long lineage must not fail in His covenant. Since he who inquired <unclear>commanded</unclear> before the king, which was worth more: a man of state, or a simple citizen? Then he went to the palace of the Bishop to ask whether it was better to be hidden or to be

<column-break/>

a simple laborer. Afterward, one day, he asked what was worth more: a monk of antiquity? Then he went before the **Pope** <note>original: "lopapa" or "l'apostoli"; the text uses "Apostle" as a title for the Pope, common in medieval Catalan</note> to ask what was worth more: that a man should love his church and always be assigned to renounce the world, or that he should be a friar who preached in truth. And in all these manners, he lived and remained as a companion to his fellows. They noted the great joy he made in the hearts of all the people of devotion, acting and singing with diligence. And the others together:
**When** "You alone are holy" <note>original: "Quoniam tu solus sanctus"; from the Gloria of the Mass</note>. He was blamed for his carnal heart, and he prepared himself by asking if there was any cradle where he might throw himself; and if so, it was like the affection for his cause. Meanwhile, a **Cardinal** entered, who was a man of great authority among the people. The Pope commanded the Cardinal to see if the man had wept much during his sermon. The Cardinal replied that he had seen nothing but many men sleeping while he preached. He said to the Cardinal that it was a great marvel how the people had such sin in their devotion. Regarding the sermon, they were in error: full of masters. The answer was that they should avoid wrath; and the Pope, who had been informed of the state of the Christians, said that the Saracens proved their devotion and their contemplations were consumed by the fear of the **infernal pains** <note>original: "penas ifernals"; the punishments of Hell</note> and the dread of them. The man wept, not with shallow devotion, for it had been announced by a Cardinal that he had been born in merit and not in

<column-break/>

nature, or naturally in such a manner according to the nature of people. Thus he joined more with a man who goes forward. If he was born as a man of lesser state, he moved through the world in abundance. In this manner, one enters into the belief of the converted will. And if the principle is not told truly and considered well, it is because such a judge would fail the Romans, abandoning the place. The **Pope** and the **Cardinals** ordained that holy sermons should be made to guide the people, and that the Gospel should be corrected throughout the world: so that it would be shared year by year, and that it would be understood by the common people—for through ignorance they fall anew into the other denials of faith. These holy sermons were general, and they wept and fought to bring the people to this order. The Pope ordered that devout men should judge everything, just as the plate is filled with different things, so should they be filled with the fear of the **infernal pains** and the **celestial glory**, so that the great message is placed in the memory of the fruitful servants in the **glory of Paradise**. When these things were finished and ordered, the Pope commanded that an official be appointed who would be solitary. The Cardinal, who had heard the man speak, told the terrified people and the officials that they should have pleasure in the infernal pains and the glories of Paradise, and the death of this messenger, which can be found in the book of doctrine for

<column-break/>

the first beloved son. After this, the venerable religious knights, who had many of these things we have named, went forward with great shouting to pray and entreat. And as they prayed according to the will and the spirit, they asked that it should not be taken from them, but rather that it should be strengthened and given to them in devotion. You alone are the Lord.
**When** a message followed from the Cardinal, regarding "You alone are holy" <note>original: "tu solus sanctus"</note>, it was a message to the Cardinal that spoke of the division of **languages**, and the paganism returned. The sermons of the students could not properly awaken the devotion of Paradise in the people, nor the fear of the infernal torments. When the Cardinal received the message, he replied to the Pope so that he might be certain that the sinners and their contemplations were freed from luxury.
**While** the Cardinal represented to the Pope the letters he had sent as his message, there arrived another message from the Cardinal of Continence before the Pope. He recounted that great troubles were made among men by luxury, and the faith was succumbing to all the nations that were divided by **language**. Because of this division of languages, they do not understand one another, and because they are divided by language, they find themselves in conflict and kill one another. The Pope was much troubled by the two messages mentioned above. Therefore, he asked all the Cardinals for their advice on how to destroy this division of faith.

In this way, there are such beautiful tears of love; and weeping so much while hearing him swear by my salvation, and this denial of all my coming into long estimation, and all those things he had said: to govern. **You alone are the most high, Jesus Christ, with the Holy Spirit, in the glory of God the Father. Amen.** <note>original Latin: "Tu solus altissimo ihu xpt cum sco spiritu in gloia dei patris. Amen."</note>

***

When **Pope Blanquerna** was in the mindset that he might bring peace and concord among the communities that are in great discord—because they do not have agreement in their granting of a prince <note>original: "pncep"</note> to hold them in his custody—while the Apostle was in this first desire to love, often with a thirst that he did not want to pass, he prayed where they wanted to take the Evangelists. And they sent letters to the Pope stating that they were in fear <note>original: "paitat"</note> because they did not want to go safely along the roads. And it happened that the Pope, who was in prayer for his own part, had with him their messenger, through whom they wished to go to that city where they were bound to go. When the Pope received the letters, he summoned the Cardinals and heard these words, and saw their prayer within us: that it is our help to endure the glory and excellence of God.

**Column 2**

...which sings in holy glory, it was decreed to assign a Cardinal to oversee this **office**. Wherefore it is a necessary cause to honor messengers to go throughout the world to the Holy Father, and that through our efforts we might go by the virtue of the word of God throughout all the world. And [he desired] that the princes, through us, should not be hindered, so that for the love of God they might be favorable to our efforts. It is a necessity that we send messengers continually to the communities so they can negotiate throughout the lands of **India**, and **Tuscany**, and **Venice** <note>original: "nenessia"</note>, and that one might hold the unity of charity from one community to another.

And he commended this office to the Cardinal who, in his current command, had not yet received an office. The office of this messenger greatly pleased the Cardinal, which the Apostle had given him. And he looked for messengers throughout the provinces and sought out clerics; it was fitting for the **brothers** <note>original: "fraires"; likely friars</note> and other men who would be apt in various languages, and that through this charitable purpose they could go to a Cardinal. 

When the Cardinal had taken these missions, the Apostle sent his messengers on these journeys to this prince so that they might keep the roads safe for those who will be sent by the Apostle. And the Cardinal made spiritual paths, explaining to the churches of other lands that the curias <note>administrative bodies of the church</note> might proceed more safely, and that through the inspiration of souls, people might be brought to concord, and the **Holy Roman Faith** might be preached in the lands of the **heathens** <note>original: "pagans"</note>. And this is so. This was...
