<meta>This page continues the narrative of Blanquerna’s papacy, specifically focusing on his decision to abdicate the papal throne to pursue a life of solitary contemplation. It bridges the administrative duties mentioned previously with his return to the ascetic life.</meta>

to the apostolic dignity <note>original: "apostolici dignant̃"; referring to the office of the Pope</note>: And with greater merit, having renounced being a servant in the great inheritance in which Blanquerna was a participant. Blanquerna responded, feeling that perfect grace had come: the cardinals were full of joy and the grace of serving God. He said to them: "Lords, we wish to renounce this **ornament** <note>referring to the papal crown or the trappings of office</note>." In his desire to pray, he wanted to elect through his clergy the successor. When he had knelt before the Pope <note>referring to himself in the third person or the office he is leaving</note>, weeping and holding them in such grace and affection, he asked for mercy. All the cardinals wished to follow his command.

When Blanquerna was finished with the papacy, it was his daughter <note>likely a metaphor for his soul or his desire</note> to go and serve their God in a **hermitage** <note>original: "ermitana"</note>. The joy and happiness he felt, no one can tell you. While Blanquerna was in a contemplative state in those pleasures, he said these words to the cardinals: "Lords, for a long time I have seen how the service of contemplation of God is found in the life of a hermit. Therefore, let it not be against our hearts that I go to serve God in salvation. I ask that after the Mass, you come to see my hermitage and take leave of me with your blessing, you lords who will certainly remain. All the days of my life I will keep you in our prayers, for you are so beloved to me; I have held the papacy a long time, as long as it pleased God." The cardinals were saddened that he wanted to leave all the burdens of this world,

<column-break/>

and follow that which he perceived to be right, leaving the city of Rome or any other high place where he might be held. And though in that community he could remain in contemplation, nevertheless, Blanquerna did not consent to their pleas, for he was inflamed by divine inspiration. The next morning, after the Mass, he wished to go to his hermitage and take leave of his companions. "Lord," the cardinals said, "we must remain obedient to you; for a long time we have fulfilled your commands. You are useful and great, and you have need of such commands and such a place where you can have physical sustenance, so that you may better work in the contemplative and essential life. Therefore, we pray you to remain among us for a while until we have built you a **hermitage or convent** <note>original: "ermitage conuent"</note>, and that it be prepared in such a way that we can live there and celebrate the divine office. We shall go with your counsel to elect a [new] Pope, and may your help and blessing be with us as we depart, for we remain very sorrowful at our parting." With such great devotion and with such reasonable words, the cardinals begged Blanquerna until he had to agree to their plea. While Blanquerna was with the cardinals, they went through the forests and over the mountains to search for a suitable place in which Blanquerna could stay. On a high mountain where there was an ancient church and hermitage, near a spring that flowed...

...wished to dwell nearby, so that a monastery located at the foot of the mountain could provide for Blanquerna’s needs for the rest of his life. At that time, when the cardinals were gathered together, Blanquerna elected as the Pope <note>original: "apostoli"</note> the Cardinal of Laudanurte <note>This likely refers to a specific historical or fictionalized cardinal; in Llull’s "Art of Election," the process is governed by spiritual logic</note>, who was to be Pope according to the Art <term>The Art: Ramon Llull’s complex system of logic and philosophy used to find truth and make divine decisions</term> by which he elected him, demonstrating its validity to the cardinals. After Blanquerna took leave of the Pope and the cardinals, he set out. Blanquerna went forth to the place he had chosen for his departure. Then the Pope sang a solemn and pious mass, lamenting the departure of Blanquerna, who had acted in the court like a ruling power <note>original: "reingia"; likely comparing his governance to that of a wise queen</note>. He went for penance to the high mountain. And as he was in the company of the dawn and other men of letters, he was not alone, for he was contemplating the God of glory.

**T**he Pope loved the man so much that he spoke of Blanquerna the hermit with such great devotion that the cardinals and the people of Rome who were at the sermon could not refrain from weeping. They mourned Blanquerna because he was departing from them, and especially because they heard how he was intending to torment his own person in solitude with a harsh life. While the Pope stayed and the people wept, the hermit remained...

<column-break/>

...in all the world of Rome it was said that words were sent to the Pope giving certainty to the city of Rome, to those within the walls who remain. And he showed that they must guard themselves, as they are in temptation, and that there is no other contemplation or pleasure like our Lord. And as Blanquerna was very accustomed to God upon leaving the world, he took the hermits of Rome as his examples <note>original: "clagina"; possibly referring to a rule or assembly</note> because he felt he should be with us in the city of Rome, and that if he died in truth, he would prefer his way to worldly vanities. 

The Pope and the cardinals begged Blanquerna to remain, for great good followed him, and they wept especially for the example <note>original: "xiple"; disciple/example</note> he would give to the people. But Blanquerna stood firm and said that as a dweller in a town, he would not be among the people. Taking leave of everyone together, he asked for pardon if he had committed any fault against them, and asked that they might pray to the glorious God for him. When Blanquerna finished his words, the hermit of greater light <note>referring to a spiritual guide or Blanquerna himself in his new role</note> spoke, and the Pope gave him his blessing, embracing him. Blanquerna put on the humble vestments of the hermit’s life, which represented his departure from the world, and he blessed the Pope's hands. Weeping, they commended him to God. The Pope kissed him and commanded that the cardinals follow him to the hermitage where he was to stay, to see if he lacked anything there.

---

The two cardinals who were his friends followed Blanquerna all day until they reached the city wall. Blanquerna begged the cardinals to return, along with all the people. But the cardinals did not wish to stay behind; they went with him to the cell <note>original: "cela"</note> which was to be called his dwelling. In that place, there was a very beautiful fountain and an ancient chapel. There was also a very beautiful cell which he called a place of military service <note>original: "miltar"; likely meaning a spiritual soldiering</note>. There was also a place where a man stayed who served Blanquerna, whom he called "Memory" <note>In Llull’s philosophy, Memory, Understanding, and Will are the three powers of the soul</note>. Blanquerna took joy in his contemplation. That man was one whom Blanquerna loved much and who did not want to leave him; he wished to stay in his company because he loved to do everything for the service of God.

When Blanquerna was in his hermitage, he had all the provisions a hermit needs. The cardinals took their leave of Blanquerna with great sorrow and commended themselves to his prayers, and then returned to Rome. 

From the hour in which Blanquerna remained:
**B**lanquerna, in his hermitage, would rise at midnight. He would go to the window of the cell so that he could see the sky and the stars, and begin his prayer as devoutly as he could, so that his soul was with God and his members were in the great temple of the world. When Blanquerna had contemplated and prayed for a long time until the morning, and the day was coming...

<column-break/>

<margin>How Blanquerna began his life among them</margin> 

...and when the mass was sung, Blanquerna spoke words of God <note>original: "pamulas de dieu"</note> to the servants of God, speaking of God and His works. They wept together out of the great devotion they had for the words they spoke. On the day that he came to these matters, Blanquerna found that glory and eternal joy for his soul from the labor he had sustained; his person enjoyed greater health. And because his soul had such recreation, Blanquerna felt restored. He remained in prayer and contemplation or read in the books of Holy Scripture. In this book of contemplation, he remained until the first hour <term>First Hour: Prime, the first of the canonical hours of prayer, usually at sunrise</term>.

During the day, Blanquerna chose herbs for his food. At noon, he went to eat. After he had eaten, he returned all alone to the church, in which he gave thanks to God. When he had finished his prayer, he went out for an hour to walk by the fountain and in those places where he could best gladden his soul. He walked with his head uncovered so that he might endure the cold of the night. When he had finished his walk and his care, he stayed until the time when Vespers <term>Vespers: The evening prayer service</term> sounded. When the day came that they had said Vespers and Compline <term>Compline: The final prayer service of the day before sleep</term>, Blanquerna returned to his cell.

# The Book of the Lover and the Beloved begins.

**B**lanquerna was in prayer and con-
templating God, and he saw truly
how a man should be who
had contemplated God. Soon there
<unclear>came</unclear> to his mind
new reasons by which to create the way
and the manner to compose the book of the
Lover and the Beloved, and that this way should be
fruitful, and that no one could find fault
nor could they easily complain. Blanquerna
began the book, which he divided into as many days
as there are in a year <note>A traditional year of 365 days, though Llull often used 366 to include leap years, creating a daily mystical meditation</note>. And each day is enough
for all the days to contemplate God, according to that

<column-break/>

**Column 2**

which is in the Book of Contemplation. Let us begin
the heart-matins <note>original: "matias corals"; morning prayers spoken from the heart</note>. 

**T**he Lover asked his Beloved if there
remained in him any other thing
to love. And the Beloved responded that he had
that for which the Lover’s love could increase
and be for loving. The paths are long, which
are made of wild sounds, and long <unclear>perils</unclear>
through the words of <unclear>contemplation</unclear>
and the pleasure that the Lover felt for his Beloved.
For he who gives you to love, loves you;
he who gives you to fear, makes you die. Sing,
little bird, of love, for my soul is 
burdened by those who feel pleasure in
my love. <note>In Llull's mysticism, the 'Lover' (Amic) represents the faithful Christian, and the 'Beloved' (Amat) represents God</note>. The sun was rising
and the world was awakening
and the birds began to sing their
songs, and the Lover <unclear>awoke</unclear>. The Lover
was in the <unclear>garden</unclear> where the bird
was singing, and it seemed to him
that the bird sang of the nature of love, and he saw that
the bird increased the flame in his soul
as it sang. The Lover asked the bird
why it moved him so with its song. The bird
answered that it was to remind him of the life
and the light. The sea <unclear>was full</unclear> and the sun
<unclear>shone</unclear> through the world. The Lover
responded: "You see and run through the sea of love,
not of water, but of the fire of the Beloved."
The sea was the mistress of the Lover,
teaching him how to speak of love. Re-
joice in love and its <unclear>fire</unclear>.

**T**he Beloved asked the Lover if he would prefer joy or pain for his
love. The Lover responded that joy and pain were joined together
<note>In mystical literature, the suffering of longing for God is often seen as inseparable from the joy of divine connection</note> so that they were one in loving, and he did not know
which one was which, for the fire of love
is that which burns and makes one live.

<meta>This page continues the text of the "Book of the Lover and the Beloved," a collection of mystical aphorisms written by the character Blanquerna. The previous page established that the book is organized as a daily devotional, with one meditation for each day of the year to help the soul rise toward God.</meta>

<margin>14</margin>
The Lover asked how to love with patience, if nobility and rank are a burden. **The Lover said** to the Beloved: "You who know how many desires I have, is there anything so great as to love you? For so many things in the world cause me quarrels and hatreds." **The Lover said**: "Why does Love not respond to the one who is lost?" If I were truly a lover, I would first ask for the mercy of the Beloved, for I have been away so long. **The Lover said**: "Why do you go in person among so many people?" Love led me to where I had fallen, and finally, the people remained to weep for the living, while I remain to love my Beloved. The one who loves more, and the one who is above, are both one in the heart of the lover. **The first one said** that he loved, and throughout everything, Love was spoken of in the soul of the Lover. In loving, he responded and said that love is born of the Beloved and the Lover; through all paths and through weeping and joy, the Lover retreats and asks the Beloved how he might protect himself from the world’s vanity. He was busy reading a book when he felt a sudden heat, for Love commanded him to love with all the great powers of the soul. **He said**, "See here, my faithful Lover, since you have seen me, it is written in your book." **The Lover said**, "I will return to my love." **The Lover said** that before he went before his Beloved, he would make a covenant that his Beloved would find him faithful. Love responded to the soul, "Who has brought you to such solitude?" Then, for a reason, Love came to him. **In that place**, the light appeared and he was finished, and the movement of his heart showed him to be a person who, in all his senses, was above the vanities of this world.

<column-break/>

**What** does it mean to cry out for the heart of the Lover? For some know that it is better to know love in one way than to break it. And the memory said that he wept humbly when he threw himself into the flames of love. **The Lover said**: "I understand that I am closer to the Beloved," and Love responded to the lovers and was before the understanding of his Beloved. And it was in the understanding of his love that he was brought further. **Against** two stood the Lover and the Beloved. The other lover had to weep so long until he could see his Beloved. One wept because of the coming of the Beloved, and as a lover, it pleased him when he felt himself love more strongly, seeing that the Beloved was closer to him and his tears were a fountain. **The Lover said**, "Enter the fountain where those who do not love cannot see themselves." And those who are in misery can see the Beloved in that fountain, for above all, the Lover is doubled with the Beloved by Love, linked one to the other. The Lover was the first to begin loving, while the other waited in patience, and with great humility, he saw it. With great wonder, **The Lover asked** of his Beloved, and he responded that there is no other greater nobility than this. And in the world, one does not find such great weeping. **The Lover said**, "In the value of my love, I climb up to my Beloved." **The Lover said**, "I am constrained to remain here or to go." **Or**

<column-break/>

**but** all <unclear>mine</unclear>. The dawn runs to wash itself, and with it, the soul awakens to its Lord. It is a plain song in the dawn, <unclear>the memory</unclear> of so much envy that it comes to the man through the gift of the bird’s song, unless they enter fully into understanding. Do not be afraid, and do not slow down, for Love presents itself. **The Lover said**, "The fountain is where I used to go to dance in the heart of my love." And I saw that he did not want to respond, but wept because he could not see his love. It was not without sense, for he remained in <unclear>contemplation</unclear> with the Lover and the Beloved. The Lover said: "I do not know how to depart, but make me a sign with your eyes that your heart is for my love." And he was in prayer. **The Lover said** to his Beloved, and he wept so much, and the Beloved did not respond to this Lover, because he wanted to recover the life he had lost. And for the love of the one who gives, he asked to be able to love. **The Lover said**, "To love is to be troubled and to find no rest, for the more I love, the more I weep." Nothing else can give me pleasure <unclear>than to return</unclear> to the future of my love for the Beloved. But I forget the world, and in my Beloved, I find great comfort and the strength to be sufficient. **The Lover said**, "I have lost my fear through diligence, and what happened at this loss was that my love entered." And the secret of long loving was that Love gave him liberty. I will go, my Lover, <unclear>to hide</unclear> myself in the mountains and speak of wanting to see my Beloved. I was made for this, and not for anything else.

<column-break/>

<page-num>75</page-num>
I complain so that I may be worthy of the love of the Beloved. My love and my soul... was a trial. **The Lover said**, "Prayer is nothing if it is not a fight for love, with which you command your Beloved." **The Lover said**, responding to the Lover: "You want to love, but you are not yet caught in the net of Love." The bird acts through the power of Love. **The Lover asked** if it seemed a crime to love, and what was the power of Love. **The Lover asked** if he should love another, and he responded that the noble soul is troubled when it sees the Beloved from afar. Love took him, and the Lover wept when he spoke of love, and said that they were one in love, entering into the kingdom of Love. He would rather have the pain of weeping for Love than any other joy. He was closer to Love than if he had been white as a lily. **The Lover asked** why he was so close to glory, and he responded it was because he was in pain. **He said** the truth because he was in a place of rest. He responded that he was lovable and he saw the love of the Lover who was before the Beloved. To the people who loved through life, he asked if a man could find his Beloved. The truth responded that whenever Love was absent from a heart, the person was lost. My Beloved is in me so strongly that he is never absent from the Love of the Lover. **The Lover responded**, and he responded that the Lover cannot be without Love. **The Lover said** that for a long time he wept, looking for the Lover and the Beloved. And with my heart, I trust that I will be filled.

The lover is such because they are of the same nature.

He asked the Lover about all things—he replied: "The poverty that I endure for the sake of loving is my defense against every injury that Love deals." The Lover sang in a place of leaves and flowers, and the wind moved the leaves, and the birds <note>original: "lauzauũ" likely refers to birds praising</note> praised the flowers. The Lover asked if the movement of the leaves or the scent of the flowers signified anything to him. The leaves responded that in their movement they signified obedience, and the scent signified their melancholy <note>In this context, "melancholy" (malenconia) refers to a sacred, spiritual longing for the Divine</note>.

Therefore the Lover asked his Beloved how he might continue with him; for a year he asked with tears, greetings, and embraces. Even if the Lover should die, he would be brought back by the remembrance of his Beloved. The Lover pondered on Love so deeply that he could do nothing but remember his Beloved. "Did it please you, that which he would hold?" Love replied: "So that you do not consider it death or a vile thing to honor my Beloved."

He asked the Lover where his loves would first begin. He replied that they begin in the nobilities of his Beloved, because that beginning includes eternal loving, whereas in other kinds of loving there is only deceit and failure. "Tell me, Sun, if I should love anything else but to love?" He responded and said that he would love because his cause is not one of worldliness, for the branches of Love are many. Love made me come to see every faithful fountain and every conduct. They asked the Friend <note>In Llull’s work, the "Friend" represents the human soul or the devout Christian</note> whose he was. He replied: Love’s. By whom are you loved? By Love. Who engendered you? Love. Where were you born? In Love. Who nourished you? Love. By what do you live? By Love. Where do you lie? In Love. Where do you stay? In Love. To the act <note>original: "aff," likely referring to the "affect" or "act" of the soul</note> of my love. He replied to him, guilty of growth against my loving. I believe, Beloved, why the Friend said that in his loving there was mercy and justice, and he passed in his spirit from fine fear into hope. The Beloved listened to the Friend; so much was the Friend within his own thoughts, asking the affection to take on the language of love. The Friend replied that his loving, which he held in his mind, was in the assurance—and he said to the Beloved that he made a great hymn to the Holy Spirit. The Beloved replied with his own [voice] that he would come to know and enjoy the outcome and the honor. Lovers praised the Friend and multiplied his sorrows—and his secret loving showed him his conduct, and the light of the Friend's soul <note>original: "ambla," likely a variation of "anima" (soul)</note> came to illuminate the chamber of the Friend because he held it in grief, and that [light] gave him more pleasures, and of the languishing and of the hunger. And the Friend cast out from his chamber all things so that his Beloved might always be there. They asked the Friend what sign his loving would make in his spirit so that a man would die of shame for why he made such a sign. He replied: because he turns from death to a new faith—and so that he who kept it, his Beloved, might follow his state. The Friend praised his loving and also his thoughts, that they might bring him from his Beloved the expectation in which the soul reigns for a long time. The Beloved gave benefits to his Friend from many sighs, languishings, thoughts, and sorrows, with which the Friend was beginning his loving. The Friend prayed to his Beloved that He might give him a wide way to praise Him in this world—and the Beloved showed His sufficiency to the memory and the understanding of the Friend and spoke to the will. They asked the Friend in what his thinking consisted. He replied: in understanding how to love his Beloved. And they asked him further in what his throne consisted. He replied: in forgetting his own loving to love his Beloved. <meta>This page continues the mystical and theological exploration of divine love, likely from Matfre Ermengaud’s *Breviari d’Amor* (Breviary of Love). It follows the previous page’s themes of the "Friend" (the soul) in dialogue with "Love" (the Divine). The text uses the conventions of Occitan troubadour poetry—such as the "Friend" and the "Beloved"—to explain complex religious concepts of mercy, suffering, and the nature of the soul's devotion to God.</meta>

# The License of His Loving.
The desire for loving appears in the Friend when he is in tribulations.

### Which thoughts we assume regarding the mercies of the people who love
<margin><image-desc>A manicule (pointing hand) in the left margin points to the start of this paragraph.</image-desc> <unclear>marginal note in cursive script</unclear></margin>
For I already had a little joy and my loving, because at the beginning one cannot [know] their soul, for in pleasure my well-educated lineage does not see its way, nor is there a wise saying from a man who is loved. I have not rightly made the throw, and that part [of me] forgets the great essence of my oblivion, of that which one loves. From you I have it, and if I am loved, let me not have the [sadness] of those loved. One is wise who gives to his [Beloved], as if with his own tears I have the [loyalty] that is in his great and my journey. Everything looked to me, both the birds and the children of that same folly. I am not at all prized by my own [deeds], and from the first when you placed me far from the Friend, because I knew that and what [was] the attachment that dared to [burden] me. The tears that I had for the Friend and the [soul], everything became much stronger. More than a [longing] for the Friend, for I was honored that I am his beloved. [By the] abundance of words, the tongue [speaks] of tears, because from a spiritual effect the Friend is moved to his loving, in that quality which you have of the heat of love. If they do not fail, the others do not [perish] as if they were enemies. Let the cries of the Friend not be separated from my [hope] in the [will], because the Friend and the Love part from you. And the cord [binds] the words; if it were not so, the longing, fear, and tears would be much greater for the Friend in the place of words. The [solace] was of pleasures, and that which was [left] was of the longing in the heart.

<column-break/>

the tears were such that I was made <note>original: "diap," likely meaning "day" or "light"</note> and now they are such that the light and the solace are in the [soul].

# Of all the love for his Friend.
Many times when he sees the ultimate [end] and the first [cause], he loves [suddenly] and goes through tribulations. He does not regard the tears [as] great, for love is great for his Friend, who does not wish to be forgotten by the Friend. He who wishes to hate, let him forget; since he who wishes [can] hate, let him not [be] in the love that he had. <meta>This page continues the spiritual dialogue found in the works of Ramon Llull, where the "Friend" (the human soul) interacts with "Love" (the personification of the divine bond) and the "Beloved" (God). Thus the Friend studies how to mock <note>original: *burlar*, likely meaning to toy with or overcome through spiritual wit</note> and give himself to eternal bliss through his loving. The Friend was asked if pleasure, haste, and <term>languishing</term> <note>original: *llungun ment* (languiment), a central Lullian concept describing the physical and spiritual suffering of the soul pining for God</note> were present there. He found either pleasure or fear <note>original: *rremer*, likely *temer*</note> in equal measure within the essence of the Friend. And for this, the Friend has in time merited a reward and a remedy, so that reigning [over his passions] would not be burdensome to him, focused as he is on one single love. For that love has nourished the soul in its pining. Because even when the passion is forgotten, the soul remains pure; the Queen <note>likely referring to the Virgin Mary or Divine Wisdom</note> returned him to his <term>memory</term> <note>In Lullian philosophy, Memory, Intellect, and Will are the three powers of the soul</term> to contemplate his Beloved. For the soul will love so that it recalls in speech the act of loving, and seeks to understand what it wanted to say, in order to heal the passion of love. The soul stood in the presence of its intelligence in such a way that it asked what the particular cause of its courage was, and the reason for its fear. It was replied that it was because that which was closest to its heart was Love; for the heart is the dwelling place of love. Speak only of truth. He replied many times that he would forget his pining when he ceased to fear love. To love and to possess the vigor of love is to be Love itself. Love pauses there, because it does not enamor itself with many images, but focuses only on the one who loves. I asked if love could be broken. He replied: only through the power of my loving, for with that strength one fights against enemies; with the strength of my loving, I recognize my Beloved through ideal strengths. 

Speak loudly: which do you love more, the <term>mercy</term> of your Beloved or the <term>justice</term> of your Beloved?

<column-break/>

He replied that he loves and fears justice so much that he has no desire to dwell anywhere that does not submit to the divine will. In loving, there is no haste that outweighs justice. Just as the conscience and the will of the soul find themselves blamed, so there is a multiplication of pining in the long duration of loving; and for this reason, the soul feels the weight of errors in its own pretensions. The soul remains firm so that it does not stray from its loving in any part. In the midst of his loving there was no failing; therefore, in this state, which was greater: the affirmation or the negation?

<meta>This page continues Ramon Llull's "Arbre de filosofia d'amor" (Tree of the Philosophy of Love), specifically focusing on the mystical triad of the Lover, the Beloved, and the Act of Loving. Folio 89 (the right page) transitions into the virtues of Wisdom and Fortitude.</meta>

### [Left Page]

<margin>Wisdom <note>original: *Sapientia*</note></margin>
...it was fitting for us to be a positive being so that personal rationality might exist in his **loving-beloved** <note>Llull uses "loving" as a noun here to describe the act of love itself</note>. It pleased the soul, as it loved the friend, that it wished for the Beloved to be more honored through the loss of loving; she turned herself toward the justice of her loving. For she remembered that those who were fitting for him were disobedient, and it was a fault that no one there was greater, or acted with more illegitimate justice toward themselves, as she saw the Beloved suffer, or a greater evil. For she wished to give her guard to the Friend and the Beloved in the hand; the difference and concordance of virtue in the hand; the contrariety of virtue and of vice is not a perfection which is fitting for the philosopher, who feels a lack of alignment in his being, for with misalignment one is not. **S**i it were for the sake of his loving, the Friend wished to bridge the concordance that revealed the plurality of virtue in his loving, primarily to join with extreme distances. **I**t happened to the Friend that if a person who is against being, in that which is against nature, which is against "is not," in another is computed, that is, he institutes a care that would not be in itself, if it is conceived with the corruption of the corrupted. Where it is ready to serve the soul in its loving, cast out and evil. **I**f there were falsehood because the Friend, through evil loving of his Beloved, made an oath because the Friend did not love his Beloved so much, and in this it followed that the definition was of a manner that entered into the Beloved. Here in the Beloved is the concordance of falsehood and lies. **T**he Friend praised his Beloved, saying this of his loving as a lover: possibility and property are known to be

<column-break/>

his loving, having assumed pure actuality in essence, nothing in part. And take note that the Friend in this praised his loving; the friendship toward his Beloved was revealed to him. **T**he Friend was in the number of A [God] and E [created beings] with greatness <note>original: *maie*, likely *magnitudo*, one of Llull's divine "Dignities"</note>: concordance which in another number, to have its own form, was fitting... it is not eternity through the number of friendship. **E** and for this the Friend, in his guard, valued virtue and the fear of his loving for the Beloved and: the concordance of number: half the way, he valued the power, the knowledge, and the will of his loving. For he who loves to create you counts as much in the power of sin, which power would not exist without the will and the knowledge and the power of his loving, to which it would not be a duty to have power, nor will, nor knowledge without the Beloved. **H**e praised his Beloved so much as my creator and he gave me virtue in his love. And he loved the branch, for it pleased him by likeness and nature, and he gave of himself a deed that is worthy of praise: to have a greater perfection. **A**nd then the Friend went from his handle and made it so that the Beloved became a man, to die in human nature or to recreate it. And the mind found itself in various places, responding that in human nature it was not fitting for him to suffer such an evil state. And the Incarnation, to grant the coming of it, responded in humility that the same one who is the lover is also the one who loves. **T**he Friend went to ask the world for a positive place where the love of his Beloved might shine. And he felt that it was of such humility that he acted as if he were a physical body, so that the Beloved would ask for it from the Friend.

### [Right Page]

<page-num>89</page-num>

<margin>Wisdom <note>original: *Sapientia*</note></margin>
...the love of his loving. And the Friend, not so foolishly as is known, but rather the general tendency was held. **I**f there were a form of recovery, the Friend recalled the clear passion <note>the suffering of Christ</note> that his loving sustained for the love of the Beloved. He had a thought and the friend had moved it so that he would have a passion and he comforted himself that the Beloved was his lover: but this was before the trees were green, and the Friend saw that: the vine of life... before the trees were green and flowering. **L**ove said to its Friend that he should love the Beloved: and he felt him to be nearby and shared the divine virtue as it was understood in the first place: that one who was there to be through love. **L**ove asked the people if they had seen its Friend. They praised the qualities of his Friend to him. And the Beloved said that his Friend was a comfort... he was brought to the law of sweetness—he spoke of being through love. And the Friend groaned to fly into the womb: his flesh... because the soul had come to its loving through such a work: so that the world might move there. **S**ay, Fool <note>original: *Digat fol*; Llull's recurring character who speaks mystical truths</note>, and speak for the Beloved: "The weeping goes through the world; the Friend sent his loving to the world." And he went to those places where he could find him. **T**he Friend bathed himself there, and the author where he is of his loving with a renewed love, because of this he spoke...

<margin>Fortitude <note>original: *Fortitudo*</note></margin>
Fortitude responding to his action. **T**he Friend went in his strength of loving. In that loving, to have the joy of this movement of death, ruled by a tempered mind, without lies, so that the world might understand it.

<column-break/>

**S**ay, Fool, that God saves you in this protected earth, as the final understanding and joy, so that my loving might be directed there. **T**he Friend said that the world is narrow, like a path that is returned, it is fitting that he be with his loving, which is a perfect end in great truth and perfection, compared to man who is a finite quantity. And for this, in the justice of his loving, the Friend saw that the virtue of his Friend, known as his lover, was restored and finished. **T**he Friend healed his loving through those who said it was dead, and virtue said that his loving would not have justice if it were finished... to which the soul would not be enough... even if it were alone... it would not be so illuminated in its loving and perfect will—the soul. **S**ay, Fool, in what would it be fitting for the act of love to see justice? And the essence of being human... is in its beauty, so that in the act of love and the infallible condition of the people and of God. **S**ay, Fool, in what does my memory begin? I respond: in the understanding where it is placed, in the beginning of the vanity of the masters of loving. And he calls the gift... with his loving, which illuminates the heart... giving the idea of devotion. **I**t was asked of the Friend: "Which thing is greater: possibility or impossibility?" He said: "Impossibility in the creature, and possibility in his Beloved," for he was confident that through possibility he could know the impossibility of the actuality. **S**ay, Fool, which is greater: the difference...

<summary>This page explores the mystical relationship between the Friend and the Beloved through the lenses of Wisdom, Justice, and Fortitude, emphasizing the Incarnation and the infinite nature of divine love compared to human finiteness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mystical dialogue between the Lover (l'amic) and the Beloved (l'amat), a central theme in Ramon Llull's philosophy. It explores the nature of spiritual solace, the relationship between unity and plurality, and the temporal nature of divine love.</meta>

This loving is of solace and it is in you that my thoughts are set. With such solace—for solace is found in making languishing loves—from my tribulations comes such solace, because those who know it are comforted. If I were not more strongly diverted, I would be a man [united] in himself, for the Beloved loved me. This elevates me or moves me, for the sense of the Beloved <note>original: "mar"; likely a contraction or reference to the Beloved as an ocean of love</note> creates in us a parting that exists only in one way. 

The Lover replied to me and said that mercy and kindness send such solace. The Lover denied it and went into the void of love, conscious in his loving, he who joins thoughts with tribulations. I replied with light and tears and languishing, because the Father <note>original: "p̃r", likely *pare* (father) or *pro* (for)</note> is above, and from the Beloved comes the birth and the lifting up; for your love cherishes you so much that through it, a high heart has been made and has come. And by releasing you, certainly my [thoughts] were cast out. Far from me were the strengths of my loving—for you are not base—through this desire my fault is diminished and healed. 

To go into your greatness makes my overcomings, my thoughts, and my labors joyful. For it is so grave that everything is joyful when you love beneficiently and with pleasure. It was greatness if there was little fear of those things that are against your thoughts and commandments. Finally, he began to recommend himself to me and began to love me—namely, finally he did not begin to count nor did he begin... for I communicate, not as a confidant in his loving, because it is formal and my unity is united. I asked my friend what would be united in such unity in light...

<column-break/>

...my thoughts and my loves, clarity... I love to love... unity, truth, and plurality. And the plurality that is in my loving, you have brought down to unity. Unity and plurality, flowing out, are the good of my loving, which is the fear and the cherishing... for it is not trustworthy for me to praise the severe one who cherishes you. 

If your loving failed me, a sinner, be you merciful and forgiving; and because this is done in us better than in a young girl, your pardon remains in you. Since you make a companion of solace and choose to suffer death for your worth. They say that through loving, my Father <note>original: "p̃r"</note> can save, filling me with mercy and pardon; and he can punish me by justice for the faults of my failings. 

And more so he does by reason, by the will in me, for everything is a completion—whether it be your salvation and help. Justice moved the concern of my heart; and I purified the water <note>original: "aigui"</note> in myself with the ocean of my will. I call out because your truth is sun-like <note>original: "soleriana"</note>. May my will come before your ocean; I would praise you and it would go toward the love of my failings. 

Nothing has been in which my loving was not; that has been done in which my loving is not; and that will fail in which my loving will not be. And for this reason, it is of necessity that all that I was be joined, if not in loving well. And for this reason, he who is in a world where my loving is not, is false, for it does not follow as I have said. Where is the Beloved? He is my light—for if loving weeps, the Lover [weeps]. For the act of loving engenders the Lover.

<meta>This page continues the mystical dialogues of Ramon Llull’s "Book of the Lover and the Beloved." It explores the internal conflict between worldly reputation ("the blame of the people") and the spiritual "passion" required to reach the Beloved. It also introduces the scholarly disciplines—Philosophy, Medicine, and Law—placing Theology above them in the hierarchy of the Lover's journey.</meta>

...to this, the Beloved—that is, remembering the passion that I sustain for the love of my Beloved. And there is no other reward <note>original: "luyer"; reward or merit</note> than to go among the faithful who invoke him. For the Lover, flowers burn <note>The OCR "cremēt" suggests burning, a common metaphor for the heat of desire</note>, remembering the shame and my avarice and envy, and the ungrateful, arrogant ones. Taught by my sorrows, I held in love those temporary joys, distancing myself from this world... in the enlightenment of the understanding, perceiving the things of God, leaving this world of tensions and durable nourishments. To those who are in the sight of the judges of this world, these things are pleasing. My Beloved was calumnied; poverty and tribulation were considered a misfortune, and would have been his end if he had not been devoted to this love. He went with hope and remembrance, with the loyalty of love, into this world; and there, through the blame of the people, between labor and pleasure, he was in the place where he lived with pleasure in the divine, also in total peace; and he approached the place and the Beloved. In his divine anger <note>or "divinity"; the text is ambiguous</note>, the Lover held the blame of the people and their contempt as his own, for he remembered the heart of his Beloved, and nothing else except the Lover, who had to have joy either from his Beloved or from the people. And the Lover turned toward Love, and had the purpose of remembering the truth of his Beloved, from which many deaths are portrayed internally. The Lover was commanded by his Love to reproach and judge the world, and to labor under this thought; and Love gave him relief, so that tempering his vision, he would see the day...

<column-break/>

...with remembrance, remembering that God must be loved for his noble conduct. The Lover takes a joy—that is, a joy that is much loved—seeking to console everything that his Beloved would be to him; for nothing is more profitable than the joy found in life, or the grace that comes to work in this world. The Lover slept, constant in the labors and appearances which accuse his Love; for if these works were not performed, they could not pass through those appearances. But he loved him internally, and that is the thing which he despised: lying in the judgments of his Beloved. Love does not want long orations and diverse paths <note>Llull often emphasized that simple, intense love is superior to complex ritual</note>; it fears that the path is sin and that it can bring nothing but Love itself, to be shared among the lovers. And thus the Lover went to lighten the soul of earthly thoughts and pleasures, so that the heart could look more clearly, and the soul could speak intimately with its Beloved. The Lover was giving—that is, giving all the truth of his Beloved—without the Lover dying or losing his Love. For who is more to blame: the people who blaspheme either Love or the Lover, or the one who goes in that state toward his Beloved? The Lover contemplates his Love and its subtlety in his understanding, in his love, and in his will. And this is how, through which of these two substances, he remembers his Love most strongly. With fervor and fear, the Lover went in his image to seek his Beloved; fervor drew him near, fear confirmed him, while he lived in his remembrance.

<column-break/>

At every hour, he sighed and wept, so that his soul would not greet anything of his Love except that which comes from the totality of his solace. The Lover went to bind himself <note>original: "Legar"; perhaps "to read" (Llegir) or "to bind" (Lligar)</note> so that his eyes would see his Love, and he looked at his Love so that his eyes would have knowledge of it; and he held himself to that which was of the divine, so that his understanding would be more accustomed to it. Regarding praise, and philosophy, medicine, and law <note>The "three faculties" of medieval university study</note>: the Lover encountered them and they asked him if he had seen his Love. **Theology wept**, Philosophy was wise, Medicine and Law were pleading <note>or "alleging"</note>; and these are the signs of truth. When the Lover signifies what his Love does, with anguish and tears the Lover goes to seek his Love, and he is more felt in the flesh and in the external world. Here is the question: in which of these two ways was he first mindful of his Beloved, or in which does Love show itself more clearly to the Lover? On the day of judgment, the Beloved said: "I have given all that I had to this world; to the poor has been given that which they adored in the world, so that they may be united with the heart and with the Love." In which of these is the heart conquered more nobly and with greater quality? The will of the Lover was turned, and the understanding commanded whether it were more similar to his Love to love himself or to love his Beloved. Since the cause is that Love is more loving of itself, this is just...

<column-break/>

...the will returns to the understanding to be enlightened, and asks which is the friendship and the most noble love that can exist in a creature. The answer is that which is established toward God, because the Creator has not made a more noble creature than this. And one night—that is, a day of meditation—those who were better at praising were those who wept, or those who made great contemplation and thought, or those who went about simply. The Lover went into his understanding, for many people are influenced by the vision of the things said about his Love; and for this reason, they are better in weeping and in the path they follow. The Lover was confident in his Love, wanting to be on the path where many people and companies were; they asked the Lover why he did not take pleasure in his Love, and he did not respond to what they asked. For he wished that nothing of his Love should be told to them, as he did not want to respond to their words. The Lover was above and below; and where was Love? He went to seek his Love, and there, that is, Love was everywhere; and he asked for help to love more. "Tell me, what is religion?" The response was: "To withdraw and define the world, to promote Love, and to love the soul so that we do not fall into the depth of its fall." Tell me, what are labors, complaints, sighs, and pleasures for the Lover? The answer: "The pleasure of loving." Why? So that he may love more, and the Lover will be more honored before the Beloved. In what state is the Lover?

<summary>This page explores the mystical tension between worldly reputation and divine devotion, using the "three powers of the soul" (Memory, Understanding, and Will) to navigate the Lover's journey toward the Beloved. It also contrasts Theology, Philosophy, Medicine, and Law, identifying Theology’s "tears" as a more truthful sign of the Beloved than the logic of other disciplines.</summary>

It explicitly mentions the book's structure of 365 daily parts intended to guide the reader through meditations on the divine essence and the soul’s journey.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the spiritual journey of Blanquerna, specifically focusing on his practice of mystical contemplation. Having established his "Book of the Lover and the Beloved" in the previous pages, he now engages in the internal process of aligning his soul's faculties—Memory, Understanding, and Will—toward the Divine.</meta>

...being in great doubt. And that which
fails and is without a firm foundation. And to
see and help in the glorious visual presence. My soul,
emptying the senses and the presence in 
that supreme queen. **W**hile Blanquerna 
stood contemplating, his heart began 
to be warmed and his eyes began 
to weep because of the great pleasure he had in re-
newing virtue, remembering and understanding 
[and] loving the Sovereign Goodness <note>Original: "soberana honeta." This refers to the supreme moral beauty and integrity of God.</note>. Thus it happened 
that Blanquerna, joyfully weeping and purely 
lifting his understanding to the imagi-
native power <note>The "imaginative power" was considered a specific faculty of the mind that processed sensory images for the intellect.</note>, such as it was presented 
as a guide as he wished, so that he 
might have the glory of God. 
And having emptied his senses, and through the practice 
of the understanding, and the imagination 
and the desire restricted in the air and the heat, 
the tears in his eyes decreased. 
And Blanquerna restored his understanding and his 
imagination and rose above that memory 
of the supreme beauty in perfect 
wholeness; and he passed through having been a helper of 
glory, and presenting the strength and the help 
with much wealth, just as he had plowed so that the mind 
was bare; and the world was not there for that reason, nor did it fail, 
for there was no one who could enjoy the sovereign light or 
receive the glory that flows from the 
virtues previously mentioned. **I**t greatly pleased the 
Beloved that Blanquerna had used his 
understanding to brush aside the imagination 
which hindered his understanding. And to 
see higher in understanding, he made the imagination 
humble before God, who is known 
above in great bounty. In ascending 
toward the light, he followed that which 
failed in the intermediate mind, and the 
virtuous union was in the Sovereign Goodness. And because

<column-break/>

it is impossible that any failure be in God, 
for that reason he was silenced; and it made the heart 
of Blanquerna so strong that his eyes 
were entirely drowned in weeping. **W**hile 
Blanquerna was contemplating and weep-
ing, in his soul were brought 
mentally his Memory, his Under-
standing, and his Will. And they understood 
the things of the virtues of God, according to 
what these words signify: "O, return 
then, Understanding, to that which is remembered; 
it does not befit you, heart, for the holiness and 
the love of God; and you, Will, who command 
it through the Memory, moving ever forward 
in the mind, keeping the mind loving and in great 
wisdom and health, and in a noble manner, 
for no virtue is so joyful 
as this: to remember the 
Sovereign Goodness and to be united in 
the Will." And God gives virtue to virtue, power to 
virtue, and unity to all. "Then I feel a 
great desire to be chastened by remembering 
these virtues." And these are many 
virtues. Memory spoke to Under-
standing, and the Will spoke in a 
similar manner, saying that in a science 
so high and so great, one should not 
lack the strength; and because it is first 
known and then loved, loving that which 
is done as it should be. "Therefore, I love and see 
the Sovereign One because He loves holiness, 
and is unique and infinite." After these 
words, Understanding spoke to Memory 
and to the Will, that they should remain in a 
similar state; and Memory and 
Will in contemplating the absolute. 
Then Memory and Will said that, 
contemplating the Sovereign Goodness, they make

<column-break/>

a joyful and glorious help. And searching for 
the union with God, infinite and unified, 
in truth and glory. And the Understanding understands 
that which the Memory remembers, and the Will 
loves that which the Memory is remembering. And the 
Will loves that which the Memory remem-
bered and that which the Understanding understood. 
Another time it returned to remembering 
the virtue of the Ruler, and to helping in the memory 
of the virtue of the Ruler, and in the infinite help of the 
highest virtues in virtue and glorious union. And 
the Understanding understood the glory and the memory 
existing in virtue and helping the sovereign 
glorious glory. And the Will loves all 
the deeds in Him, and actually in Him, and per-
fectly. **B**lanquerna asked the Un-
derstanding: "If the Sovereign One were to save me, 
what would you understand of Him?" And the Under-
standing [replied]: "To understand the bounty 
and the emptiness and the largess of God." 
"And you, Memory, if the Sovereign One were to take me, 
what would you remember?" "I would remember the 
presence, the light, the perfection, 
and the power of God." 
"And you, Will, what would you love?" "I would 
love that which the Memory remembers, 
provided that it be in a place where I can love it, 
for the virtues that are in the Sovereign One 
are lovable in themselves." **A**fter these 
words, Blanquerna returned to his prayers. 
And he entered with joy and perfect 
extension. For if there were no presence in God, 
immediately he would become a shadow 
of this world. And for that reason, God and 
His Will, and the mind [must] recognize the 
presence of God, which has sustained him. 
The Will rejoices that it would love 
the Sovereign One; and it would be that the 
false things that the Understanding took 
as true would be judged. For this reason, 

<column-break/>

Lord, it pleased Blanquerna that the 
Will had replied. And he praised Blanquerna 
and promised him all the divine virtues 
as a gift from God. **I**n this manner, 
Blanquerna contemplated the virtues of God so that 
he might be grateful to God. And he was 
emptied of that which he had directed in his 
contemplation and presence. And because he wished 
to be cleansed and filled, he wished to give 
merit to that which he loved; 
for the presence of God does not love him 
contemplating as highly as the [...] 
united. For that reason, he saw the contemplation 
with the guarding of the three [faculties], according to 
what was said. And for that reason, he knew the truth 
of the Ruler in the contemplation. And it is said that he 
was approaching and contemplating the presence of God 
in this. He had returned to the Sovereign 
Goodness and to the reason and the other virtues 
without any difference <note>Llull often emphasizes that while humans see different attributes in God (like Justice or Mercy), in God's essence, they are all one and the same.</note>. And for that, the Under-
standing was used to his mind and his 
perfected deed. And he had returned to 
the holy virtue. And thus the Memory 
remembered that in God the virtues have 
no difference from one another. 
Just as the works of God are 
divine. And not just as one speaks 
of the joined virtue when one looks at the 
[...] and the Ruler. And the virtue 
is one in each virtue without any 
difference. In what manner does Blanquerna 
contemplate God from month to month in His virtues? 
**B**lanquerna, you are great and infinite, 
true and joined; you contain within yourself 
the virtues of great truth, which are glory 
and principle. And in the virtue of truth [...] 
[...] between that which you are [...] 
[...] union [...] 

<summary>Blanquerna engages in a deep mystical contemplation where he coordinates his Memory, Understanding, and Will to focus on God's divine attributes. He concludes that in the divine essence, all virtues such as goodness, power, and truth are perfectly unified and identical.</summary>
<keywords>Blanquerna, contemplation, Memory, Understanding, Will, Divine Virtues, Sovereign Goodness, mysticism, unity of God, Ramon Llull</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the spiritual exercises of the monk Blanquerna. Following his retreat to the forest, he focuses here on the "Three Faculties of the Soul"—Memory, Understanding, and Will—which were central to Ramon Llull’s philosophy. The text explores how these faculties must be unified to perceive the "Beloved" (God). There is a particular emphasis on the concept of "Volentat" (Will) and "Amor" (Love) as the driving forces of mystical union.</meta>

-> [Left Page] <-

Whenever our understanding [reaches] your kingdom
to be built from the passion of love, our [worth]
is your gift, providing as much as is fitting
in your praise, to be together with you,
so that the spirit may be set free by love.
Born in your power, which cannot easily
be measured, nor understood, nor loved. To you,
I ask that you make my will <term>uoler</term> <note>Llull uses "uoler" (will/desire) as the active faculty that drives the soul toward God.</note> greatly fitting
in your remembering, understanding, and loving;
your power, which for being infinite, shall be
such an influence that it is the cause
made great in the womb, from which may be great
harmonies in the soul. And invited that
your power might jump without end, to be like
hunger: in itself creating a way to divine
without end; creating a power that saves
in anyone. That which God is, in others
he creates: in the soul he is sufficiently seen as a savior,
to see and to empower, for in a creature he does not look
at reason nor any movement. Nor is it his
will to do that which he judges. For my
power to do has a beginning: grant that
to do what pleases you, whenever
my will and my divine knowledge
are known to the sight, and that it comes to the sight.
**P**ower of love, seeking all your truth.
Falsehoods that come, seek to lie to you.
Desire that your will <term>uolentat</term> might all merit you;
false: for my will is more when it has entered
to reach reason in us.
You, through your known will, are beforehand
in the arrangement. Being less, being
nothing less. And we, being false, fail you
in having your worth. And our wanting does not
want your power or will. In wanting
to speak the truth. If your "Yes" is in the soul,
nothing else can move
the memory. For your so great in-

<column-break/>

-fluence of beauty, as much as the soul
can perceive, so that everything responds and can act
in choosing itself, in honoring your sa-
-cred power and my weak failing in ignoring
your knowledge; and my will in thinking of
another: Its truth originates in you and is
your essence: we falsely place ourselves
in a path without direction to your
will, [which] shall be another honor—worthier
than all other falsehoods. And if the man
should fail in knowing my will and in deceiving
himself in loving a living will: it is necessary that
knowledge knows living love in loving in [will],
so that the friend might love in loving. And if
we do not fail in this, one will know living
truth... in love and in
will, so that the friend might see the friend's wisdom,
nor will the will fail to be one with the
frequent desire to contemplate your
mind, so as not to lie; for Blanquerna <note>Original: "Blauena." A common scribal shorthand or error for Blanquerna, the protagonist.</note>, contem-
-plating within himself, entered into such a
frenzy <note>Original: "rabia." In a mystical context, this refers to a divine madness or intense spiritual fervor.</note> that his will began to prize, but could
not join your hunger. Through your beauty,
he understood that whoever would love God
needs to join with the desire to hunger
and thirst. And since Blanquerna wept, his
tongue [was] silent, as he remembered he was guilty
of others: in the response that he who loves shall be. Love
originates in your power and is the true path to
truth, which joins—for if the union is true,
which the fool <note>Likely a reference to the "Holy Fool" or the character of the Lover in Llull's "Book of the Lover and the Beloved."</note> in humanity
found, then the knowledge will be much more certain,
as it is the union that you have in loving.
For if differences are in the fool, it is to
please: without the strength to be warm—
to come to the union of love, union which
has no power of understanding: for when-
-ever living love comes in union, it shall be.

-> [Right Page] <-

What their souls do when they love and want,
and if their life remains in tranquility. And since
yours is known in itself, may my love
come to join the truth. If now the remains
are yours, your worth and power: or
how to love your faithful ones toward your truth,
to join in one virtue, for one virtue is
divided among us: For it is hard to join that which is
born or the hidden thought with the people.
For contemplating his loving, being
alone, and in that retreat, you are famous for
joining and seeing clearly in his thought and his
will. Thus it is that we do not under-
-stand any difference because in loving
everything is light: with clear understanding
the will [is] his loving, as a friend is joined,
who goes about preparing for your union
in a manner: judging for your guide in that
which would be this glory in com-
-ing to the union that no rest shall cease
to give glory to anything. **W**hen Blanquerna
saw the beauty of the truth, it made him love a
stranger <note>Original: "forenne." This often refers to God as the "Other" or the "Transcendental Stranger" in mystical prose.</note> who presented himself, which was true,
which was, is, and shall be; this understanding
sought you, making an offering, and the
truth of the virtue was seen, nothing was hidden from
love, in which he saw no creature without an end.
Blanquerna said: "It is in your union that there was
glory and truth; the memory was outside of things
and if it were not for yours, which was glory and truth,
the will would be made error; for what is
not hidden is the failure of things."
Blanquerna changed color through the dif-
-ference of beauty and light and contrast,
trembling and growing. Considering that it might be perfect,
not justice or reason, to love in him through true
essence or commandment. Conser-
-ving in praising, and not understanding the dif-

<column-break/>

-ference of the will: considering that there would be
understanding through perfection, feeling that which is in virtue,
not understanding through perfection equally.
Blanquerna wanted to see truth, considering
nothing in himself nor in others, in the conside-
-ration of his understanding and in his will,
standing to contemplate his loving. [Words] of the friars
and of your intellects, weeping and praying and fearing
the justice of his loving. In Blanquerna,
it was so high as to sharpen and refine the memory,
understanding, and will—lamenting,
he wanted to go through the sea, through the depths, through perfection—of under-
-standing and to understand and to come—the will through
largesse <note>Original: "largueza." This refers to God's infinite generosity and the soul's expansive response.</note>. In none of these [faculties]—could the passion
overcome the other's perfection, for one
serves the memory, the will—union—his loving
was fixed so as to join—as one heart,
so he loved in truth as he had seen
completely: re-established... through the
memory through the veins of truth, running to hold
the understanding in truth, his eyes wept, yearning
that it were pure to come to these afflictions: and from
largesse, those who ask for it want to fly. And
through the understanding, largesse sees the
truth of loving, and he joins and loves
to pass the sweetness of your world—in his
soul which remembers, and God is between
fearing—reason through the will and my will
brings the memory to lead: and the
understanding to understand if one is near:
memory gives itself to Blanquerna,
fearing to contemplate that which joins
them in love, nor in wanting. **T**hey
look—as Blanquerna contemplated in his
love's largesse—my humility reveals
the ways and the most noble [things], praying to the
contemplated one in himself. And before the
Lord, in lying that in loving he could not.

<summary>This page details the mystical meditations of Blanquerna as he attempts to unify his Memory, Understanding, and Will to achieve a direct experience of God's "largesse" and truth. He experiences intense spiritual emotions (weeping, "frenzy") as he realizes that true love of the Beloved requires a total alignment of these internal faculties.</summary>
<keywords>Blanquerna, Ramon Llull, three faculties, Memory, Understanding, Will, mystical union, largesse, divine love, contemplation</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the contemplative discourse from the previous folio, moving from the internal faculties of the soul (Memory, Understanding, Will) to a deeper ontological investigation of the Divine Essence. The speaker, likely the character Blanquerna, is exploring the "Unity" of God—a core tenet of Ramon Llull’s philosophy—where God's attributes like Power and Will are not separate parts but are identical to His very being.</meta>

...to write, nor are they [distinct] things to be placed. For if there were no difference in the end where the knowledge of the soul resides, then it would not be the will; for I find in your greatness how it is. And therefore, since the nobility of your being is an eternal essence of the highest degree, in this same thing I will place my [thought].
<gloss>It is not possible for you to have failure, nor a lack of essence.</gloss>
If it were possible to find in you any such weakness, it would not be a supreme greatness. Toward which, then, all the joy of my hand is directed: in adoring and contemplating the unity and the glorious essence in nature, which endures with power, knowledge, and will.
As the power is seen, so is the knowledge and the will. And therefore, the essence would not be perfect if it were not for the power, the knowing, and the will. And because in the greatness of your being there is no difference between your power, knowledge, and will, there is no difference in your being or in your will.
Therefore, those essences that are there, between your power, knowledge, and will, there is no difference at all. Sixth: let this be the antecedent. Because you are supreme, for everything else is subject to the knowledge and will of this same essence. With your essence, you are to be loved; you who are the primary person <note>Llull often refers to the "Dignities" or attributes of God as active "principals" or "persons" in a philosophical sense.</note>, and there is no lack of sufficiency in that essence. For in that essence, there is no reason for any failure to exist. For my power and my effort can act against my being—which is a failure—and therefore, because [in me] the cause is different from my being, it is one essence different from my power.
My power is like a branch, and my essence is another essence. But in you, because there is no difference between your power and your essence, nothing can be found there that is not essence itself.

<column-break/>

And by this, one can finish and conclude that power and being are a unity which is perfected in Him. It is called "humanity" when it is with a man, and it is called "chivalry" <term>cauallaria</term> <note>Llull frequently used the concept of Chivalry as an analogy for spiritual service and the inherent "virtue" of a state of being.</note> when it is in a knight. From being God, He is called God. And for this reason, God says of your essence: because your unity and your doing are one, your being and your identity are held in perfection. Entering into this same essence when it is in all your being—this is the truth. In this being, there is no other [state]. Therefore, many people and many knights, for this reason, are shifted in their souls when they think of your unity and the union of chivalry. Just as this letter of your essence is not [merely] known in its glory, but in its unity, it is joined without any difference of thing or effect. Knowledge is not in the miserly.
And the power and the Lord are things that have been seen in other gods, as is the power of man.
<margin>I am not yet a man, nor have I been judged.</margin>
Humanity is not a thing that can be [fully] seen. Now, in our justice, we feel that in us there is nothing of justice except for the reward. For in joining with man, I cannot feel the essence of human nature. In joining together, one cannot be just without justice being prepared. In joining with man, one cannot be without the elements from which man is made, by fortune or accident. But in your justice, your essence would not be equal to a just man, if it did not have the same quality of being. You are Justice itself, a necessity of that path which God made. And in this world, there could not be a finite and perfect thing of essence, or any other thing above that which is reasoned here.

<summary>This page explores the Lullian concept of Divine Unity, arguing that God's attributes—Power, Knowledge, and Will—are identical to His Essence, unlike in humans where these qualities are separate and fallible. It uses analogies of "Humanity" and "Chivalry" to explain how an essence defines a being.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative and theological meditations of Blanquerna. ...for a holy will [is] that we men should be helped to love one God alone. For if there were many gods, according to perfection and justice, He would [not] forgive and save, nor would He wish to give God such great favor; for all creatures are loved by man <note>The argument here is that a plurality of gods would divide man's loyalty and diminish the perfection of divine justice.</note>. Of unity, Blanquerna <note>The text reads "blaquina," a common variant for Blanquerna, the protagonist of Ramon Llull’s work.</note> [was] prayerfully full of the virtue of God, according to the nature he had previously described. His memory, his understanding, and his will ascended very high. Man is given a will that wants to have all for itself alone, because it wants to possess its own kingdom, or health, or its wife; and if he [man] also hides his memory, his understanding, and his will from others, he seeks to improve himself in these things through a passion that covets glory and eternity. And when Blanquerna remembered these things, he remembered the glory and the eternity of God, understanding that if there were many gods, their glory or their eternity would not be as great as it is being one God alone. For it is fitting for God to be known and loved: glory and eternity. By this, Blanquerna’s understanding sought to show that there is one God alone. And because the understanding perceived nothing more, his will was more inclined to serve: to contemplate his love and the hope of his will with these words: "O Unity and Eternity: God, what other god is there? None but You alone. You alone are my master—let it be that You alone are perfect—for in Your bounty there is nothing that [is not mine?], but only the way. One thousand [times] I humble myself—to serve You—to have mercy on our enemies, if I can only pass through..."

...that in which I am, or that which I am with, so that His love alone [guides] me. You alone are without sin and [I am] guilty. You alone remain perfect in us, and through You I hold... [if] we are not to labor... we fear You alone. You alone [are] with glory—through You and for You I hold love—I do not wish to be without You.

</margin>

...to be illuminated according to His will. For this reason, the contemplative spirit, ready for perception, feels that nothing follows against God. While Blanquerna <note>The protagonist of Ramon Llull’s 13th-century novel, representing the ideal Christian life.</note> was contemplating in this way, the Friend <note>In Llull’s "Book of the Friend and the Beloved," the Friend is the soul/devotee, and the Beloved is God.</note> asked the understanding if there would not be a movement of the will in loving Blanquerna, given that he was so moved by God; it was as if loving Blanquerna meant completely imitating his union [with the Divine]. And so Blanquerna remembered a similar likeness that existed partly in perfection and nature, as it ought to be understood: just as through the light he is joined—for he does not feel it through himself as one who wills—and according to the illumination, the will is given the power to be a "willer" <term>voleti: a technical term in Llull's philosophy referring to the active power of the will</term>. For in this way, just as in the Holy Trinity there is a Lover, an Understanding, and a Being, there is a joined Willer. In this way he conceived that the soul was to be set on fire. For if the unity of God is in itself inflamed with sovereign love, then it is a Unity Beloved. Thus he conceived that the Sovereign should not be limited, but should be set on fire in that infinity where God and unity are one, so that man does not tempt <unclear>m'o ma i o</unclear>; for His greatness is no less. My understanding, in that it perceives His essence, perceives a greatness that was never made, because His greatness is infinite and also a perfection of love. And my understanding and my sight <unclear>con uis</unclear> perceive His greatness in itself, and in seeking it, they find something greater than they can grasp through love. This was not always so; for it is certain that love began <unclear>m're if ab ino</unclear>. And so it was, for it is certain that I was less than the joint understanding that was placed in me; and these are the children of <unclear>ito</unclear> who are deprived of the light, so that there does not follow a reduction of the understanding. Perfection remains in this love—certainly in itself.

In this way, one must see the works and enter with reverence into that which the Divinity has sounded. If one looks into the divine truth as a communion of the divine persons, then the person of the divinity is that which <unclear>i'se'rar</unclear> the divine person.

Whereupon Blanquerna, through his love, presented himself to God, so that he might render a conceived and flowing understanding of the great glory of the Creator, placed therein so that grace might love...

<column-break/>

...now understands. And so they state the sentence, along with Saint Gregory <note>Saint Gregory the Great (c. 540–604), whose works on contemplation and the "Life of Benedict" were standard medieval texts.</note>, regarding the part which concerns the divine persons: it was agreed that God took on human nature, in which and through which alone is signified the flow of the divine and His works. Through these divine persons and through this sign, the will of Blanquerna was set on fire to love God and his holy Passion. Blanquerna said these words: "O my Beloved God <note>original: "O n'ma am'at d'u'"</note>, Blanquerna is not infinite, yet he is within Your greatness and Your truth, because he must love by nature. Through the Passion, the soul is set on fire in greatness and divinity, through which the soul is most dearly loved. Through greatness and truth, the soul finds a perfect love; for it cannot be that this love would fail to receive greatness. For every soul remains finite until it finds the way. Through the power of truth, the soul is set on fire; no cause can beat down this suffering servant <note>original: "pamulu[m]" (servant/slave)</note> when the work begins without an end. For truth is made known here <unclear>bit' o i i n'as</unclear>, otherwise it would not be loved. For it is more dear and greater and more virtuous than all other souls and created things. And the Son of God wished to be a Person with that beloved water, signifying the way of youth, being able to give a greater death <note>likely referring to the death of the self/ego in mystical union</note> than other creatures. Thus he was able to be greater than any other created being. No other soul has been loved more, for he loved with a perfection that surpassed all other ladies <note>Llull often uses the language of courtly love/troubadours to describe divine love</note>. Through the Passion, he wished to give more...

And so the way was hinted at, that that soul had a created nature. Because this is so, it is converted so that the Creator, the angels, and all other souls are delighted by it. Thus the soul does not lack grace in the natural life, and it suffers so that it can love more greatly than it could in its [original] nature.

<summary>This page features a mystical and philosophical meditation on the Passion of Christ, viewed through the internal dialogue of the soul's faculties—Memory, Understanding, and Will—following the character Blaquerna. It explores the relationship between finite human devotion and the infinite "Dignities" (divine attributes) of God, though much of the text on the second leaf is obscured by damage.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the theological meditation on the Passion of Christ and the internal operations of the soul. It focuses specifically on how the "Act"—a key concept in this philosophy—of God's grace and justice operates through the suffering of Jesus. The text maintains the previous page's focus on the soul's powers (Memory, Understanding, and Will) but shifts toward the concept of "perfection" and the "mental blasphemy" that occurs when one fails to appreciate divine grace.</meta>

This sign is the greatest of the sovereign divinity sacrificed; just as this omnipotent wisdom <term>Wisdom (Sabieza): In this context, one of the "Dignities" of God, an essential attribute that acts upon the world.</term> is made in the union of absolution: He alone, in his bodily members, understands how he owed as a sacrificing virtue—a virtue he did not offer merely according to his eminent devotion, nor did it diminish the estimation of the action and the greatness of justice for itself: even in imitating effort. 

And the more he was in the act of virtue, the more fitting it was that the will of the blessing was pronounced from below. <summary>This text provides spiritual guidance for a soul suffering from intrusive, "blasphemous" thoughts, explaining that such torments are not sins if the Will does not consent. It emphasizes that God sustains the soul through "poverty" and "consolation," distinguishing between the involuntary Imagination and the intentional Understanding.</summary>
<keywords>soul, understanding, memory, will, imagination, mercy, patience, consolation, dread, actual love, spiritual poverty, mental blasphemy</keywords>
