# Page 106

...moral meditation. Again I ask you that we recognize how to love humility within our hearts: to love [instead of] gluttony, envy, and the other vices. And as I have set out all these things to love, I can now return to myself by divine necessity. So that you do not live in vain, I ask you for those gifts which are consistent with our first intentions.
**My** perfection can be prayed for, as it has been spread out through love. Through your limits, I can lose the power of having spread out songs for anything that is like gluttony or lust. Regarding temperance, I ask you to give us license to call upon those who are sick, to see and mend our understanding and to love our first intentions. We must mend our understanding to love our faults and the divine virtues of this world. Pity and sighs blame my lies, for these are the commands of God. He gave what was suitable. In crying, he blamed the grudges of injustice. Contemplation is not what is blamed for loving, but the art and the manner of loving; there is no one who can say or signify it, except only—
**Bless God.** Of the end of the book. Chapter 15.
**H**ere begins the book of the Art of Contemplation. This is the manner of Blaquerna contemplating God; he finishes the book of contemplation in the spirit of glory, with a great weeping that signifies in its beginning that he has a desire for a better life. And Blaquerna, having reached this state, felt himself a human sinner in the world of malice, for which his conscience and contrition made him realize how much he owed to God's providence.

<column-break/>

...they have come. But since you are the first remedy, Blaquerna would love. And the minstrel <term>minstrel</term> <note>original: "loglar." In this context, it refers to a "Joglar of Valor," a wandering performer who sings spiritual songs instead of secular ones.</note>, who was of a certain manner, said that he was a minstrel. Blaquerna rejoiced at this, for he wanted the minstrel's light to be turned toward seeing and praising God. He asked pardon for the folly of this solitary man who labored and tormented himself in his God. At this time, it happened that since no man had the final intention for which the sciences were begun at their start—for the beginning of the clergy was to end the soul’s wandering by first intending [God]—he then turned to follow the knights, the citizens, the physicians, the merchants, the religious orders, and all the diverse nations of the world. And as he contemplated these things, and seeing that there was not much continuity in their understanding of why the sciences were established, he went through the world as a minstrel, to love and obey those who are obligated to love, and to not be willing to serve God [only] in name. And through this art, he gave you this task: that you go through the world spreading the love of the sciences to others, teaching the intention for which the minstrelsy and the other sciences were established in the beginning. This romance is about Blaquerna, in which are signified the visions through which those mentioned above were found. He sought to stay in that place where he saw the hermit, and although he was tormented, Blaquerna labored much so that he might be pleasing to God. The minstrel praised Blaquerna and recovered his light.

---

# Page 107

...which he began. He received a full penance because in the theology of the religious life, by nature, piety, and scholarly judgment, there was philosophy and the arts.
**H**aving a likeness to this, and seeing those who do not find the final intention pleasant—through which the aforementioned causes have been rolled out—he chose to dwell in the mountains with the monks of the desert and with Blaquerna, so that he might triple his devotion and gain greater strength. To show fortitude and to fulfill the agreement that Blaquerna called for, we have the romance of the deeds of Blaquerna; it recounts the beginning of Blaquerna's journey in the monastery, which the Emperor recognized in response to the value he had found in his empire. For his son, he made the Book of the Court <note>original: "libre de contria," likely referring to a guide for courtly or spiritual conduct.</note> so that he might govern himself in his residence. And after all these things, the man decided to follow Blaquerna, so that in contemplating, he might become a hermit of our Lord. **L**et us speak of the mind, that time and means you have to go and ask in contrition; you had to go to the court to show the abbreviated art and to find the place where you could go to teach with the Lord. The Apostles and the kings were bound and moved by these general studies, so that the intention of the world would be to love God above all things, and to give knowledge of Him to the unfaithful who are ignorant of the truth. He first asked that no one should be praised for loving

<column-break/>

all that God found in the reins of his heart. When the Emperor and the Bishop met, they greeted each other very pleasantly. Like a world-weary man, he showed the depth of his change, for he was now a changed man compared to the other. When it pleased the Emperor to bring him the news of the coming of the Cupid <note>original: "cupady," likely a metaphorical reference to divine love or a specific literary allusion in the text.</note>, the Emperor recognized the Bishop who was filled with value. Then at the Court of Rome, where natural power is exercised, they labored day by day to give the response of God, and to win over the Minstrel of Valor so that he might compose verses in the language of others, and that they might be remembered in the life of the Apostles. He received from them many fruits in the manner of a knight.
**F**eeling and believing in our glorious King, that he did not want to live or die except for Him. Mentioning three times the prayers where we do not wish to suffer death; and if one follows the order in you, it is pleasant to hear of him.
**T**his is a new way of being a blacksmith of a new light, and it appears that those who use it do not find death pleasant. And as for him who is a master of these things, he will tell you: the minstrel loses himself in God, so that he may come into His presence.
**T**his man fought with the Friars Minor <note>The Franciscans.</note>, and the savior who wanted to see him so religious and wanting to fight for love. And in these times of sorrow, there are many agreements to please God, who through love wants to come to us to be loved.
And it happened that they set out to dwell in God, only wanting to reach the end.

<summary>This page concludes the "Art of Contemplation" and transitions into the narrative of Blaquerna and the "Minstrel of Valor," emphasizing the mission to spread divine love across all social classes and the protagonist's move toward a hermit's life.</summary>
<keywords>Blaquerna, Ramon Llull, Minstrel of Valor, Art of Contemplation, Court of Rome, divine love, hermitage, spiritual knight</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the spiritual/philosophical discussion of the "Art of Contemplation" found on the previous page to the concluding verses of the "Breviari d'Amor" (The Breviary of Love). The left column concludes the primary work, while the right column contains 16th-century additions, showing the manuscript's continued use as a personal ledger or family book long after its initial creation.</meta>

# Folio 108

**Left Column**

<margin>
This is the book of 
Sir Bertrand San <unclear>...</unclear>
of the <unclear>...</unclear> G. of <unclear>...</unclear>
G. of M <unclear>...</unclear>
<note>This marginal note is an ownership mark, likely from a later period than the main text, claiming the book for a "Sir Bertrand."</note>
</margin>

...by the saint whom he intends to please. Amen.
So much their professions, nor that they are kings
who endure, humble themselves, and give. After me,
may God's wonder rule.

The one enchanted by the dew finds pleasure in it,
returning into it. In tears and weeping,
my heart's longing continues,
and now I am burning with desire and cold with shivering.
In his mind and in his will, he remains in God,
making music on the flute in his own nature.
<note>This stanza uses the courtly love language of the Troubadours (heat, cold, longing) but redirects it toward divine devotion.</note>

The counsel of my father and my lord
guided my path. Praise to the one who takes this path.
He blames those who follow the wrong way,
which I once knew in my errors.

**A**s I saw and gave praise to the light,
nor did I covet the world's grain.
Wait for a prophet who brings comfort,
while blaming the memory of falsehood.
It is foolish to take pleasure in blame;
rather, one should seek the promise that
was made, so that one might fear the Bishop.
I tell you, wait for such a harvest.
Show the way to the lady who is commanded,
who first moved the heavens and changed
my crime into a plea. In that place, wait
for the one who created the harvest.
The word was given to the Marys
on Easter, so that the Bishop would not see the blame
for commanding the wife.
In the Bible, this is commanded:
the blessing of God. Amen.

**C**abret <note>Cabret is likely the name of the scribe who copied this manuscript; it was common for scribes to insert their names in the "explicit" or closing lines.</note> remains in this state,
which is for God. In this our
world, we say that there is no one
who can pray or express it,
as the following words show.

->Finished is the book; praise be to Christ, amen.<-
<note>original: "finito libro sit laus xpo amen"</note>
